Introduction 75
Fig. 24: The mystery of conjunction in the vessel. By following the sage's advice

76 Commentary on the Hall ar-rutmiz
alchemists. The fact that the art of alchemy is connecting this world (ad-
duny cl ) to the other world (al-âhira) with the help of dreams and visions
gives the adepts the possibility to influence this world. But if that is done
according to their own desires, and not in accordance with God’s will, it
will corrupt the world (fascid ad-dunya). A later author, Fahr ad-DIn, says:
«Whoever knows the secret of the spirits of the upper world (- the arche­
types) can easily be gripped by hubris, trespassing the border of being
human over to the realm of the divine. And whoever knows the names,
substances, and actions of the spiritual beings (ar-rühânïya) can thus
influence whatever he wants, be it for good or for evil.»128 The fact that this
connection to the other world can work in the outer world synchronistically
or magically gives the adept an immense responsibility, as any misuse of
his or her knowledge could become destructive to the world. So we read
repeatedly in alchemical books that the knowledge of alchemy must be
hidden from those who do not deserve to know it. They are the people who
are, according to Ibn Umail, caught victim of their «own desires and conflicts
[coming from] their devilish imagination». The need to keep this know­
ledge secret from those people becomes drastically visible when reading,
for instance, a passage from Zosimos’ Mushaf as-suwar. «Therefore they
disguised it [the work] with examples and names, and with this they veiled
the science. They wanted thereby that no one should attain the science from
their books except a sage who is persistent in reading their books, and is
devoted to them being patient with the suffering which comes from the dif­
ferences of the statements in their books. [...] because if it [the work] were
made visible the world would become corrupt, and it [the work] would
become something like the making of glass. [...] Therefore the exalted God
advised Seth, the son of Adam, [...], not to reveal it to the creatures.»129
This passage, and many similar to it, point to the fact that the connection
to the world of the archetypes is dangerous, as it can lead to an inflation of
the ego of a human being who all of a sudden thinks he or she is godlike.
The past century has given us enough proof of the possible collective
disasters that such identification with the archetypal realm can bring.

2. Symbols of the First
and the Second Stone
Text: (7.1) Know that the first of the names of the sages’ stone is so
lead-copper, magnesia, lead, tin, iron, copper and silver. And it is the white
earth, the holy thirsty earth, the earth of pearls, the earth of silver, the earth
of gold, the starry earth, and the snowy earth. And when they say: the
filings of silver, and the filings of gold that is pure, without debasement,
they mean their second stone, which is lead-copper. It is their silver, and it
is their body to which they return its spirits, which they had extracted from
their first body at the beginning of the operation. (7.8)
Ibn Umail starts with the first names of the stone, meaning those at si
the very beginning of the alchemical work.130 Then he goes directly to
various names of the second stone, which he calls «their body to which they
return its spirits». These spirits were extracted from the first body, which
must be identical with «the first stone», because he first calls the stone lead-
copper and silver—the names that he gives to «the second stone» as well.
In order to be able to understand what Ibn Umail means by these 82
many strange names and operations, we will now follow the method of
«necessary association» or amplification that was mentioned above. Our
first source of associations must, of course, be Ibn Umail’s own books,
especially his other explanations of the same symbols at different places in
our Hall ar-rumüz. Secondly we will look at amplifications by those
authors whose texts are quoted in his writings. When we have collected
no The pronoun refers to the names: «The names of the stone at first are lead-copper ...»
The interpretation that these names refer to the first stage or part of the operation is confirmed
by Ibn Umail's other writings.

these amplifications it will be possible to see what Ibn Umail meant in his
own «Explanation of the Symbols», and to render it in today’s language.
83 At first this stone is called lead-copper (Arabic = abär-nuhäs).m
This is indeed a name of outstanding importance in Ibn Umail’s writings.
In his Kitäb al-Magmsiyä ( Book of magnesia), a female, called Hilcil (new
moon or crescent moon),131 132 asks about «the science of the high lead-
copper».133 This shows that, for Ibn Umail, alchemy is equated with the
work with, or on «the high lead-copper».
84 The answer is given by explaining first that «the glorified lead
(Arabic = abär134) is the female» that is described, among other things, as
the «despised [...] thrown on the roadway».135 In the famous poem entitled
Risäla as-sams ilä al-hiläl (Message of the Sun to the Crescent Moon),
which is found at the beginning of Ibn Umail’s al-MäJ al-waraqî, lead is
associated with the moon, the watery spirit which is able to extract the fiery
soul from the body.136 From this we can understand lead as a symbol of the
feminine personification of the spirit of the unconscious that is rejected by
the prevailing collective views. In later Latin alchemy, this rejected spirit
became known as sapientia dei, the guiding principle, leading the alchemist
in his work.137 From our text, and from other books by Ibn Umail, we know
that the seven planets played an important role in the work.138 As for earlier
alchemists, also for him they were connected to the seven base metals.

[Page 80]
Symbols of the First and the Second Stone 79
From a later book of Ibn Umail, called ad-Durra an-naqïya (The Pure
Pearl), we see that he associated lead with Saturn.139 This is in accordance
with the long tradition which held that the black, heavy metal lead was a
symbol for the restrictions that worldly existence imposed, evident in the
lead-coffin of Seth in the Osiris myth of Ancient Egypt, or in the Saturnal
bondage to bodily existence which we find in texts since Hellenistic times.
The fact that Ibn Umail connects lead with the female receptive moon and
the male Saturn, points to the female-male or passive-active double nature
of lead, symbolizing the spirit of the unconscious psyche in its paradoxical
wholeness. Owing to this fact, lead on its own could be a symbol for the
stone also,140 as it was for Greek alchemists in earlier times.141
Copper, the other part of lead-copper, is described by Ibn Umail in
his Kitäb al-Magnïsiyâ as the glorified male, «being the red stone, working
wonders».142 This red stone brings copper into relation with the fiery red
sun, the partner of the moon, the watery spirit that we saw symbolized by
the lead. As with lead, we find a bipolar symbolic meaning in copper.
When copper is purified and polished we can touch something warm, and
see a reddish shining metal that, for Ibn Umail, was also connected in the
traditional way to Venus.143 In his book al-Mä1 al-waraqï, Ibn Umail says
more about copper, explaining that they name their body the copper,144 «and
it (the copper/their body) is the mother of the gold and the father of the dyes.
And it is what gives birth to the gold for them».145 And in the same text at
another place, he quotes a well-known comparison of the sages: «Our

copper is like a human being which has a spirit, a soul and a body; and the
three of them are one.»146 He explains that the sages said this «because [our
copper] was a body, then it became a soul, and then it became a spirit.»147
He points out «that they [the sages] were therefore able to say three, and
the three are one. [...] Thus spirit, soul and body are one, and all of them
are from one. And this operation is what they name the transformation and
the dividing up, because it becomes transformed by itself from one state to
another, and from weakness to strength, and from thickness to fineness and
subtleness [...]. Then it returns to become a whole human being (insän
kämil)».m Later in our Hall ar-rumüz, Ibn Umail also speaks of «their
copper—meaning their stone»,149 and in al-MäD al-waraql we read that for
Ibn Umail the copper is also the second body, being the goal of the work.150
From all these statements of Ibn Umail we see that like lead, copper—due to
its double nature—is also on its own a symbol for the stone. Already in Greek
alchemical texts we read that this copper of the sages has to be purified by
the work in order to turn it into a copper without shadow.151 The result is
equated with the sages’ gold,152 which is also called by Zosimos «a sun and
a king on earth».153 In the same way as for the Greek alchemists before
him, for Ibn Umail copper is a symbol of the inner god-man that has to be
extracted and purified from the individual bodily existence.154 The result is
the complete human being, the homo totus in later Latin alchemy.
The union of lead and copper is the aim of the first composition
(at-tarklb al-awwal), as Ibn Umail explains in the same Kitäb al-Magnlsiyä.155

[Page 83]
82 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
This first union of lead and copper is black, incomplete and unstable,
as it is only a latent quatemio, based on the double-nature of lead and copper.
In the individual this is experienced as a desperate attempt to contain and
understand—with the help of a vessel—an inexplicable infinite otherness.
However, this container or frame seems meaningless in view of the infinity
of the divine darkness that causes a chaotic and unfree state of a flooded
psyche, as can be seen from the symbolic illustration taken from a later
Latin alchemical text (fig. 25). On all four sides of the black square we read:
Et sic in infinitum (And so on infinitely), indicating the abysmal darkness
that the adept must have been exposed to when creating that picture.
This first union of spirit and soul, of watery moon and dry sun, or
of silver and gold, is here in our Hall ar-rumüz the very first symbol for the
stone, indicating its importance for Ibn Umail. The result is a two-oneness,
a rebis, as this was later named in Latin alchemy. This imperfect lead-copper
has then to be purified by the work in order to be turned into the second
body, which in our passage he also calls lead-copper. But this second body
is then named the perfect lead-copper (abär nuhäs tämm).
The union of «lead-copper at first» alludes to the fact that the stone
of the sages originates in the coming together of an overwhelming fiery-
erotic impulse (copper) within the limitations of an individual existence
(lead). The outer reality always somehow painfully restricts the over­
whelming impact of such a divine-erotic impulse, leading to conflict. This
is what made Zosimos write that this black (= incomplete) lead-copper is
a bitter state.160 In the context of Islamic mysticism, this means that the
prima materia of the alchemist is the encounter of the devilish-fiery
impulse of the embodied soul (nafs näriya = fiery soul)161 in the narrow
frame of an individual, symbolized by the lead. But due to, and, paradoxi­
cally, with the help of, this restricting lead—a symbol for the image-creating
spirit of the unconscious (rüh)—the meaning of such a fiery impulse can be
recognized by a process of distillation. That leads to its transformation and
containment, sublimating this energy into the love of God.162 Our lead-
copper at first is in that sense the first realisation of an overwhelming, and
initially inexplicable, hot creative energy coming from the bodily drives.
That autonomous otherness seeks to become incarnate in the psyche of man.
Zosimos, Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 123a. 12-13.

[Page 84]
Symbols of the First and the Second Stone 83
The symbol of lead-copper can therefore be understood as the bewildering
mixture of the dark-light double-nature of the divine nature of the uncon­
scious, where the two parts—its constructive and its destructive side—need
to be separated, looked at individually, and pondered over in order to be
able to integrate the constructive aspect of the unconscious to the individual.
In Greek alchemy, lead-copper was named molybdochalkos (molybdos
- lead, and chalkos = copper), and was already a well-known symbol.163
M. Berthelot and other chemically-oriented researchers thought that
molybdochalkos must have referred to an alloy of lead and copper.164
However, as there is no mention of such an alloy except in symbolic
alchemical texts,165 we have to focus on the various descriptions that can be
found. Zosimos writes in extenso about molybdochalkos,166 * and equates it
Fig. 26: Tail-biting serpent (Gr.= ouroboros). The text inside the tail-biting serpent

red one and the white one. This bipolar image of totality was named molybdochalkos
(lead-copper) in Greek alchemy.167

84 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
with «the All», which is written in the Codex Marcianus 299 in capital letters
as «TO FLAN».168 The one substance that is here called «the All» is the
famous hen to pan (= One is All, or All is One), which was in Ancient
Egypt since the New Kingdom, symbolized by the Ouroboros (= the tail-
biting snake, see fig. 26).169 Often the words continued: kai pan ho theoù*
(= and All is God).170
91 In the lexicon of the same codex we read that molybdochalkos is
identified with the divine water (hydor theion).171 According to Zosimos,
molybdochalkos is, however, also that which leads the seekers into error,172
a well-known description of the dangerous side of the unconscious. In the
Kitab al-majatîh ji as-san ca (The Book of the Keys to the Art) of Zosimos,
we read about a dream of the author in which Democritus told him: «“Turn
the copper into gold.” Then the sage continued: “Write down these things
till you reach lead-copper, in which the secret lies. But I think that you
should not write these many compositions in any of your books for who­
ever comes after you, because all of the work is in lead-copper.” When he
made me understand his statement, he disappeared from me, and I woke up
wondering.»173 From all this it is evident that lead-copper was understood
by the Greek alchemists in a similar way as it was later by Ibn Umail,
namely as a symbol for the totality of their work with divine matter, its
beginning, its process, as well as its end.
92 Seen psychologically, lead-copper is a symbol for the bipolarity of
the conscious-unconscious unity, its totality, and at the same time its
steering centre, which C. G. Jung called the self.174 It symbolizes the para­
doxical light-dark, solar-lunar, or dynamic-static nature of the psyche. In
addition, both consciousness and the unconscious, are to be understood as
bipolar themselves: on the one hand we have the bipolarity of the unconscious
that manifests itself with its bewildering chaos and horrifying darkness, as
well as its meaningful order- and light-bringing quality. On the other hand,
consciousness is also known for its bipolarity, symbolized in alchemy as
M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs III.XXVIII.2, where Zosimos equates molybdochalkos with the

After lead-copper, the next of the «first names of the sages’ stone» 93
given by Ibn Umail is magnesia (magnîsiyâ).In our text we find it mentioned
frequently, sometimes together with lead-copper, which shows that for our
author magnesia must have been as central a symbol as lead-copper. The

86 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
connection of these two symbols can also be found in Greek alchemy.177
Later in our Hall ar-rumüz, Ibn Umail points out that «they named the
magnesia, which is their stone, with the names of their divine water, and
they named their divine water with the names of their dry body, which is
the lead-copper».178 This is one of the clearest passages which show that
for him these two symbols, lead-copper and magnesia, were interchangeable.
Later in the Hall ar-rumüz he also tells us that the earlier alchemists called
the magnesia «the temple and the mosque [...] because it collects whatever
they need»,179 and that they named it also «the immaculate virgin»180 and
«the magnet because it attracts the iron so that it clings to it».181 This
indicates the magic quality of attractivity of the material world that has
always been a fascinating riddle and a threat to humans, endangering the
freedom of conscious control. Furthermore, in our Hall ar-rumüz, Ibn
Umail tries to explain the symbol magnesia by calling it a «bunch of grapes
because they squeeze their water out of it»182, and «a book and all that
flows out of it books».183 But then Ibn Umail also says that they named the
magnesia «every thing».184 And yet in another place, in his al-Ma} al-waraqï,
he says that magnesia «is put together from four bodies [...] they coagulate
and turn into one body».185 In the same way as Ibn Umail speaks of lead-
copper as lead-copper at first and as the second stone, later in our Hall ar-
rumüz he explains that magnesia is black in the beginning and has to be
turned white and then red.186 He compares the whitening or purification of
the magnesia with the transformation of the black new moon into the
shining full moon, as depicted on the tablet in Ibn UmaiT s vision in al-
Mâ: al-waraqï, where he comments «The full moon is the magnesia,
which is the complete lead-copper [...], and the two birds [male and
female = spirit and soul] originate in the full moon.»187 And again later,
in our Hall ar-rumüz, he says that the white magnesia is the second

iss Al-Ma> al-waraqï, p. 56.4-5.
Hall ar-rumüz, p. 7.1, 7.6 and 37.5-7.

[Page 88]
Symbols of the First and the Second Stone 87
stone188 which is also called the immaculate virgin,189 and the mother of
gods.190 All this shows that what Ibn Umail meant by the whitened magnesia
was the purified, or ‘pure at heart’, individual body. This body gives birth
to the unified spirit and soul which the sages see as being of divine nature.
United together, spirit and soul are called the divine water.191 From these
different statements of Ibn Umail it is clear that magnesia symbolizes an
otherwise inexplicable divine mystery, the multiple oneness of the stone
of the sages and how it functions.192 In al-MäJ al-waraqï, Ibn Umail sums
it up by saying: «The world is magnesia. Hermes called it by that name, for
he said: “It is the microcosm [dunyâ sagîra], and it is alive, not dying until
the day of resurrection, as long as the world shall last. It revives all the
dead, and manifests the hidden and concealed colours, taking away the
external colours”.»193
When Ibn Umail writes of magnesia that collects external things
and manifests hidden colours, he has in mind a substance quite different
from the magnesium of today’s chemistry. From Zosimos’ Mushaf as-suwar
we learn that in Egypt the glass-makers used a dyeing substance called
magnesia that then became an alchemical symbol.194 According to
E. O. von Lippmann, this magnesia of the glassmakers is manganese dioxide
(Mn02), a poisonous and easily ignitable stone, the colour of which varies
from dark brown to deep black.195 It dyes glass purple. In the above-
mentioned text Zosimos warns: «So the ignorant hastened to the magnesia
of the people and took that. The magnesia of the people, however, is only
good for dyeing glass, but it has no power to fight the fire, because when
the fire touches it, it turns into ashes.»196 It seems that in later Arabic alchemy,
magnesia as a symbol was also understood as being related to the magnesia
of the glassmakers.197 Ibn Umail’s later comparison of magnesia with the
iss Hall ar-rumüz, p. 7.6 and 31.10.

194 Zosimos, Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 107b.4 for example.

197 In the Kitäb Qalübatra malikat Samannüd (Epistle of Cleopatra, the Queen of Semennud

[Page 89]
88 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
magnet is based on the fact that the magnesia of the glassmakers has the
ability to attract—like a magnet—and absorb all unwanted colours in the
dark, liquid raw glass.198 In this way, the glass becomes bright.199 Thus
magnesia (sometimes also named body of magnesia) and magnets are
connected by their capacity to attract other particles.200 Because of its
purifying capacity, magnesia is also called the «soap of the glassmakers».201
While with lead-copper the two-oneness or active-passive double
nature of the stone is emphasized, magnesia, being a dyeing and purifying
substance, symbolizes the enigmatic active power in the unconscious that
is able to attract impurities and thus purify the soul of the adept, like the
«soap of the glassmakers». This permits the true, concealed colours to
become manifest, which Ibn Umail (quoting Hermes) relates to the resurrec­
tion, «the reviving of all the dead.»202 Magnesia as a symbol for the stone
points to that divine mystery of attraction that comes from the stone, and
that can first lead a human being into an abyss of darkness before a whitening
or purification leads to a gradual understanding of the symbolic meaning of
this very blackness, finally giving birth as «immaculate virgin» to the
realization of the divine inner centre, the self. It is not out of place to recall
that in Islamic literature Satan (Iblis) is often presented as attractive.
The stone at the beginning, the black magnesia, symbolizes an
active, overwhelming dynamism of the self which is unconscious (black)
and therefore disconnected from conscious life. Any fascination, any

glassmakers. They answer that this was because when they mix it, a mighty blackness results,

Ullmann argues that this text could have a Greek origin (p. 174), but the whole text reminds
him of Arabic alchemical texts of the 9th /10th century. As a substance for dyeing glass it was
still familiar to a traditional glass-maker near Bäb al-Futüh in Old Cairo in 1998.

and make it white. It is even able to make glass white. M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs VI.XXII.27.

[Page 90]
Symbols of the First and the Second Stone 89
falling in love, but also any neurotic symptom that an adept may take up,
look at, and ponder over while keeping his or her drivenness in suspension
for a while, can be the prima materia which is the beginning of the work.203
Magnesia can thus be understood first as an inexplicable feminine power of
attraction and love, greater than the ego, and second as the principium
individuationis, that inner psychic reality which is able to gather what really
belongs to an individual and reject what is alien to it. But it depends on the
individual whether or not he or she does the work on and with the magnesia,
whatever that means for the individual. As for the different colours that are
then related to magnesia, these will be amplified later in this book.
In the quotation from Hermes mentioned earlier by Ibn Umail, 97
magnesia is also called a microcosm. This had already been formulated by
Zosimos204 and remained central to the texts of later Arabic alchemists of
the religous-symbolic branch, such as Morienus (in Arabic: Mariyänus
ar-Rähib, Marianos the monk), who lived in the 8th century. He explains to
Halid ibn Yazld: «This stone is from the most honoured of what God
created for you. And it is with you wherever you are. Whoever of God’s
creatures loses it, dies. [...] it is extracted (muhraga) from you and you are
its source. It is found in you, and it is taken from you, and after the work
is done, you desire it, and your confidence in it will increase.»205 This
passage was known to Ibn Umail.206 It clearly indicates that the honoured
stone of the sages is something hidden inside every human being that has
to be extracted from the individual by the alchemical work.
After the magnesia, Ibn Umail gives the names of five metals, all 98
of them further names for the first stone. They are symbols of the materia
prima that can appear on their own as origin of the alchemical work. But
our author considers that four of these metals, namely lead, tin, iron and
copper belong together.207 As for him the base metals were associated with

unattainable lady Theosebeia, who was his student.
?» For instance, his treatise “Peri Aretês”, M. Berthelot, Alch. Grecs III.I.4-8.

the seven planets, lead, tin. iron and copper are representing Saturn,
Jupiter, Mars and Venus on earth. Ibn Umail considers these four metals as
dyads, with copper and iron belonging to the sun, and lead and tin belonging
to the moon.208 As both Venus and Jupiter were considered benevolent, and
Mars and Saturn rather malevolent, both of the great luminaries are seen as
having a problematic aspect as becomes visible in figure 28, where we see
the symbols for Mars related to the Sun and Saturn to the Moon.
Fig. 28: Four planets/metals and their relation to sun and moon, according to Ibn
Umail. The sun is related to gold, the moon to silver. In the centre is uniting mercury.
Already in Greek alchemy, lead, tin, iron and copper were well
known as a four-fold body (tetrasomia), meaning they were considered a
four-oneness.209 Like the four elements, the four seasons, or the four
directions of the wind, the four-oneness of these metals is for Ibn Umail
See al-Mä: al-waraqT, p. 4.10, where the sun calls the moon «0 tin» and a little later
208
says: «I am the dry and strong iron», while the union of sun and moon is «cast like the cop­
per with the lead.» (on p. 4.8).
209 Ta de tessera hen (the four are one); see E. O. von Lippmann, Entstehung I, p. 340-346,

[Page 92]
Symbols of the First and the Second Stone 91
«one body of four parts».210 In the commentary to his al-Qasïda an-nünïya
he writes: «But with regard to their statement: “Make the iron soft, whiten
the copper, make the squeaking of the tin disappear, and remove the moisture
from the lead”, they [the sages] made clear that their stone was composed
of four bodies.»211 Then he comments on the purification of these four
metals: «... when you make white (= soft) the iron, [...] you make white
the four [metals] because all of them are united».212 From this it becomes
clear that each metal is a symbol for the whole stone. The mutually related
four aspects of the stone are a latent quatemio, which points to a potential
wholeness within the psyche that can become conscious. Each of the metals,
when purified, can lead to the stone of the sages.213
In a text, quoted by Ibn Umail in his al-MâJ al-waraqï, Zosimos too
explains to Theosebeia: «Know that the bodies which are extracted as spirits
in the operation are the gold, the silver, the iron, the lead and the tin which
are from us and for us and part of our work and our operation.»214 For Ibn
Umail, as we have seen, every base metal is associated with a planet, and
those divine forces refer ultimately to the (astrological) temperaments, that
is, to psychic factors. Therefore we can say that this interrelation of the four
metals points psychologically to the work on a complex that always has an
archetype in its centre. The work on such a complex—symbolized by the
purification or the whitening of one of the metals, i.e. the representation of

Second body (lead-copper, magnesia, silver)
Fig. 29: The development from the first to the second body. The body is symbolized
in Ibn Umail’s text by lead-copper, magnesia or silver at, the first and the second
stage, or here in our figure at the lower and the higher level. The higher plane is
reached through a process of transformation and integration, consisting first of an
unfolding of the totality into four parts (the tetrasomia) or entities, and then by a
reuniting. In other words there is a process of differentiation by the four functions,
which alone changes the originally unconscious totality into a conscious one.215
an archetype (= planet) on earth, leads to a balancing of the whole psyche,
represented by the quaternio of metals. It may start with the softening of
the iron (Mars). That would be a symbol for the humanization of an
excessive power drive. Or it may begin with the whitening of copper
(Venus), to prevent the copper from developing poisonous verdigris.215 216
Symbolically, this activity points to the work of purifying an overwhelming
215 See C. G. Jung, Aion, § 410, for the four functions of consciousness see glossary.
Till recently, all over the Orient, this whitening of the copper was regularly needed for
216

[Page 94]
Symbols of the First and the Second Stone 93
problem arising from falling in love that would otherwise poison the
whole psyche. The removing of the squeaking of the tin (Jupiter) could be
understood as the reduction of the ‘squeaking sound’ of a dominant self-
righteous attitude, while the removing of the moisture from the lead
(Saturn) points to the need to start the work on the «damp and heavy», i. e.
depressing dominance of a force that imprisons and reduces a person’s
outlook to the ‘nothing but’ factual side of life. Such a purifying work on
a constellated archetype establishes an equilibrium in the whole psyche,
and can turn into something that is experienced as a healing medicine ( fig. 29).
After these four metals, Ibn Umail mentions silver as the last of the 101
first names of the stone. Yet, like lead-copper, silver also appears in our
passage to be a name for their second stone. In his al-Mä: al-waraqï, he
speaks directly of water for «the making black of their second silver, which
is their second body, and [...] making it white also.»217 Being a passive or
corrodible metal that changes easily from shining whiteness to blackness,
silver has been generally associated with the feminine and the moon. The
work originates in the moon, out of which spirit and soul are extracted, as we
have seen in Ibn Umail’s great vision before (§ 22). Silver as a name for the
stone points to the earthen aspect of the moon and characterizes the stone’s
ability to change by itself, like the moon, from blackness to shining
whiteness.218 Silver as a name for the second stone emphasises the receptive
aspect of the purified stone—being the subtle body—that is now able to
receive the gold or the divine water. Thanks to this capacity, the stone can
give birth to itself, bringing its own renewal. This is because the reviving
divine water was first extracted from the moon whose metal on earth is the
silver. The moon is a symbol of the first body that we have seen on the right
side of the tablet in Ibn Umail’s vision, while on its left side there is the union
of the gold (the three suns) with the moon, the silver on earth (see fig. 7).
The mention of the metals leads to an enumeration of the many 102
different aspects of the earth (ard), «the white earth, the holy thirsty earth,
the earth of pearls, the earth of silver, the earth of gold, the starry earth and
the snowy earth.» Refering to these symbols, Ibn Umail says in his al-Mäy
al-waraqï that the holy thirsty earth is the «second white body» and «their
copper vessels in the households, in order to prevent them from developing the poisonous
verdigris. Today, aluminium vessels have replaced copper vessels.
Al-Mä} al-waraqï, p. 49.22.

second earth, which is their second body.»219 This holy thirsty earth is
«their dead body», whose life has been extracted by the first part of the
work.220 The shining whiteness of the snow of this dead body during the
‘winter stage’ of the second stone is aptly named snowy earth. It is this
«second white body» or «second stone» to which «they return its spirits
which they extracted from their first body at the beginning of the opera­
tion», as we have read in our text.221 In springtime the flowers and colours
emerge from the snowy earth through the reviving effect of reddening six
waters, as Ibn Umail says ,222 This resurrected body is then named the starry
earth, because the divine water (= spirit and soul) rained down in the second
part of the work, reviving the earth and causing its flowers to appear. These
are the stars on earth, the reviving result of the influx of energy from the
light bringing insights, distilled out in the first part of the work.
In al-McV al-waraqï we are given a detailed explanation of the three
103
earths, «the earth of pearls, the earth of silver,223 and the earth of gold».
They are stages of purification of the first blackness that is taking place in
the first part of the work. So we read: «That is because the bodies are the
united, knotted male and female».224 We remember the conjunction of the^
sun and the new moon at the beginning of the work in Ibn UmaiT s great
vision. Then Ibn Umail continues: «They named the first three soakings the
nutrition, the salting, [...], and they named it with every dry one and what
is similar to that. Then they [these three] coagulated with the male and the
female [i. e. the sun and moon], and they are from them, originating from
them. One of these three [soakings or saltings] is a male, and they name it
air because it became a male. So they became two males, having between
them a female. Then on those two males and one female another female
entered, so all became four, two males and two females. Thus the house got
completed by four walls, and on the whole entered the third salting, and so

[Page 97]
96
104 «Filings of silver» (burädat al-waraq) is another symbol for the
second stone or the second body, as becomes clear a few lines later in our
text (7.13) where we read that the «filings of silver» are also named «ash
[...], and the seeker». As filings of silver are neither solid nor liquid, they
represent a loosened up state of the «silver at first» that was a symbol for
the stone at the beginning of the work. Thus this new symbol stands for a
second receptive state of the adept (the seeker) that is now ready to receive
the influx of the divine water or the gold, represented on the stone tablet of
Ibn Umail’s great vision by the enlightening of the lower lunar world by
the rays of the three suns (fig. 15).
105 From the explanation of the symbol «filings of silver» it becomes
understandable that the second stone is also named «filings of gold»
(burädat ad-dahab). The filings of gold can be understood to symbolize
the entry of subtle-touchable, incorruptible quality of the divine water into
the purified second body that is also called the filings of silver, ash or the
seeker, perfectly uniting but not mixing with the receptive reality of the
adept, staying now in a lasting two-oneness. The name «filings of gold»
for the stone also goes back to Greek alchemy. In the Mushaf as-suwar of
Zosimos we read about the filings of gold as being the result of the operation
on the copper of the sages.226 Earlier, in Ptolemaic Egypt (3rd-lst century
B.C.) the rising sun, symbol for the reborn and renewed consciousness, was
compared to gold dust, filling the land.227
Zosimos, Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 196a. She said: «Then tell me about the statement of the
226
sage: "Operate it [the copper] till it becomes like the filings of the gold that do not get burnt.
If it does not become like that, then do not blame the copper, but blame yourselves, because
you have performed a bad operation.» He said: «The sage meant by this statement that if you
take copper that is not our copper, and you operate it, nothing will come out of it. But if you
operate our copper with the eternal water, then in the operation you will find it to be as the
sage told you.» In his book “Physika kai mystika”, Democritus also speaks of golddust
(psëgma chrysou). It is the outcome of the treatment of the pyrite, which has to be freed from
its black colour and becomes resistant to fire. M. Berthelot, Aich, grecs II.I.6. Later on, in
Arabic alchemy ibn Arfac Ra3s (12th cent.) wrote a book with the title Sudur ad-dahab (The
Filings of Gold). It is a poem of 1460 verses to which he wrote an explanation in the form of
a dialogue with his student. He also quotes on different occasions Zosimos’ Mushaf as-suwar.
(See M. Ullmann, Natur- und Geheimwiss. im Islam, p. 231 f., and later commentaries to this
book, e.g. of Gildakï, ibid., p. 239) Gold-dust as a symbol continued to be known in Latin
alchemy as «limatura auri» drawn this way ffj . See G. W. Gessmann, Die Geheimsymbole
der Alchymie, Arzneikunde und Astrologie des Mittelalters, Tafel XXI.
227 D. Kurth, Treffpunkt der Götter. Inschriften aus dem Tempel des Horus von Edfu, p. 83:
A morning hymn to Horus.

[Page 98]
97
3. Symbols of the Worked-upon Stone
Text: (7.9) When the stone becomes white, they name it the flower i06
of the salt. The envious ones of the sages named it zinc ore and the earthen
zinc ore, zinc ore being the white body. That is before it becomes black for
the second time, after which comes the whiteness, after which comes the
redness. And it is the filings of silver, the filings of burnt copper, ash, dust,
the slave and the seeker. It is the thing which is dyed and the dyer, the
holder, the regulator, and the clinger. (9.1)
This is a continuation of the description of the white earth. The io7
flower of the salt is another symbol for the goal of the first part of the work.
In al-Mä: al-waraqï, Ibn Umail refers to the whitening process of the lead-
copper in this first part of the work as «the three saltings» (see § 23) that is
for him a process of purification.228 The symbolism of salt is readily under­
stood, when one remembers that in antiquity, salt was a key substance for
purification and preservation.229 The ancient alchemists considered the
flower of a substance to be the refined, worked-upon substance.230 From a
later comment in our text, we can see that by the flower of the salt Ibn
Umail means the result of the salting or purification process, namely the
raised, pure, divine water «which they named the holy water».231 He also
r:s See our text p. 63.11-14, where Ibn Umail says«the water and the fire with which they
[the sages] burn and wash [...] are named «every salt».
R. J. Forbes, “The Salts of Preclassical Antiquity”, unpublished typescript, The Museum

process, see e.g. Synesios in M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs II.III.5. See also I. Vereno, Studien,

in Hall ar-Rumüz, p. 75.10 and 77.9.
r

[Page 99]
98 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
calls the salting process the whitening, and obviously by that he means the
same thing as Zosimos does by the salting in the mummification ritual,
namely the process of purifying the mortal man to become Osiris, the God-
man.232 This ritual can also be considered as a symbol of the first part of
the work in the quest for immortality, namely to create a solid incorrupt-
able vessel for the soul’s journey, described in the different Pharaonic
Books of the Afterlife.233 It can be understood as a symbolic precaution that
the individual ego is not dissolved in the world of gods or in today’s words,
in the realm of the archetypes.
The expression earthen zinc-ore (al-qadmiyä al-fahärlya) connects
us with the world of the potter (al-fahhârî), who creates earthenware using
zinc ore, a white earth. Chemically this is impure zinc carbonate that turns
into white zinc oxide when heated.234 The name qadmiyä for zinc ore goes
back to Greek alchemy. We find in the “Physika kai mystika” of
Democritus a passage where he explains how to make white the Cyprian
cadmia (kyprian kadmian),235 and Zosimos speaks of the cooked qadmiyä
as the purified lead-copper.236 For Ibn Umail, zinc-ore is one of the ten
names or aspects of magnesia which are mentioned in the following passage
of our text.237 But Ibn Umail also warns the reader that it is important not
to become confused by the many names as they all point to the same riddle,
«their hidden stone».238 Later in our text he becomes more explicit about
what he means by earthen zinc-ore: It is the dried, thirsty, holy earth, the
sediment or the second body that is longing for the water that has been
extracted in the first part of the work.239 In our text, earthen zinc-ore is,
however, clearly described as the first whiteness, meaning the whiteness of
the first half of the work, after which comes the second blackness, followed
by the second whiteness and the redness.
See the description of the work by Zosimos and Olympiodoros in M. Berthelot, Alch.
232
grecs III.VI. 1 and IH.IV.1-7. By the Greek word taricheia Olympiodoros means salting and, in
certain contexts, also mummification. See E. O. von Lippmann, Entstehung I, p. 48.
233 See E. Hornung, “Vom Sinn der Mumifizierung” in Festschrift für Hellmut Brunner in:
Die Welt des Orients 14, 1983, p. 167-175, and E. Homung, The Books of the Afterlife.
234 I. Vereno, Studien, p. 116 n.318; the Arabic transliteration is also kalimya and kalimina
which became lapis calaminaris in Latin alchemy. See E. O. Lippmann, Entstehung I, p. 593.
235 M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs II.I.9.
236 Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 216b.7.
237 The lexicon in M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs I.n. says: kadmeia esti magnesia. See also
M. Berthelot, La Chimie au moyen âge II, p. 272.
238 Hall ar-Rumüz, p. 73.12-13. Ibid., p. 83.4-5.
239

[Page 100]
Symbols of the Worked-upon Stone 99
When it turns white, Ibn Umail also calls the stone «filings of silver, 109
filings of burnt copper, ashes and dust». This state is produced through a
process of burning, cooking, pulverization, threshing, etc., of the first body
till the thick parts turn into filings, ashes and dust. It is described in many
and varied ways in Ibn UmaiT s works. Zosimos is especially explicit on
pulverisation as part of the work. «If its thick part is not destroyed and
burnt until it turns into fine ashes having no touch, its dyer spirit does not
come out. It does not come out except with the subtle thought in the same
way as the subtle thought of the sages does not appear until he liberates the
soul and purifies it from the passions and the afflictions about the thought
into which he entered. That is what the work is like.»240 This reveals that
Zosimos was already aware of the psychological dimension of the work.
All this burning, cooking, etc., is a purification of the soul of the adept in
order that he becomes subtle enough to be receptive to the divine spirit.
Later in our text Ibn Umail calls the earthen zinc-ore and the ashes the
«crown of victory».241 This points to the highest value of what comes into
existence by this purification of the soul. Then, as al-MäJ al-waraqT says
«[...] the lower body rejoices in the heavenly nature because it is of the
same form and is produced from it [...]».242 This union takes place
between the purified and therefore completely receptive psyche of the
adept and the divine influx from the heavenly nature.
The symbol of the slave ( cabd) is mentioned later in our Hall ar- 110
nimüz as «a fugitive slave who is prevented from escaping by the holy
water».243 And in the last written document of Ibn Umail, called ad-Durra
an-naqTya (The Pure Pearl), we read a description of the work with the not
yet purified and the purified red slave, symbolizing red mercury.244 This
indicates that the slave is a symbol for the fire that needs to be contained
by the holy water. Together with the holy water, the fugitive slave turns
into a red stone, which would then change «into the sun, standing for
liberation» as we read in the above-mentioned ad-Durra an-naqTya.245
mo
Zosimos, Mafätlh as-sanca, fol. 102. See also Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 173b.9-10.
mi
Hall ar-rumüz. p. 83.3-4.
242 Al-Mä} al-waraqï, p. 137a.
243 Hall ar-rumüz, p. 41.8.
244 Ad-Durra an-naqTya, fol. 25 f and 35 f. The word cabd (slave) is also found in other
Arabic alchemical texts. For instance in M. Ullmann, “Kleopatra”, p. 172, or A. Siggel,
Decknamen, p. 45.
245 Ad-Durra an-naqTya, fol. 25 f.

[Page 101]
100 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
This shows that the whole process is a psychological development, finally
creating consciousness (= the sun) that liberates man from the not yet puri­
fied red slave that is meaningless, hellish darkness. The description of the
slave or the fugitive slave shows that this symbol refers to the problem of
uniting the slavish or unfree drivenness (red fire) with its own opposite
(white water). This means that an evasive, autonomous drive is brought
back into equilibrium with the rest of the psyche thanks to the pictures (=
the holy water) that lie behind these emotions and so reveal their deeper
meaning. C. G. Jung describes this process in his book Memories, Dreams,
Reflections. He writes: «For decades I always turned to the anima when I
felt that my emotional behaviour was disturbed, and that something had
been constellated in the unconscious. I would then ask the anima: “Now
what are you up to? What do you see? I should like to know.” After some
resistance she regularly produced an image. As soon as the image was
there, the unrest of the sense of oppression vanished. The whole energy of
these emotions was transformed into interest and curiosity about the image.
I would speak with the anima about the images she communicated to me,
for I had to try to understand them as best I could, just like a dream.»246
in The next symbol is the seeker (tälib = student, seeker). In Ibn
Umail’s al-M& al-waraqï we find his description of the end of the work
when «all of them [the ten] are in their [second] body, which is the earth.
And the seeker held the fugitive and the two became one thing while they
are, with regard to the name, two.»247 From this we can see that at that time
the purified body of the adept himself—that means his subtle body or his or
her completely receptive psyche—is in a mysterious way united with the
fugitive spirit. For that union the two, spirit and soul, have to be turned into
one spirit, as will become clearer later on in our commentary. The name
lead-copper points to this two-oneness of the seeker and the fugitive spirit.
112 The dyed one and the dyer are the lover of God and God himself,
a central topic in the second part of the work that will also be explained in
detail later. The one who holds, who regulates, and who clings give further
aspects of the quality of the stone that attracts the attention of the seeker
and is able to regulate or control (däbit = controller) the process of self­
unfolding or individuation, just as the genetic blueprint of a seedling
regulates the growth of a plant.
246 C. G. Jung, Memories, Dreams, Reflections, p. 187 f.
247 Al-MäD al-waraqï, p. 70.7-8.

[Page 102]
Symbols of the Worked-upon Stone 101
Text: (9.1) And it is the oxymel, and the explanation of the oxymel 113
is the moderator. That comes when the stone becomes moderate in its
mixture, so that it becomes neither hot nor cold, and neither moist nor dry.
And it is named pyrite when it becomes white, and the ten colours, and the
ten things are the magnesia. And when the body and the water became red
after the dyeing, they named it the flower of gold. (9.7)
Now the stone is named oxymel (aksimJl), which is explained as the
114
moderator. The Arabic word aksimTl comes from the Greek word oxymeli,
a mixture of sour vinegar (oxos) and sweet honey (.meli). Later in our text,
vinegar mixed with honey is explained as the spirit and the soul carried in
the water, with the soul being the honey. Obviously this union of the
opposites (sour and sweet) in the oxymel enables it to be a moderator,
mediating between the opposites of hot and cold, dry and wet.248 Here Ibn
Umail has in mind the four basic qualities of matter that are connected to
the four elements, e.g. fire being hot and dry. Later in our text, oxymel is
water
Fig. 31 : The four elements and the four qualities of matter as pairs of opposites. The
stone is the centre and the periphery.
:48 Oxymel is also a powerful remedy in folk medicine, as described by D. C. Jarvis,
Arthritis and Folk Medicine.
Hall ar-rumüz, p. 65.2.
249

[Page 103]
102 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
equated with the «crown of everything, and everything is their stone.»249 We
should therefore recognize oxymel as another name for the perfect lead-
copper, the stone that is able to give birth to gold. The description of the
stone as the moderator between the opposites points to its capacity to unite
the opposing inner-psychic tendencies. Jung called this central uniting
force in man’s psyche the self.
115 The white pyrite (bürnâtïs, bürïtïs) is another name for the stone
that has turned white. The Arabic word goes back to the Greek word
pyritës. This is a sulphite of either iron or copper,250 and closely related to
copper, as the main source for obtaining copper is copper pyrite (CuFeS3),
which is named chalk pyrite in Greek.251 But like magnesia it is a symbol
for the philosopher’s stone and not the outer mineral pyrite.252 As is
explained in the Kitäb Qalübatra malikat Samannüd (Treatise of
Cleopatra, Queen of Semennud): «The philosophers call the stone pyrite,
because its origin and its completion is in the fire. It exists in it and from it.
It is fiery from fire.»253 It obviously symbolized the quality of the stone that
can permanently contain the fire within itself. Like magnesia, it can change
its colour from black to white and finally to red (see also page 157).254
116 Our text then relates pyrite to ten colours and ten things that are the
magnesia. Later in our text, Ibn Umail speaks of the pure water of the sulphur,
which is another name for purified pyrite,255 with many colours and ten
names.256 In his al-Mä2 al-waraqî Ibn Umail explains that the ten means a
union of the four elements. He writes: «The first number is one and the
second they call two. Consequently they are three numbers. Then they say
three which (with the previous 1+2) become six in number (l+2+3=6).
Then they say four which (then) becomes (with the previous six) externally
250 See, for instance, M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs III.VI.3; see also M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs I,
p. 252.
251 I. Vereno, Studien, p. 123.
252 In our text, magnesia is called red bürïtïs (6,14). In the Greek Lexicon we read s.v.
mystërion«The mystery of all metallic stones is the pyrite», and s.v. pyrites it gives: «Pyrite
is magnesia». M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs I.II; see also A. Siggel, Decknamen, p. 17. For pyrite
being a 'non-body', see I. Vereno, Studien, p. 123.
253 M. Ullmann, “Kleopatra”, p. 166 f.
For black pyrite, see J. Ruska, Turba, p. 283. In our text we find red pyrite on fol. 6.13.
254
On fol. 15.1 Ibn Umail speaks of a first and a second pyrite.
255 See Hall ar-rumüz, p. 69.1.
256 See ibid., fol. 13.10. This idea goes back to Greek alchemy. Zosimos speaks in different
places about the one and the ten in his Mushaf as-suwar and in his Mafâtïh as-san ca. See also
J. Ruska, Turba, p. 37f.

[Page 104]
Symbols of the Worked-upon Stone 103
ten, but in its hidden meaning is (still only) four. This number completes
the magnesia, which is lead-copper, which is composed of four. The ten are
four, and from the ten proceed four, and from the four proceed ten.
Consequently these (four) are the four natures, earth, air, water and fire:
and all created beings come into existence from them. Understand this!»257
H. E. Stapleton says in a footnote that «this is an excellent illustration of
the way in which a belief in the tetractys of the Pythagoreans as the basal
principle of matter survived until the time of Ibn Umail. The tetractys
represents the number 10 as a triangle made up of 4 showing at a glance
that 1+2+3+4=10.258
fire
air
water
earth
Fig. 32: The Pythagorean tetractys shows at a glance how the earth (no. 1 or 4)
together with water (no. 2 or 3), air (no. 3 or 2) and fire (no. 4 or 1) make up ten.
This union of the four elements into something durable is beauti­
fully explained by the alchemist Maslama al-Magrîtï 259 in his Rutbat al-
hakTm wa-mudhal at-ta clTm (The levels of the Sage and Introduction to the
Teaching).260 There he quotes Hermes, who said: «O my son, look at the
potter, how he kneads the earth with water [to form a pot] and then dries it
in the sun [air]. Next he treats it with fire and it becomes a body. Water and
earth have come together [in a way] that prevents the body turning into
earth again, and after that this holds the water and does not disintegrate
anymore.»261 First earth and water are brought together, then air and finally
fire are added. This union of the four elements leads to the creation of a

[Page 105]
104 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
Fig. 33: The sage Hermes says: «O my son, look at the potter, how he kneads the
earth with water [to form a pot] and then dries it in the sun [air]. Next he treats it with
fire and it becomes a body [...] that does not disintegrate anymore». The subtle soul-
figure inspires the adept to do this work on himself. (Picture by Hossein Behzad)

[Page 106]
Symbols of the Worked-upon Stone 105
Fig. 34: The soul-figure receives the divine spirit with the help of a container that
has been created in the alchemical work by uniting the four elements of earth,

[Page 107]
106 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
pot which can receive and hold the fiery soul. It is a striking symbol for a
structure based on the four functions of consciousness that can receive and
contain, i.e. live with, the divine spirit of the unconscious (see fig. 33 and 34).
In our text, the result is called a flower of gold. Flowers are for Ibn
Umail, as we saw with the flower of the salt, the emerging visible result of
a repeated refining work.262 Later in our text he explains that the flower of
gold is a symbol for the union of spirit and soul in the divine water,263 and
a little further on we find the statement that, at the time when the divine
water and the body unite to become one single dye and hence improve each
other, this state is also named the flower of gold.264 In his al-M& al-
waraqï, Ibn Umail states that «when the stone becomes red, they name it
flower of gold, aqzal gold and purple gold».265 Ibn Umail also equates the
flower of gold with the ferment of gold, the glue of gold, etc., all of which
are names for this divine water.266 From all this we can see that the golden
flower symbolizes for Ibn Umail different aspects of the final stage of the
work, either when the divine water manifests itself or when it is able to
coagulate in, or unite with the bodily reality of the adept. The symbol of the
golden flower is already found in Greek alchemy, where John, the arch­
priest of the temple of Ebagia, speaks in his treatise “On the Divine Art” of
the spirit of the holy black stone that causes the golden flowers to appear
(chrysanthimon) which is in fact gold that resists fire.267
With the mention of the flower of gold we have reached a point in
the work that is seldom further elaborated.268 Ibn Umail seems, however,
to have been inspired to speak of a first part and second part of the work,
stating that some earlier alchemists saw these two parts of the work as
being one.269 We can assume that our author was led to this by his great
vision that he explains in al-M& al-waraqï. This made him ponder more
deeply on the mystery of the conjunction of the opposites and to further
differentiate some of its manifestations.
Al-Mä3 al-waraqï, p. 91.1 f. and 47.21-25.
262
263 Hall ar-rumüz, fol. 9.14.
264 Ibid., p. 10.7-8.
265 Al-Mâ3 al-waraqï, p. 47.18.
Ibid., fol. 9.9-14, 10.4-5; we find this connection already in Zosimos’ Mafâtïh as-san:a.
266
267 M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs IV.III. 10, see also E. 0. von Lippmann, Entstehung I, p. 70.
This first part of the work is, for instance, in the Risälat as-Sirr (Epistle of the Secret,
268
ascribed to Hermes) the whole work. There the marriage of the king with his four wives is the
goal. Text edited by I. Vereno, Studien, p. 136 f.
269 Al-Mâ3 al-waraqï, p. 50.12-16.

[Page 108]
107
4. From the First to the Second Part
of the Work
Text: (9.8) In the whiteness they named it white lead and ibsimïs 120
lead, and it can be named ibsimïth, and called ibsimïs with the letterfa?, but
more often it is used with the letter ba: because of its intense whiteness, and
the alabaster (nastarïs) and the bastarit. All that is because of its intense
whiteness and the corporeal chrysocolla. All these are names at the time of
its second whiteness, after which comes the purpleness. (9.13)
Here we now have names of the second whiteness that appears after 121
the second blackness. The first symbol is white lead (basic lead carbonate,
PbC03) that Ibn Umail also names «ibsimïs lead».270 Black lead turns
when heated into white lead. In al-M& al-waraqï, Ibn Umail equates
«ibsimïs-lead» or «ißimls-lead» with the complete lead-copper and the
white magnesia because of its intense whiteness.271 He also names this
state the white stone, and describes it as the result of the three saltings that
take place in the first part of the work.272 The «intense whiteness» points
to its shining quality as a result of this process of purification that leads to
the union of the four elements now called the complete lead-copper, as we
saw before. It is a symbol for the light-bringing feeling of a unity of the
adept’s conflicting parts. It is the final acceptance that they all belong to his
or her unique personality.
Alabaster (nastarïs) is another name for this intensely white stone. 122
In its raw state, however, this alabaster stone, a gypsum, is quite ordinary
:t> This name that reads in Ms G as-simïs comes from the Greek ifa^vdiov. See CALA I, p. 9.
eil Al-Mä> al-waraqï, p. 24.12-13, p. 55.20, p. 68.15, and p. 88.19. There it is written
absamït-lead and aßamis with the [letter]
272 Ibid., p. 24.9-13.

[Page 109]
108 Commentary on the Hall ar-rnmuz
looking (fig. 35). In Egypt, alabaster is of varying brownish and greenish
colours, but there are also white alabaster stones. One of its main characteri­
stics is the softness that makes it easily workable. Since early Pharaonic
times, the white alabaster stone was used especially to make statues, vessels,
jars, etc., mostly for funeral rites (fig. 36). Vessels are beautifully translucent.
Fig. 35: The raw alabaster stone looks quite ordinary.
Here in our text, Ibn Umail talks of the black, white and red alabaster stone
and points out that it is another name for the magnesia.273 In al-McP al-
waraql Ibn Umail mentions the white alabaster-stone as being a symbol for
the intensely white stone that is the perfect lead-copper.274 An Arabic text,
attributed to Zosimos, explains this: «It is the lime. When it became a stone
we named it alabaster. We named it alabaster because of its intense white­
ness, and we named it lime because in it is concealed the fire in the same
way as the fire is concealed in the lime of the people.»275 Lime becomes hot
when mixed with water. When worked upon and polished, alabaster also
becomes pleasantly warm as if there were a mild fire inside. This is due to
the fat that is in this stone. We find a further revealing description of the
creation and function of this intensely white alabaster stone in the Risäla
273 Al-Md? al-waraqï, p. 24.11.
274 Ibid., p. 24.12.
273 Mushaf as-suwar. fol. 220.7-9.

[Page 110]
From the First to the Second Part of the Work 109

(The Egyptian Museum, Cairo)
Hurmus Budasïr (Epistle of Flermes Budashir), a text quoted by Ibn Umail
in his al-McP al-waraqT.216 There we learn that the stone has to be cooked
[...] until it turns into «a shining white spirit that dazzles the eyes with its
beauty and whiteness. It is the outstanding alabaster stone that was looked
for by the ancestors for the sake of eternity.»277 This alabaster stone has
”6 Al-McP al-waraqT, p. 53.15.
277 Risala Hurmus Budasïr fol. 9a, 126 f.; see Vereno, Studien, p. 152 and 204. I prefer to
translate calä wasn ad-dahr according to Vereno’s second option with «for eternity's sake»,
as the whole alchemical work revolves around the quest for immortality.

[Page 111]
110 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
then to be operated on in order «that it may receive the colours of truth and
the everlasting flowers.»278 For a Muslim reader, the colours of truth are
the living presence of Allah, as the Truth (al-Haqq) is one of His 99 names.
At the end of the epistle, Hermes writes to his disciple Amütäsiya that «she
will have to elevate this outstanding alabaster in the mihräb (prayer-niche) of
her temples.»279 This shows the psychological dimension of the work: The
union of the purified alabaster with the colours of truth is a symbol of the
unio mystica of the adept’s psyche with God that will become the centre of
the house of God. The alabaster stone with its inspiring qualities is the
symbol for a consolidated, unique personality that can contain the divine fire,
thus solidly uniting all the different emotional tendencies.
123 Chrysocolla (harsqulä) literally means gold-glue. It is already
mentioned frequently in Greek alchemical texts.280 As a substance
(CuC03) it was used to weld gold.281 For Ibn Umail, as for Zosimos and
other Greek alchemists before him, it was another symbol for the stone.282
It is one of the many names of the stone, as Ibn Umail explains later in our
text.283 There he also says that it is the same as magnesia,284 honey285 and
the «flower of copper which is the thing that grasps all the things».286
Chrysocolla or gold-glue can therefore be understood as one of the actively
unifying aspects of the white or second stone.
124 According to our text, «these are all names [of the stone] at the time
of its second whiteness». This shows that we are now in the second half of
the work. The result of the first half of the work is the creation of a psychic
unity that is separated from the bodily reality. The first part of the second
half of the work starts—as we know from Ibn Umail’s great vision—first

where we find a whole paragraph on chrysocolla.
