spreads the dye, which they named the soul. And Hermes named it gold by
saying: “Cultivate the gold in a silvery white earth." By this he meant this
dye which they also compared to the flowers of herbs. And they named it
the saffron and the safflower. And this earth is dyed, then the spiritual
water of the sages disappears, and the soul, which is the dye, remains in the
body, and it caused its colour, its beauty and its splendour to appear in it.
For it is a subtle vapour, that is neither felt nor seen, except for what it does
in the second body, and what it does is to cause the colours to appear. And
Hall ar-rumuz,
370 See p. 5.25-26, 95.12-13, and 97.7.

[Page 151]
150 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
it is a fire and it comes into being from the fire, I mean it is bom from the
fire, and it is nourished by the fire, and it is the daughter of the fire.»371 This
text shows the mutual dependence of the dyer and the dyed, whereby the gar­
ment is the seeker’s purified psyche that holds the “fugitive dye”, the eternal
fiery soul. The text also illustrates that “penetrating insights” into the mean­
ing of the fiery soul leads, when reconnected with the psyche of the adept, to
an enduring union of the psyche with the divine spark, light, or colour, which
was at first concealed inside the original incomprehensible fiery drive,
isi Text: (23.7) This is the explanation of what the sages meant by their
statement «Make the gold white and make the gold red». Otherwise there
would be no benefit for them. What wisdom could oblige them to turn into
silver that which has greater value than silver? But by that they rather
meant what I explained to you about the whitening of the stone and the
black body in order that its colour turns into a colour like silver, after it was
black. Then it has to be turned into red by the rest of the water, which is set
aside for the reddening which they named the dyes, and the manifestation
of the herbs in order that it turns into gold, meaning that it turns into a
dyeing elixir. When many people heard the mention of gold and the statement
of the sages «make the gold red» and «dye the gold» they took the gold of
the people and sought from it what is not in it. Therefore it did not give
them a dye, because it is [already] perfect in its dye and it is self-sufficient.
It does not give colour nor does it accept the dye. since the dyeing is for
the spirits, not for bodies. (25.4)
1S2 We know from the great vision of Ibn Umail that the second part of
the work consists of nine soakings. The first one triggers off the previously-
mentioned second blackness, while the next two soakings are for the second
whitenesses. The rest of them are for the six soakings that make the second
body red. To understand this passage, one has to remember that what Ibn
Umail means here by gold is the second body that is finally turned red as
a result of these six soakings.
183 Text: (25.5) But in fact, by the statement of the sages: «Bodies get
dyed by bodies», they only meant by the bodies the six soakings for dyeing
from the water, with which they make red because they were bodies,
which the sages made from a subtle spirit. Then they returned them by the
Al-Mä al-waraqî, Risälat as
.ui J p. 49.25-50.9: the sym bol of the ship also appears in the
Sirr Studien,
of Hermes, ed. by I. Vereno, p. 160.

[Page 152]
The Dyer and the Dyed 151
dissolution and the operation into dyeing souls and fiery spirits with which
they have dyed their single body, which comes from their work in the other
operation. Therefore they said: «Bodies are dyed by bodies». And they also
named their stone, which is the body, bodies, because of its many sisters
that entered into it. Thus it is a body and in number it is bodies. Whatever
enters into it from its sisters is a body and in number it is bodies. (25.14)
The second body is the one that receives all the soakings with the 184
divine water. They originate from the first body and are for that reason also
called bodies. Ibn Umail names the water, which is the soul united with the
spirit, sometimes sister and sometimes brother.372 Both indicate a direct
relationship to its origin, the body. The six soakings give the six colours to
the second body, which is the purified psyche of the adept. This one, subtle
body unites these six bodies or sisters. It symbolizes the experience of the
purified psyche of the adept as it reunites with the different aspects that
became conscious by the process of distillation in the first pail of the work.
It is the stage of becoming conscious of the different archetypal energies in
the circle of time, divided, as we have seen, into six double months.
Text: (25.14) And in the dissolution after solidification it is a spirit. i85
because it was a corporeal spirit, so that it resisted the fire and took on the
colour and great power. Thus it became a great matter. When it gets burnt for
the second time with water and decays, it turns again into a spiritual body.
Thus the spiritual fiery spirits enter into it [the body] because it became
subtle and similar to them and belongs to them. And it has a soul and a
spirit similar to what is in that water. But both are not operated. Then the
soul and the spirit which are in the dyeing water, meet the soul and the spirit,
which are in the second body. Therefore the sages said: «The close one met
the close one. (The beloved meets the beloved.)» And also he said: «Nature
enjoys nature and nature holds on to nature and nature conquers nature», and
«being enjoys being and being clings to being.» By all of this they [the sages]
meant the reunion of the soul and spirit, which are in the body with the soul
and the spirit coming out in the divine water, from the first body. And this is
the complete lead-copper. Thus, when the perfectly operated [and thus
refined] soul and spirit enter into the second body, the whole becomes one
perfect thing, beyond perfection. Then the dye gushes forth from it, which
makes imperfect things perfect. Then the perfect gold comes from it. (29.4)
AI-McV al-waraqï,
37: p. 15.21, 25.4, 70.7, 71.24-25, 91.17, 93.19-24.

[Page 153]
152 Commentary on the Hall ar-mmüz
Fig. 53: Traditional dyeing place in Fès, Marocco. Like Fès also Akhmim in Upper
Egypt—praised by Ibn Umail for its Pharaonic temple—was at the time of Ibn
Umail a centre for dyeing. As Zosimos from Panopolis (= today’s Akhmim) says:
«[...] I have told you before that the sages described their work with any of the
crafts similar to it, in order to cover it.» (Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 8a. 16-17.) The
process of dyeing was an often described craft, symbol for the alchemical work.

[Page 154]
The Dyer and the Dyed 153
The spirits that enter the body are the spirits containing the soul. i86
When spirit and soul unite in the air, Ibn Umail calls the soul also a spirit.
Thus they are called two fiery spirits or just two spirits. By the process of
purification during the first part of the work, the body also became subtle
and is called the second body. It is now ready for union with the divine fire
contained in the divided up water. This divine water, consisting of the
operated soul and spirit, meets with the not operated soul and spirit that are
still in the second body. The main idea here is that the soul and spirit were
extracted bit by bit from the first body. In this way, the rest of the soul and
spirit, which remain unoperated in the body, are united in this second part
of the work with the soul and the spirit that were operated on the first part
of the work. It is the light of a newly-created consciousness, the sun, which
then connects with the reality of the worked-upon individual nature. What
was extracted by the adept in the first work “against nature" is now recon­
nected step by step with the reality of the pre-conscious totality: the operated
soul and spirit in the second body, which is also called the moon. The
reunion of the second part of the work leads first to the second blackness.
This expresses the paradox of the separation within the adept and brings
about a duality where nature starts to relate to nature, and being to being.
This is the two-oneness that Ibn Umail refers to, and it is the essence of all
alchemy. The statement: «Nature enjoys nature, and nature holds on to
nature, and nature conquers nature» goes back to Democritus, who found
this as the essence of the teaching of Ostanes.373 It summarizes the miraculous
creation of consciousness, «that raises the adept above nature by working on
the world of nature».374 The development starts from the original participation
mystique going to the separation of the ego from the unconscious to the final
establishment of consciousness. With the statement: «The beloved meets the
beloved» (27.6) we have a new aspect that seems to be a central feature in
Arabic alchemy: It is the union in love of spirit and soul with the second
body. This is, as Marie-Louise von Franz has worked out in her commentary,
«the mystical wedding of the divine spirit and the purified soul of the
alchemist».375 When the gold or the divine water penetrates the second body,
or the purified psyche of the adept, and unites with this «whitened gold» of
the earth that is silver, then Ibn Umail speaks of the perfect gold.
Alch. Grecs
See Dem ocrites’ "Physika kai M ystika" in M . Berthelot. II.1.3.
Lectures given at the ETH 1940,
"4 C. G. Jung, p. 41.
Commentary on the Hall ar-rumiiz,
"5 M .-L. von Franz, § 145.

[Page 155]
154 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz
187 The soul in the second body is not operated, but, when connected
with the contained fire in the divine water, it becomes connected to some­
thing eternal, in other words the contained eternal being sheds light on the
meaning of the divine fire itself. In this way nature is redeemed and
enlightened by the work: God becomes conscious of himself. The one and
the many become reconciled as two aspects of the divine and form now a
two-oneness, the perfect lead-copper.
188 Text: (29.5) Thus I have explained to you the meaning of their
statement: «The close one met its close one» and their statement: «Nature
conquers nature, and being holds on to being.» The soul which is in the
second body, holds on to the soul which is in the divine water. And each
of the two rejoiced at meeting the other. (29.9)
189 In al-Mä0 al-wamqï Ibn Umail writes more about this union that
takes place in the second body: «And it is the colour of the eternal life, the
purple redness. And because their stone often changes its colours they
named it etesian stone, meaning, as we have seen, the one having many
colours. Thus they named this second body, hidden with lime, Hermes, the
controller of the dyes with its ferment. Thus the soul which is in this second
body becomes happy because of the entering of the spiritual, supreme,
heavenly, airy, fiery soul, which is in their water. In the same way, the
supreme soul becomes happy with the earthly soul, which it encounters in
this body. Thus they hold on to each other. And this is the meaning of the
saying of the sage: “The close one encounters its close one.” And it is the
meaning of their saying: “Nature rejoices in nature, and nature clings to
nature, and nature holds on to nature, and nature conquers nature.” And it
is the meaning of their saying: “Being holds on to being, and being clings
to being.” By all of this they mean the nature which is in the water, which
is the soul, and the nature which is in the body, which is the soul that is in
it.»376 This text shows that in the end the operated soul which is contained
in the divine water unites with the unoperated soul that is still in the body.
The operated soul would then be that aspect of the divine fire that could
be made conscious. The divine water is a symbol for that aspect of the
collective unconscious which could be made conscious, while the soul that
is still in the body is the divine fire in the collective unconscious, the
essence of nature. Thus the amount of the divine fire that has been integrated
376 AI-Mä: al-waraqi , p. 60.20-22.

[Page 156]
The Dyer and the Dyed 155
in consciousness (the upper God image) reunites in the second work with
the lower God image and brings consciousness to the dark-fiery inner
nature. Marie-Louise von Franz comments on this in her book Dream and
Death: «What has been made conscious from the collective unconscious
and connected to the individual consciousness remains eternal.»377
Text: (29.9) Therefore the body revived and lived the eternal life. 190
Thus the meaning in their statement: «Nature is for nature» and «Being is
for being» is the same and brooks no disagreement. And therefore Maria
the Hebrew put forth this statement and said: «We [the sages] are the
inhabitants of one city and each of us recognizes the language of the others.
Thus whoever is not from our city and does not know our language must
not enter our work.» (29.14)
The final reunion of the second body with the divine water (that is i9i
the soul united with the spirit) makes the whole eternal, as Ibn Umail
explains in greater detail in al-Mä3 al-waraql.m This shows that symbolic
alchemy is in fact a psychological quest for immortality. The creation of the
second body, which is receptive to obtain the dye of the eternal divine
water, has a potential that stands beyond the decay of time. This second
body, that is the subtle body, can be understood as the liberated personal
myth of an individual that has become conscious by the intense work of
continuous introspection. The union of the divine fire with this purified indi­
vidual subtle body allows life to continue to grow according to the divine will,
connected with this inner, unique pattern. It is no longer affected by decay.
The quote from Maria is word for word the same as in al-Mä- al- 192
waraql?19 The problem of understanding the language of the sages is to be
seen in the light of the fact that one cannot understand what all these
symbols of the stone really mean as long as there is no personal experience
to link it to. But whoever realizes that there is an inner dreamworld and
fantasy life filled with mysterious symbols that have a numinous quality,
he or she is given the key to an understanding of this symbolic world and
lifestyle that Ibn Umail talks about. Then one can connect to this inner city,
whose inhabitants speak the same language. This reminds us of the unifying
effect of the Holy Spirit at Pentecost that spontaneously enabled people
with different languages to understand each other. Yet in spite of all this,
M.-L. von Franz, On Dream and Death, p. 118 f.
377
378 Al-Mä•> al-waraqî, p. 35.1.
379 Ibid., p. 69.27-28, 80.4-5 and 100.23-24.

[Page 157]
156
Ibn Umail states in al-Mä: al-waraqï that «even if somebody understands
their sayings, he is surprised how it was possible for it to be hidden for him
at the beginning. It [the stone] is visible, clear, and not covered for all who
are intelligent, but for those who ignore it, it remains hidden. And although
it is easy, it is difficult and hard to extract it from their books.»380 As Ibn
Umail points out in different places, knowledge and experience of the
healing effect of the unifying symbols of the stone emerge only from
continuously looking down and inside, and from accepting the lowest in
human nature as well as the highest. The symbols that emerge from this
work are ultimately those that can mysteriously reconcile opposites of the
low and the high. This is because symbols have both a material and a
spiritual aspect as C. G. Jung has worked out (see fn 402 and § 271). Thus it
will be necessary, indeed, not to speak in the future of allegorical alchemy
but to call the alchemist’s quest for eternal life symbolic alchemy.
Fig. 54: Human excrement as fire for the oven. All that is indigestible, dirty, stinking
and collectively left behind is a symbol for rejected matter. This is the fire that
keeps going the alchemical process in the adept, who is drawn as being the oven.
The adept’s head is the alembic. From Geber(t)i liber transformationis seu liber
florum, 15./16. century. (Bayrische Staatsbibliothek Munich, fol. 21v and 22r.)
380 Al-May al-waraqï, p. 101.20-21.

[Page 158]
157
7. The Container and the Contained
Text: (29.15) They named their stone the red pyrite, the iron, the 193
reddened sweet and the reddened copper. All of these are names of the
magnesia. (31.1)
A pyrite-stone (bwntis) contains different metals. E. O. von Lippmann 194
attributes its frequent mention in alchemy to the fact that this stone appears
in beautiful varieties that resemble iron, silver and gold.381 According to
ancient belief, it contains fire inside (gr. pyros = fire), as since prehistoric
times pyrite was used to create fire by hitting it against another stone. That
fire is the highly inflammable sulphur of the iron-bisulphate (FeS2). When
heated, pyrite loses its sulphur and turns red. As a name of the stone we
Fig. 55: The Pyrite-stone appears in varieties that resemble iron, silver, and gold.
381 E. O von Lippmann, Entstehung I, p. 86.

[Page 159]
158 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
came across pyrite earlier in our text.382 The key to understanding what Ibn
Umail meant by pyrite can be found later in our Hall ar-rumüz, where we
read: «They named their water [...] pyrite, the explanation for this being
the sulphur water. And it is the first pyrite, while the second pyrite is the
sediment and the master of the waters.» 383 Therefore the first or black
pyrite is the sulphuric or divine water that has to be freed from the body by
fire, i.e. by cooking, distilling, etc.. What remains is the white pyrite, which
Ibn Umail calls the sediment, the ash, or the dead first body which, in the
second part of the work, must be turned into a red pyrite that is resistant to
fire. A passage from the Mushaf as-suwar of Zosimos clarifies this aspect
of the red pyrite. Zosimos explains to Theosebeia: «“It is a stone [...]
which [...] they named pyrite.” [She said:] “Why did they name it pyrite?”
He said: “That is because it is bom in the fire, and it was nourished by it.
And the translation of pyrite is ‘the son of the fire’.”»384
195 This red pyrite, which in our passage is also called «the reddened
sweet» and «the reddened copper», is the goal of the work, the second
stone, or the second pyrite that turns from white to red.385 The alchemical
process describes this transformation of the black pyrite to the white and
finally to the red pyrite or, in other words, from the first stone to the second
stone. When the fiery soul is extracted or separated from the black (uncon­
scious) first body and united with the watery spirit, i.e. when a certain over­
whelming drivenness is contained in a symbolic meaning, then this «fiery
sulphur» develops the possibility, when reunited with the second body, to
turn into something that is resistant to fire. By the gradual development of
a symbolic attitude towards any strong emotion, this overwhelming (=
divine and yet also devilish) fire can be contained. In a later text we find
an image of the stone of the sages named rock of the devil (sahrat as-
Saitän), pointing by that to the fact that the purified psyche of the adept
contains, and by that humanizes, the hellish-divine fire (see fig. 56).386
196 From all this we can see that Ibn Umail describes the mysterious
development of a solid container for the archetypal influx in humans. The
creation of an attitude that permits the divine fire to live inside a human
382 This in connection with the symbol «filings of gold» on p. 96 fn 226, and later as «white
pyrite» in § 113 and § 115.
383 Hall ar-rumüz, p. 67.12-69.3.
384 Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 58b. 10-12. Zosimos often mentions pyrite in his books.
385 Hall ar-rumüz, p. 69.2-3.
386 Up till now this is the only place where we could find the name «rock of the devil».

[Page 160]
The Container and the Contained
159
Fig. 56: Rock of the Devil (sahrat as-Saitän). This name for the stone of the sages
is written inside the ouroboros. The corrupt hieroglyphs could point to an Egyptian
origin of this text of Abu al-Qäsim’s K. al-Aqâlïm as-sabaca. (British Lib. London)

[Page 161]
160 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
being is like a solid stove in a house that can contain the heat of fire,
warming up the house, yet protecting it from being burnt down.
197 Text: (31.1) They also call their stone glass and a piece of glass. It
was thus named because of its whiteness, and because it does not accept
any dirt, nor anything that is not from it like the pure body of glass. But it
accepts the dyes in the same way as the glass accepts all the dyes. (30.4)
198 The next symbol, glass (zugäg), clarifies further aspects of the
stone. In the Koran, glass is known to be a symbol for the faithful: «Allah
is the light of the heavens and the earth. His light is like a niche in which
there is a lamp. The lamp is made of glass. The glass itself does not shine.
But when the light enters it, it shines like a brilliant star. Thus is the trans­
parent and pure soul of the faithful illuminated by Allah’s Light (mir calä mir).
Like glass, it can let the inner light shine through» (Sura 24.35, and also
27.35). Regarding the material aspect of glass, this substance is made out
of the commonly found quartzite-sand, which is mixed with other materials
in order to be worked upon. This sand mixture melts after about three hours
of heating. After around four hours, the liquid glass is still full of bubbles
and lumps, and now needs to undergo a process of purification that lasts for
about another two hours. We are familiar with this method of glass production
already from Mesopotamia and Ancient Egypt, its origins going back to
over 3,500 years before our time. In some places in the Orient it is still
possible to witness a traditional glassmaker in front of his simple clay oven,
where out of a heap of sand he produces a liquid which, after being purified,
formed, and dyed turns into a transparent, shining vessel that remains stable
as a “solid liquid” in whatever form and colour that has been given to it.387
Witnessing this miracle of transformation enables one to understand why
for the alchemists in ancient times, glass became a symbol of their stone. It
was the ultimate expression of that mysterious metamorphosis that can take
place inside the adept. Out of the many incoherent particles of a personality
appears, by God’s grace and by means of the art, the creation of something
coherent: a glass body symbolizing a united, stable ego-consciousness
that in spite of being solid remains transparent for the divine light. This
387 Quartzite-sand (Si02) melts at the high temperature of around 1,500° C. But when mixed
with soda (Na2C03) or potassium (K2C03), the melting temperature comes down to around
850° C. Glass produced in this way is, however, soluble in water. In order to make it solid,
lime (CaC03) is added to the melted sand.

[Page 162]
The Container and the Contained 161
pure body of glass is white. When cooled off, it remains a white body,
which does not accept anything alien, and is—under normal circum­
stances—resistant to fire. For Ibn Umail, glass is a symbol for the solid,
transparent second body, or the second stone that is born of fire, and
resistant to fire. The final colour red for the glass is considered to be the goal
of the reddening, or the solar work. This colour is acquired in the process of
glassmaking by adding a colloidal solution of gold to the melted sand. Red
is the colour of the rising sun in Pharaonic Egypt and in general the colour
of blood and of life. In alchemy it has become the symbolic colour for the
creation of a renewed consciousness and for new life.
Fig. 57: Traditional glassmaker in Old Cairo in the year 1998 in front of his simple
clay oven (Arabic = at-tannür). He produced glass in almost the same way as it was
done in Ancient Egypt.

[Page 163]
162 Commentary on the Hall ar-ntmuz
Text: (31.4) And they named the magnesia, which is their stone,
with the names of their divine water, and they named their divine water
with the names of their dry body, which is the lead-copper. It is also the
magnesia because it is from one thing, I mean it is from one water, in order
to confuse whoever hears it. But it is not obscure for the one who knows
the water and the body. (31.9)
The names of the body and of the divine water are interchangeable
for the alchemists. This points to the two aspects of the one being, which
is the adept him- or herself. The solid aspect is the individual body with its
urges and desires. The liquid aspect consists of all the images that arise
from deep within, appearing in dreams, fantasies and imaginations. The
latter regulate the drives, giving them direction and meaning. The goal of
the work is the reunification or reconnection of these two aspects, matter
and psyche, or drive and drive-control, in order that these two sides of the
individual can work again in harmony. For the compensatory or self­
regulating relationship between instinct and image I refer the reader to
the results of C. G Jung’s basic research.388
Text: (31.10) Among the names with which they alluded to the
magnesia is a bunch of grapes because they squeeze their water out of it.
And they named it a book and all that flows out of it books, and arsenic,
and sandarach. And when it became dry and solid they named it mercury,
white sulphur, whitened sulphur, and white arsenic. (31.14)
The symbol of the bunch of grapes ( cunqüd) connects the stone of
the sages to the ancient wine-gods such as Osiris and Dionysos. The bunch
of grapes is filled with sweet grape-juice. This can turn into wine that contains
alcohol which can bring divine ecstasy. In Greek alchemy, Hermes, the
father of alchemy, was known as the vinemaker (botrychitës),389 and in
Arabic we know of a book written by Hermes that is called The Book on
the Making of the Bunch of Grapes.390 In Sufism, wine became an
important symbol for the hidden knowledge that brings forth or triggers off
divine ecstasy, the experience of something divine within.391
388 See C. G. Jung, Myst. coni., § 603.
389 See M. Berthelot, Alch. Grecs VI,V.3, and III,VI.5 where Zosimos quotes Ostanes, who
said: «Press the grape {staphyle)» explaining later that you have to go to the floods of the Nile
«where you will find a stone that has a spirit etc.». The grape is here also a symbol of the stone.
390 Kitab cAmal al-cunqüd, J. W. Flick, “The Arab. Lit. on Alch., Ambix, 3/4, (1951), p. 91.
391 See e.g. the spiritual wine in Rubaiyats of Omar Khayyam.

[Page 164]
The Container and the Contained 163
Fig. 58: The teacher points to the one bunch of grapes, barely recognizable in the
vineleaf garment of Lady Nature. She is represented in front of a window that gives
view to the greater cosmic dimension of life that is. however, only visible during
the night. Her grape is a symbol for the hidden stone. (Picture by Hossein Behzad)

[Page 165]
164 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
Our next symbol for the stone is called «book, and all that flows out
of it books». Ibn Umail does not write kitäb, the usual word for book, but
uses the old word mushaf. This is a word that is also used for the Holy
Koran. Thus the Holy Book of the Muslims is for Ibn Umail also a symbol
of the stone, and he understands «all that flows out of it» to be a symbol of
the stone, as well. By that he means, as Marie-Louise von Franz observes,
«the Shi Utes symbolic interpretation of the Koran [that] is the true water of
life».392 This passage also reminds a Muslim reader of the mother of the
book mentioned in three places in the Koran (Sura 37.5, 13.39, 43.3-4).393
This expression is not always used in the same sense but its general meaning
is that there is an archetypal scripture of the divine revelation that is kept in
heaven, of which the Koran is a realisation on earth. The stone seems to be
considered by Ibn Umail a direct emanation and revelation of Allah.
Furthermore, Ibn Umail names the stone (here as the magnesia)
arsenic (zarnïh) and sandarach (zandanh) when it is flowing. And «when
it became dry and solid [they named it] mercury, white sulphur, whitened
sulphur and white arsenic». Again it is impossible to determine exactly
what substance Ibn Umail meant by arsenic and sandarach, so we have to
look to see what he has to say about them. Later on in our Hall ar-rumüz, Ibn
Umail speaks again of arsenic and sandarach: «And likewise they named
their stone the sea of the two vapours. And they named this soul red sulphur,
arsenic, sandarach and every sulphur that rises up in the vapour of the
people.»394 395 Then at another point in our book the soul is called sulphur and
arsenic.39^ Both, arsenic and sandarach obviously belong to the family of
the sulphurs and are for Ibn Umail symbols for the fiery soul. From al-Mä:
al-waraqï we get further information about what Ibn Umail meant by these
two symbols: «They also named the soul and the spirit that come out of this
mixing the red and the yellow arsenic. By the red arsenic they meant the
dyeing soul, and they also named this soul the red sulphur. And they named
the spirit the yellow arsenic, because the spirit makes the soul white and it
covers it with its colour. [...] [They also named it like this] because the
spirit covers this soul with its colour and makes it white in the same way
See M.-L. von Franz, Commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz, CALA I A, § 160.
392
See M. Ullmann, Das Schlangenbuch des Hermes Trismegistos, p. 144 and literature
393
quoted there. The Umm al-kitäb mentioned in the Koran points to the feminine aspect of the
self.
Hall ar-rumüz, p. 55.12-14.
394
Ibid., p. 49.8.
395

[Page 166]
The Container and the Contained 165
as the arsenic of the people makes their copper white.»396 From this state­
ment we can see that the red arsenic is a symbol for the fiery soul while the
yellow arsenic is a symbol for the watery spirit that is able to whiten the
copper, i.e. to contain the fiery soul.397 We see that in his al-M& al-waraqT
Ibn Umail calls the sulphur the soul and the arsenic the spirit. But then he
Fig. 59: Red and the yellow arsenic stones. They are for Ibn Umail symbolic
substances pointing, like sulphur or the two sulphurs, to the soul and the spirit.
states: «They named this [divine] water the arsenic, because it makes white
their copper, which is their second body.»398 Ibn Umail obviously under­
stands arsenic and sandarach as symbols for the soul, but for him, arsenic
is also a symbol for the spirit, and for the divine water. When it solidifies
in the second body, making the copper white, it turns to «dry solid mercury»,
symbol for the white coat that now contains the copper that is also called
«white sulphur, whitened sulphur, and white arsenic» in our quoted
passage. In Greek alchemy, too, we find arsenic (arsenikon) and sandarach
(,sandarachë) mentioned repeatedly.399 Democritus, for instance, speaks in
his “Physika kai mystika” about the whitening of the copper (leucosis
396 Al-Mä3 al-waraql, p. 29.17-21.
Sandarach (zandanh) = As203. It amalgamates together with copper to a white-silvery
397
substance that prevents oxidation. See E. O. von Lippmann, Entstehung I, p. 6, and p. 678. In
later Latin alchemy, sandarach was translated as auripigment, and in the statement of Socrates
in the Turba philosophorum it is the female receptacle. See J. Ruska, Turba phil.. p. 200.
398 Al-MäJ al-waraql, p. 70.28.
E.g. M. Berthelot, Alch. Grecs p. I.XIH.17, p. II.I.4, and p. II.II.4.
399

[Page 167]
166 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
chalkou) with the help of arsenic and sandarach.400 In this text, both
arsenic and sandarach, have—as for Ibn Umail—the capacity to produce
a copper without shadow, which means a copper that no longer produces
the poisonous verdigris.
205 What is confusing in symbolic alchemical texts is this interchange­
able use of names for the two aspects of the stone. Later in our text Ibn
Umail mentions that problem himself.401 According to our text, as well as
to Greek sources, what makes copper white and is therefore able to contain
its dangerous tendency to produce the lethal verdigris is a sulphury substance.
This points to the psychological experience that there is an immanent ability
of every drive to create symbolic images that can, if connected to con­
sciousness, become a container for that drive, giving it direction and
meaning. Drive and image belong together and both have a dynamic
structure (= sulphur). The ability to create images originates in the arche­
types, the counterpart of the drives.402 The importance of this insight cannot
be overemphasized, as today we so often hear desperate pleas that humans
must learn to conquer their drivenness: greed, aggressiveness, addictions,
desirousness, etc.. But reason alone has always proved to be too weak to
cope with such a deep primordial fire. A greater power is needed, as Marie-
Louise von Franz has pointed out, to counter the one-sidedness of such a
fiery drivenness. «That other power is», as she writes, «the constellation of
an opposing archetype, which today is the archetype of the feminine, and
which so far has never been adequately integrated into our religious and
our scientific images of the world.»403 Seen in the symbolism of Ibn Umail.
we can say that fire is a symbol for the male archetype of the drive, while
water is a symbol for the female archetype that gives the drive a receptacle
and meaning. The union of the two sulphurs, of soul and spirit, or of drive
and drive-regulation, makes the divine water flow. This has been beautifully
expressed in a later medieval alchemical picture (see fig. 60).
wo In the Greek lexicon, M. Berthelot, Alch. Grecs I.II, sandarachë is called «mercury
extracted from cinnabar». Arsenic is, according to Lippmann, Entstehung I, p. 33, the yellow
auripiment while sandarach is the red realgar. Zosimos speaks in his Mushaf as-suwar on
fol. 141b.1-4 of arsenic, sandarach (zandanh) and sulphur as being the three mercuries. See
also G. Brey in: C. Priesner and K. Figala, Lexikon der Alchemie, s.v. Arsen, p. 63 f.
40î Hall ar-rumüz, p. 41.13-15.
402 See C. G. Jung, Chapter “Patterns of Behaviour and Archetype", in Structure and
Dynamics of the Psyche [Coll. Works 8], § 397-420.
403 M. -L. von Franz, C. G. Jung, His Myth in Our Time, p. 146.

[Page 168]
The Container and the Contained 167
Fig. 60: Alchemist standing on a double lion. A symbol for the union of soul and
spirit, the red and the white sulphur holding each other and becoming a two-oneness,
a rebis. The union of the two makes the divine water flow. (Detail of fig. 98,
Basilica philosophica, 1618 British Library, London)
Text: (31.14) They also named it a mortar, and all parts which are 206
in it, and with it, and from it, mortars. And they named the mercury “every
thing”, because it is the origin of everything they have. Likewise they
named the magnesia “every thing”. And they named the mercury Hermes
the sage, “the skilful teacher” because it operates their stone, which they
named “every thing”. They also named the male, which they have—and
which is the fiery stone—the teacher, because he teaches the natures; [by this]
I mean the spirits, how to fight the fire, and how to be patient with it [the
fire]. This is the meaning of the statement of Hâlid ibn Yazïd, may God be

[Page 169]
168 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
pleased with him, who said: «They [the sages] said: “No human being
knows them [these spirits] without a teacher”». By that he meant that
nothing of the work could come into existence except from the male and
the female. Therefore they symbolized it by the cock and the hen. (33.10)
The next symbol for the stone is the mortar (,saläya), which is a
resilient vessel that allows substances to be pounded with a pestle. This
points to the feminine-containing and transformative capacity of the stone,
the feminine aspect of the self. The following statement that «all parts
which are in it, and with it, and from it are also called mortars» (i.e.
containers or collectors) refers to all those symbols of the stone which
emerged as a result of the devotion given to the constellated archetype of
the feminine self. These symbols were bom out of the tension between fire
and water. Pondering over these different symbols reveals how each one of
them turns into a mortar as well, thus psychologically becoming a point of
orientation. Every one of these mortars can lead to the one single mortar,
and thereby allow a gradual assimilation of the central archetype of the
feminine, which seeks incarnation in the human psyche. This leads to what
C. G. Jung called symbolic life. That means that the different key symbols
of a person become a guide to the optimal development of the unique
potentialities of the individual. All the different symbols of the stone ulti­
mately express the one myth of the unique individual: they are all, as Ibn
Umail says, «in it, and with it, and from it». In this way the individual can
become conscious of the unique underlying pattern or meaning of his or
her life, and develop a stable relationship with it.
Then mercury (zPbaq) is mentioned again, the silvery metal nor­
mally found in a liquid state and therefore called quicksilver (in Latin
argentum vivum = living silver).404 Here in our text, Ibn Umail relates
mercury to Hermes, who revealed the secrets of the work to humans. This
teacher Hermes turns out to be the fiery stone. The fiery soul itself, which
is the dynamism of the drive structure, is the one that knows, and it is ready
to give answers as to how to fight the fire.405 This points to a mysterious
aspect of inner compulsions and complexes: When asked for their meaning
and kept in this way in suspension, these emotions produce symbols that
404 C. G. Jung has written an extensive explanation on mercury entitled “The Spirit
Mercurius” in: Alchemical Studies, Part IV, § 239-303.
405 See also al-Mä1 al-waraqï, p. 4.4-6, where the sun ( =fire) is giving the crescent moon
the strength to fight the fire.

[Page 170]
The Container and the Contained 169
contain a guiding, and thus a redeeming light, pointing in a symbolic way
to the constellated archetype behind the drive. By a process of distillation,
i.e. pondering over these symbols, the subtle aspect of the drive becomes
visible. This reflective activity can connect the previously autonomous
complex to the conscious personality, whereas the same energy left to
operate on the instinctual level would have remained unconscious, just
seeking fulfilment on the drive level again and again.406 This is confirmed
by a statement in al-MäJ al-waraqï, where it becomes clear that Hermes is
also the spirit that is able to contain the fire: «At this time it [the dye = the
soul = the fire] penetrated and expanded in the bodies together with the
moist spirit, which they name the sage. And they name it Hermes, because
it is the operator of it, the one which transforms its body, the extractor of
its soul, and it is that which returns it to its body, and which revives it [the
body] after it was dead, to a life after which is no death.»407 The alchemical
work consists then in reuniting this inner polarity of fire-soul and water-
spirit with the second body, symbol for the purified psyche of the adept. In
this way the two sides of the personality no longer need to fight but can
work together creatively. On the one hand, Hermes is the hellish fire, and
on the other hand, he is the one who offers human beings the possibility of
containing that divine fire. This inner two-oneness explains why the
interchangeability of the names for the stone is unavoidable, as drive and
drive regulation basically belong together and are the two sides of one
reality, which is at the same time psychic and material. To find a way to
unite these opposites, however, as Ibn Umail points out in our passage, «a
teacher (is) needed». What he meant by that can be seen in greater detail in
his al-MäJ al-waraql: «The skilful teacher is the sage who operates their
stone. It is their water. And therefore the sages are the soakings [with their
water] that nourish and dye their stone, and which are dyed by it. The sages
named every soaking a sage, and they named all of them sages, so know
that!»408 This shows clearly that the teacher emerges from within the adept
him- or herself. Yet initially an external sheikh (male or female teacher or
master) usually carries the projection of this inner teacher. In due time the
to An instructive example of this inner teacher of the fire is given in the diploma-thesis of
B. Hoffmann, Mythologische Frauenfiguren in der griechischen Antike, unpublished thesis,
Research and Training Centre for Depth Psychology, Zurich 2001.
407 Al-MP al-waraql p. 27.6-8.
408 Ibid., p. 94.15-17; this is also emphasized by Ibn Umail's statement: «And the teacher is
the male, because it teaches the natures to fight against the fire.» Ibid., p. 94.5-6.

[Page 171]
170 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
teacher is—hopefully—able to give that projected image back to the student.
Today we could call this water which comes out of the individual the system­
immanent knowledge. This inner wisdom is experienced in psychotherapy
as the immanent image-knowledge of the psyche, which can teach the ego
by the means of dreams, fantasies, or active imaginations about the right
action and the right time for making a necessary correction of the ego-
attitude.409 The therapist has rather the role of the midwife helping to shed
light and understanding on the images from the unconscious. It is finally
up to the ego of the analysand to continue to register these images, and to
draw the necessary conclusions by pondering further and deeper over their
symbolism. Then this divine water of the symbol and its meaning—when
reunited with the individual reality—can become the sage, teaching the
individual how to contain the fire.
209 The next statement that «nothing of the work could come into exis­
tence except from the male and the female, [...] symbolized [...] by the
cock and the hen» alludes to the value of realizing the inner bi-polarity of
the psyche: It is out of this inner two-ness of the sun-fire and the moon-
water that the conscious personality is born. The symbolism of the cock
and the hen probably has its origin in Ibn Umail’s great vision, where he
saw the two birds holding each other by the beak. In his love-poem about
this vision, in his Risäla as-sams ilä al-hiläl [Epistle of the Sun to the
Crescent Moon], he speaks of the cock being the sun or the fiery soul,
while the hen is the moon or the watery spirit. There he writes that the sun
needs the moon «as the cock needs the hen».410 The cock and the hen point
to the trans-human realm of the inner polarity that reaches down into the
animal realm, and at the same time up into the spiritual realm of the winged
creatures (see fig. 61, the upper realm in this image is symbolized by the
parents Sol et Luna). The reconciliation of these opposites is symbolized by
the two united birds in Ibn Umail’s great vision, or by the cock and the hen.411
210 Text: (33.10) They also named the male the reddish, the cinnamon,
the saffron, and the blood because of its redness. And they named the female
the camphor, and the flower because of its coldness and whiteness. (33.13)
This inner teacher was long ago recognized by tribal societies, for example, as «the great
409
old man» as he is called by the Naskapi Indians who live in the Labrador Peninsula in Canada.
See F. G. Speck, Naskapi, The Savage Hunters of the Labrador Peninsula.
410 AI-M& al-waraqï, p. 3.11-15.
411 See the dream of a woman in which the question of the mating or not of a hen and a
cock was a key motif. M.-L. von Franz, Comm, on the Hall ar-rumüz, CALA I A, § 47.

[Page 172]
The Container and the Contained 171
Fig. 61: King and queen. The ‘legs’ of two birds point to the trans-human realm of
the inner polarity that reaches down into the animal realm, and at the same time up
into the spiritual realm of Sol et Luna. The union of the opposites is the child
Mercurius. (J. D. Mylius, Anatomiae auri sive tyrocinium medico-Chymicum, 1628)

[Page 173]
172 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
The male, which is the fiery soul, is then named the cinnamon
(idârsïnï). This brown spice has a strong flavour, and can permeate a dish
in a specific way. Cinnamon was used for mystical-magical practices, and
was a feature of royal regalia up till Ptolemaic times.412 But as yet there are
no further traces of this symbol, either in Ibn Umail's other texts, or in any
of the texts that he quotes, we can only conclude from this passage that
cinnamon is for Ibn Umail another symbol of that mysterious quality of the
fiery soul which is able to dye the second body.
Saffron (zacfmn), the next name for the male, is also a spice. The
purple saffron-flower (crocus sativus) grows from a bulb. Inside this flower
one finds intensely gold-red-coloured stigmas with a very strong fragrance
(see fig. 62). These threads are called saffron and are also referred to as “red
gold” because of their colour and high price. In Egypt, saffron has a long
tradition going back to the 16th century BC. The Papyrus Ebers reports over
30 recipes with saffron; the substance was also used to dye fabrics.413
Saffron was always a precious commodity, as from 120 saffron-flowers
only about one gramme of dried saffron can be obtained. Unlike cinnamon,
saffron is quite frequently mentioned as a symbol for the sought-after red
dye, as mentioned, for instance, in Zosimos’ Mushaf as-suwar.414 Also in
his Kitäb MafatTh as-san ca, we find the symbol of saffron repeatedly. There
it is an ingredient to create a substance that is resistant to fire.415
Both cinnamon and saffron demonstrate the miraculous effect that
spices have on food. In a similar way, the alchemical dye that is the unified
soul and spirit can give an added taste to human life. It seems that by
adding just a little, the individual can taste it clearly, and it makes all the
difference to his or her life. The experience of the contained and rightly-
412 See E. O. von Lippmann, Entstehung III, p. 36.
413 See E. O. von Lippmann, Entstehung I, s.v. Saffran, esp. p. 34 and p. 82. Up to the
Middle Ages, saffron was very rare and even more expensive than gold.
414 Zosimos explains to Theosebeia: «Then take that copper and cook it with airy water and
eternal water, and know that this water is good for the pounding of the rust. Take then the rest
of the water, and soak with it the rust enough to be seen with the naked eye. And know that
if it is cooked, matures, and dried after the soaking you will find that the rust has turned into
saffron. Therefore if you read in the books of the sages: “Take saffron", then it is this.»
Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 215a.13-17. See also J. Ruska, Turba philosophorum, s.v. Krokus.
Ruska considered saffron just to be a code-name for mercury (p. 285).
415 «And I tell you, that nothing in the world is better, and more pure, and more dyeing than
this rust, if you operate it according to the operation of Agathodaimon, because he ordered us to
take the saffron until we turn it into a fighter against the fire.» Kitäb Mafäffh as-san ca, fol. 78a.

[Page 174]
The Container and the Contained 173
Fig. 62: Safron-flower and its stigmas. The name “red gold” for the saffron flower
comes from the red colour of the dried stigmas, and their high price.
measured divine fire that is added to life is like when a spice is added to
food. To find this primary experience and to contain it in this way was
the original aim of all religions. But if the container becomes empty, and
therefore this taste of life fades away, this primary experience has to be
regained. This is the aim of alchemy.
Blood (demi) is the next symbol for the male or the fire. Later on in 214
our Hall ar-rumüz, Ibn Umail quotes Maria, who «named [...] their water
blood».416 By their water she meant the divine water that contains the fiery
male. Also in al-MäJ al-waraqT, Ibn Umail states that «they name this divine
water with the name of every moist thing, like vinegars, urines, milks, fats,
bloods, and semens because it has life in it.»417 418 Later in that same text, Ibn
Umail says that the fiery soul which is the male is hidden inside the blood.41 s
Hall ar-rumuz, p. 53.1-4.
416
417 Al-MdD al-waraqï, p. 17.12-13.
418 Ibid., p. 75.29-76.1 and p. 103.17.

[Page 175]
174 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
This shows that for Ibn Umail, blood is a symbol for the fiery soul and the
divine water. This symbol connects the stone to that aspect of human exis­
tence which generates the feeling of aliveness, excitement, love, hatred,
drivenness, etc.. The sought-after fiery soul, which is the eternal (as we
saw earlier), can be found in that mysterious emotional depth of the body
that compels humans to move in a certain direction. This drivenness is first
experienced as what forces humans to do certain things, robbing them of
their freedom to choose. In Hindu mythology this is called Maya. But that
same blood also contains hidden images. Thus it already ‘knows’ that there
is a meaning hidden in that “bloody drivenness”. In later Latin alchemy,
this insight was expressed in the statement: Venitur in vena, sanguine
plena (It [the stone] is found in the blood-swollen veins; source see fn 85).
The transformed blood is the divine water which contains the fiery male
by the action of the water that is the distilled-out meaning of the images
contained within the emotions, the images that are in fact symbols. In
the Kitab al-Habîb (The Book of the Lover), an anonymous compilation
partly derived from Zosimos, this transformed blood is called «spiritual
blood» (dam rühânî).419 As the blood of the body, it is the origin of any
compulsion or drivenness; transformed into spiritual blood, it turns into
redeeming redness: the fertile, vitalizing divine water.
215 The female aspect of the stone (that is the spirit) is named «camphor,
and the flower, because of its coldness and whiteness». Camphor (käjur) is
a white, waxy substance that is rather volatile, and possesses a penetrating,
somewhat musty aromatic odour and mild antiseptic and anaesthetic
properties.420 Later on in our text, we find a more explicit statement about
the female camphor: «They named the male cinnamon, saffron, the reddish,
and the red-brown, and they named the female camphor. Both of them
cause burning, because even though they differ, and their natures are
different in regard to hotness, coldness, moisture, and dryness, they have
one effect, namely to cause burning. And the cinnamon is hot burning,
while camphor is cold burning. Thus both of them are burning.»421 The
white camphor is, like the white sulphur, a symbol for the female spirit
because camphor is also an active, penetrating substance. Yet camphor is the
M. Berthelot, La Chimie au moyen âge III, p. 52.9. This book contains large parts of the
419
Mushaf as-suwar of Zosimos. See Mushaf as-suwar, CALA II.l, Introduction p. 49-51.
420 Encyclopaedia Britannica, s.v. Camphor.
421 Hall ar-rumüz, p. 73.2-7.

[Page 176]
The Container and the Contained 175
opposite of the red-hot burning sulphur. As a white, cold, active substance
it is a symbol for the subtle regulating aspect of nature’s drive-control.
The flower (zahra) is also a symbol for this female spirit. For Ibn 216
Umail, flowers have a quite specific symbolic meaning, as we can see from
al-M& al-waraqï, where he writes: «Therefore the water is their cultivated
gold in the pure white earth which is the earth of the gold. And in it, and
from it its flowers and its dyes appear by [soaking this earth with] the
spiritual water.»422 For Ibn Umail, the flowers are the emerging and visible
fiery dye in the thirsty, white earth at the time of the reddening. Therefore
Ibn Umail also calls the flowers «daughters of fire».423 Psychologically, we
can understand the symbol of the flower as the transformation of a fiery,
possessive passion with the help of the symbolic insights that come from the
water of the female spirit of the unconscious, the result being a contained
life- and meaning-giving divine energy.
Text: (33.14) And they named the magnesia magnet because it 217
attracts the iron so that it [the iron] clings to it. They named it [the magnesia]
also the vessel, because it is a body, and the body is a vessel for the soul.
And [they named the magnesia] the furnace, and the congregational
mosque because it gathers everything they have in it and to it. They [also]
named it the city. And they named it the meeting place of the birds, the
dwelling place of the spirits [...] (35.1)
In this part we find different aspects of the collecting or uniting 218
quality of the stone that is the magnesia. First it is described as being like a
magnet (mignafis), attracting the iron to it. What Ibn Umail meant by magnet
was the lodestone, as we have already seen on page 138. It is a naturally
occurring magnet that has been known in the Orient since around the 1st
century BC.424 In al-MâJ al-waraqï, Ibn Umail states that what attracts
the iron is a spiritual nature in the lodestone, a power that is concealed
in it: «And the lodestone is seen, but its power cannot be seen, nor has it
any weight. But what is seen is its effect on the iron.»425 This invisible
J AI-Mä: al-waraqï, p. 56.24-26.
22
Ibid., p. 50.9.
4:3
Lodestones are made of magnetite, a hard black oxide mineral that is strongly magnetic,
4:4
and is commonly found in igneous rocks.
Al-Mä: al-waraqï, p. 103.18-23 reads as follows: «It is a power like the heat which is in
425
the fire that burns things. The fire can be seen, and that heat cannot be seen because it is con­
cealed in those flames which you see. And it is like the spirit concealed in the water with

[Page 177]
176 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
effect of magnetism could not be explained in ancient times. Gäbir ibn
Haiyän (8th/9th century) also considered it a spiritual power (qmva
rühämya), as he states in his Kitäb ar-Rahma.426 Therefore the magnet
became a symbol for that mysterious power (mysterium fascinans) which
causes the lover and the beloved to be attracted to each other, and forces
them to cling to each other. Psychologically, the stone of the sages does
indeed have that magic force which makes a person do things that are
seemingly beyond reason, like an inexplicable love or desire for somebody
or something. This is first seen as a projection onto somebody or something
outside that then has a “magnetic attraction”, leading to an unfree state. In
the 20th century, it became popular to reduce such compulsive attraction to
“nothing but” e.g. transference, perversion, addiction, compulsion, etc.,
that has to be disenchanted. But when such an inexplicable numinous
attraction can become the motivation for reflection, then it starts to function
as an inner magnet that in time attracts the different parts of the personality
to one centre, the second body.427 Then this mysterious magic power (in
Arabic hawäss) of the magnet turns out to be part of the inner, eternal
divine fire which first attracted the seeker to “some body” or “some thing"
in the outer world. As soon as the object of attraction is acknowledged in
its symbolic meaning, the previously projected form of the stone becomes
the centre of reflection. This leads to the collection of this energy in a
newborn personality that Ibn Umail calls the second body.
219 The next symbols in our passage also hint at something similar. They
are all collecting and containing: The stone that is named the furnace (,qamln)
collects the heat, the mosque (garnic) attracts and contains the faithful, the
city (rnadïna), with its market and facilities, attracts people, and the meeting
place of the birds (magmac at-tuyür) is a congregation of different spirits.
which everything becomes alive and lives. The water is seen, while that spirit cannot be seen.
It is similar to the spiritual nature of the lodestone which attracts the iron by its power and
effectiveness, even though it is concealed in the lodestone. The lodestone is seen but its power
can neither be seen nor has it any weight. But what is seen is its effect on the iron. And it is
like the fire that is concealed in the flint stone, which does not appear except by the hand of
the one who strikes it, and its adhering to the body of the burning object which was burnt and
became subtle and has in it some remaining power from the fire.»
426 M. Berthelot, La Chimie au moyen âge III, 145, 1-5; for details see M. Ullmann, Natur-
und Geheimwiss. im Islam, p. 396 and in general s.v. hawäss; see also J. Ruska, Das
Steinbuch des Aristoteles, p.16, where all sorts of legendary magnetic stones are mentioned,
including one that attracts fishes.
427 See for details M.-L. von Franz, Projection and Reflection, Chapter 1.

[Page 178]
The Container and the Contained 177
Text: (35.1) [...] and the linen rag. Therefore, when they say: 220
«Wipe the vessel with a pure linen rag at its sides in order to remove what
has risen up of the medicine to the sides of the vessel» they meant that the
vessel gets wiped with the medicine which comes from the bottom of the
vessel, which did not rise to pick up what ascended from it [the medicine]
because of the heat of the fire. Thus it stuck to the sides of the retort. Then,
when it gets wiped with the body, which they alluded to without mentioning
it by naming it a linen rag, it clings to whatever has risen up from the
medicine. And whatever falls back returns to the medicine, which is at the
bottom of the vessel. This is the explanation of their statement: «Wipe the
vessel with a linen rag.» Therefore their statement «wipe the vessel» is
correct, without any symbolic meaning, but their statement «with a linen
rag» is a riddle, as they were alluding to the body. (35.13)
Then the stone is also called a linen rag (hirqat al-kitämya). This 221
simple piece of cloth, which is able to absorb and collect whatever has
become too much and cannot be sublimated by the process «because of the
heat of the fire», is also a symbol of the stone. By this Ibn Umail indicates
the difficulties that arise with regard to the process. That part of the first
body which cannot be sublimated and remains coarse is not to be thrown
away but has to be collected as well. In al-Mä3 al-waraqï Ibn Umail states,
quoting Asfïdüs (Secundus): «Do not be scared by what you see of the ugli­
ness, grossness, extensive dirt and blackness of these natures at the beginning
of the operation, because that dirt and grossness is turned by the fire into
goodness, splendour and clarity. And all of it turns into a dye.»428 The work
of Ibn Umail seems to be focused on recycling, and hence ultimately also
integrating that part of human nature which is ignoble, dark, corrupt, and
normally rejected (see also fig. 54). It is precisely that part which has to be
integrated into the personality for the sake of wholeness. At the end of our
passage, Ibn Umail points to a mysterious aspect of the symbol of the linen
rag, which he calls a riddle (lugz), «as they alluded with it to the body».
What collects all that has spilled out because of the heat of the fire is a
symbol which can obviously not be “pinned down”. It can only be
expressed by a symbol, although the vessel, as our text says, can be further
clarified. The sages’ mention of the linen rag when alluding to the body has
to do with the hidden side of the stone that always remains a mystery, and
this must be so, because otherwise the critics of alchemy would be able to
Al-Ma: dl-waraqi, p. 78.19-21.
428

[Page 179]
178 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz
ridicule the work, pointing with their fingers and shouting scornfully:
«Look, the whole work is nothing but... !» This is why the stone was always
named with symbols, because the sages felt that it was the most exalted of
all things, yet at the same time the lowest. Only a symbol could express this
paradox. Keeping this tension between the opposites inside, protected by
symbols, allowed the stone’s hidden meaning quietly to unfold. In this way
the adept could gradually integrate the archetype of the self in its bipolarity.
222 Text: (35.14) They also named the magnesia the sulphuric body, and
they named the sediment “foam of the moon’’. And they named it (the
magnesia] claudianus and that is when it turned white, to a white like marble.
These names are in different languages in accordance with the language of
the people of all times. They also named it [the magnesia] nature. When the
body became red they named it burnt copper. And they named it [the
magnesia] in the state of blackness the black alabaster, and when it became
red they named it the red alabaster, and when they said the white alabaster
they distinguished between it and the black one. Likewise when they said the
red alabaster they distinguished between it and the white one. (37.9)
223 The foam of the moon (;zabad al-qamar) becomes a name for the
sediment because of its subtle quality. This subtle part of the moon is the
second body, as we learnt from Ibn Umail’s description of his great
vision.429 It is the purified first body that will receive the gold that is the
divine water in the second part of the work. This is then the coniunctio of
sun (= gold) and moon (= the second body as «the foam of the moon»),
of consciousness and the unconscious.
224 Claudianus (qaludiänus) is another word for the stone in its white
state. This is an example of «a name from a different language that is in
accordance with the language of the people of all times» as our author
explains. This name cannot be traced in other works of Ibn Umail.
However, it is found in works of authors such as Democritus,430 Maria,431
429 See fig. 15 and p. 51 in this book.
430 Quoted in Zosimos, al-Mafîitîh fi as-sanca, fol. 53a. 1.
431 Maria the Jewess, Risäla. Ms Äsaf. Lib. Hyderabad No. 941, fol. lb.8, Maria states:
«That comes when you take the claudianus, which means “the high-ranking stone”. Then
pound it and cook it till the plates dissolve and it becomes a silvery stone.» On fol. 5a.l-3 she
says: «This is our silver which we made from our copper. Then cook it and repeat this, till it
turns red. This is our high-ranking red magnesia, the pyrite, the cinnabar, the claudianus, the
zincore, the androdamus, the lead-copper, the unbumable sulphur, and the rotten chrysocolla.»

[Page 180]
The Container and the Contained 179
and in Zosimos’ Mushaf as-suwar.432 These three authors use Claudianus
for the golden or the red stone. The name originates from the Greek word
klaudianos,433
Marble (riihäm) is another name for the stone in its white state that 225
we saw earlier in our text (p. 111). Lime, under great heat and pressure,
changes to marble. Polished marble with its wonderful shining whiteness
is a frequently occurring symbol in alchemical texts expressing that surpris­
ing light effect coming from a redeeming psychic insight or illumination
that allows a wild fire to be contained.434 The subtle body is the result of a
metamorphosis under painful heat and pressure.
Burnt copper (nuhäs muharraq) is another symbol for the trans- 226
formed stone when it has turned red (see p.79 f.). According to other texts
of Ibn Umail it is the final red state of the purified copper that is resistant
to fire. This symbol for the final state is found frequently in earlier sources,
as e.g. J. Ruska has shown in his book on the Tabula Smaragdina.435 The
different states of the alabaster have been set out above (p. 107-110).
Text: (37.9) Marqunis named it the ostrich because the ostrich
221
swallows embers and red burning iron and sheds both of them as flowing
water. Likewise their female stone swallows their fiery body, then it gets
dissolved by the rotting, and it [the fiery body] comes out from the female
inside as flowing dissolved water. Therefore, everything about their stone
is made similar to something for a meaning that is no jest. And because it
[the female stone] is from flying water, which cannot fly because it became
fixed, likewise the ostrich is also considered to be a bird yet it is not able
to fly. When they say alabaster, and do not mention white, red, or black and
keep silent, they mean by that nothing else but the white one. (39.4)
In the Mushaf as-suwar of Zosimos, fol. 126b. 12-14 we read: «She [Theosebeia] said:
432
“What is the hollow stone?” He said: “It is the golden stone.” She said: “Where can it be
found?” He said: “How many of them there are on our earth!” She said: “Do the claudianus
and the matitus become solidified in it?” He said: “Yes. And know that it turns yellow every
hard stone which is mixed with it.»
See e.g. J. Ruska, Turba phii, p. 28, Nr.l 1, p. 35, p. 281, and in general s.v. Klaudianos;
433
I. Vereno has discussed some of the opinions regarding claudianus in Studien, p. 59 fnl7.
See, for instance, in Kitäb al-Habib, M. Berthelot, Chimie au Moyen âge III, p. 59.4 f:
434
«If copper is mixed with its water and operated till it becomes water, and then turns solid, it
becomes a shining stone, glowing like marble.»
See J. Ruska, Tabula Smaragdina, p. 53 fn3 and p. 55. Nuhäs muharraq corresponds to
435
the Greek chalkon kekaumenon).

[Page 181]
180 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
228 The ostrich (na cämat) is a new symbol for the watery spirit that is
able to contain the fiery soul and transform it into divine water. We have
only been able to find this symbol in this text of Ibn Umail. However.
Zosimos in his Mushaf as-suwar mentions a male ostrich when he explains
to Theosebeia that after the mercury (= water) swallows up the copper (=
fire), both become solidified. This hard substance is the true dye, which he
then calls the male ostrich.436 Zosimos understands the symbol of the
ostrich in a similar way to Ibn Umail, namely that the water (mercury) is
able to contain the fire (copper) and that they become united as the true dye
(= they coagulate as solid divine water, the perfect paradox). As for the
understanding of Ibn Umail’s symbol of the ostrich, Marie-Louise von
Franz was able to show that the aforementioned ability of the ostrich «to
swallow embers and red burning iron and shedding both as flowing water»
is a symbol for the successful integration of fiery passions and aggressions.
Instead of letting them explode, they are taken inside and transformed, and
they emerge as divine water, the «source for creative spiritual life».437
229 Text: (39.5) And they named it the mould, a stone with three angles,
the mountain of lead, the triangular stone, the square stone, a round stone,
the litharge, and the eagle stone that is a stone in whose inside there is
another stone which is moving. Their stone is like that, as when the male
unites with the female and is entering into her, he becomes concealed in her
because of her victory over his red colour with her white colour. Thus it
[the red] cannot be seen. (39.10)
230 The next name for the stone is the mould (qälib), a container into
which, for instance, molten metal is poured to create a specific new object.
This symbol points to a new quality of the stone: It creates forms, imprints,
images or new beings. This describes the way the structuring archetypes in the
collective unconscious function. The archetypes as such are imperceptible, as
C. G. Jung has pointed out (see fig. 22). They are recognized only indirectly
by the images they mould around them, called the archetypal images or
symbols. The mould as symbol of the stone points to the feminine
“moulding aspect” of the archetype of the self that structures the whole
process of the creation of the unique personality from beginning to end.
231 Mountain of lead (gabal ar-rasäs) is the next symbol of the stone. B\
mountain, Ibn Umail means the vessel. In ancient times, lead was considered
436 Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 45a. 18.
See M.-L. von Franz, Commentary to the Hall ar-rumüz, CALA I A, § 179.
437

[Page 182]
The Container and the Contained 181
to be moist, i.e. to contain water.438 This explains a thought found in Ibn
Umail's al-Mä1 al-waraqï: «Marqünis named it [the stone] mountain of
lead because of the blackness that appeared on it. And he named it the
mountain of springs because their water comes out from it, and flows.»439
This shows that Marqünis, quoted here by Ibn Umail, esteems the black,
heavy Satumal mountain of lead as the container of «their water», the life­
bringing water of the sages. In Greek alchemy, lead was called Osiris,
alluding to the lead-sarcophagus of Osiris in which his hostile brother Seth
enclosed him. For the Ancient Egyptians, Osiris was the god who brought
back the life-bringing water of the river Nile. In utter introverted self­
limitation—in the prison of ‘one-self, suffering the melancholy of Saturn
(lead was always considered to be his metal)—the individual can become
a container of the divine influx of Allah, whose water can then gush forth.
The stone is then described as triangular (mutallat), square 232
(.murabbac) and round (mudawar). The circle represents the original
wholeness. The triangle relates the stone to the dynamism of the process
(blackness, whiteness and redness), the water and fire having the signs \7 and
respectively.440 Finally the square relates the stone to the fourfold
structure of consciousness, the famous squaring of the circle that is the
goal of the work, the creation of consciousness. It brings the latent quater­
nion that became visible in the first part of the work to its completion and
to a conscious realization as the square stone: «This is the complete lead-
copper, which is made out of four, and it is the magnesia», as we can read
in Ibn Umail’s al-Mä1 al-waraqï.441 These symbols of the triangle and the
square can also be found in the Mushaf as-suwar of Zosimos.442 The different
shapes of the stone are different stages of the original one single stone. From
later Latin alchemy we have an attempt to represent these different aspects
or stages of the stone all brought together: as circle, triangle, square, and
circle again. The second circle represents the first circle from the beginning
as goal of the work, the original wholeness now made conscious (see fig. 63).
438 Al-Mä1 al-waraqï, p.48.18. Ibid., p. 99.21-22.
439
See for details Th. Abt, Introduction to Picture Interpretation, Number Symbolism, p. 124.
440
Al-Mä3 al-waraqï. 68 13
441 p. . .
Mushaf as-suwar, (fol. 43b.16-44a.12) «She said: “Then tell me, O Zosimos, about the
442
statement of the sage that everything is based on three. The meaning of the body has to be
known from the width, the length and the depth of the fixed bodies.” He said: “He indeed put
this as an analogy to the four natures of the world, namely the fire, the water, the air and the
earth. They held each other, and they loved each other till they became one mixed nature. And

[Page 183]
182 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
Fig. 63: The round, triangular and square stone that becomes a round stone again.
(Stolzius von Stolzenberg, Chymisches Lustgärtlein, 1624)
Figure 63 shows the origin of the stone as the earth, matter. That
reminds us of Ibn Umail’s black earth (see p. 27). Out of this round,
unconscious wholeness comes from “the one and the other” the dynamism
of the alchemical process, the triangle that leads to the square, a conscious
that is at the time of the mixing and their gentle flavour, when they hold each other. Know that
the thin ones among them cannot be seen and it never clings, except to the thick ones among
them, because there is a relationship between the thick and the thin. Then when they become
united, they cling to each other, because the two fixed thick ones [the water and the earth], from
which is the beginning of the creation of the things comes the smoke, which rises from the dry
[earth] and the vapour, which comes out from the moisture. Thus from these two, the smoke
and the vapour, all of the air comes into being as well as the substance of the body of every­
thing that moves in the air, because the vapour became a nutrient of the air, and the smoke
became a nutrient of the fire. So from these things wonders come into existence. Know that
everything with four angles is firmer and stronger than what has three angles, because the
uneven moves quicker than what has four angles. Therefore the sage said: ‘Four angles are
equal to nine’. Because if the nine is multiplied, it would stop at the one, which is the origin.”»

[Page 184]
The Container and the Contained 183
realization of this original wholeness on a new level, represented by the
circle inside the square. The whole process is the child of a relationship of
a male and a female, the inner psychic opposites of consciousness and the
unconscious, personified as anima for a man or animus for a woman. The
alchemical process can thus be understood as bringing the self from
potentiality to a reality that becomes fourfold or conscious and one again.
This is achieved by a continuous dialogue of the ego with the products of
“the unknown other’’ who is speaking to us in dreams, fantasies, etc. The
result of this quest is a unity on a higher or more conscious level.443
The next symbol of the stone mentioned is litharge (martak). When 234
lead is placed on the fire it changes into yellow litharge, and further heating
turns it into red lead that is litharge.444 Ibn Umail explains in his al-Mä:
al-waraqT that «litharge comes from the lead when it gets dry: Therefore
when you hear about litharge they mean by that their stone at the time
when its moisture disappears and it becomes dry. And likewise with lead.
When its moisture dries up and it is burnt in the fire it becomes litharge. So
they named their stone in this state litharge, because it is burnt and loses its
moisture; at that time it becomes solid. This is because initially they named
their stone lead, and all this is a comparison.»445 For Ibn Umail, litharge or
red lead is lead with fire inside it, i.e. another symbol for the final stage of
the stone. The changing of lead from blackness to final redness by the heat
of fire inspired the alchemists to see in lead a symbol for this mysterious
transformation of the adept from an original state of just a potential unique
existence in the sea of unconsciousness (= blackness, black lead) to a
coming to life as a consolidated individual (= redness, red lead).
Then this stone is named «eagle stone (.hagar al-cuqäb), which is a 235
stone in whose inside there is another stone, which is moving». The symbol
of the eagle stone first of all means the uprising of spirit and soul above the
first body, as we saw with the symbol of the nine eagles in the great vision
of Ibn Umail. But then this name also points to an attitude that can observe
the bodily cravings from above, like an eagle. Today this is called a sym­
bolic attitude, which neither despises nor devalues the divine-hellish fire and
its manifestations by trying to reduce such emotions to a “nothing but ...”,
nor does it become possessed by it. Ibn Umail explains in greater detail in
See also figure 29 on p.92.
443
E. O. von Lippmann, Entstehung I, s.v. Bleiglätte; litharge is chemically PbO.
444
AI-Mä1 al-waraqï, p. 55.21-24.
445

[Page 185]
184 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
his al-MäJ al-waraqï what the sages meant by the eagle stone: «When they
dissolve it [their stone], their water, which was solid in it, comes out of it.
Its moving is its rising up and elevation to the air. By this they meant the
spirit and the soul that were in its inside. Thus it is a stone with another
stone inside it.»446 From this we can see that it is the spirit containing the
moving soul, each one being individually called a stone here, and together
they are named eagle stone.
236 This image of a stone, inside which there is another stone that is
moving, appeared once in a dream of a married woman with whom I was
working. She was suffering from a passionate love for a married man that
could not find fulfilment. But she stayed faithful to her love. During that
period, in a phase of despair, she dreamt:
«I see a stone in whose inside there is another stone that is moving.
I want to grasp it with my hands, but at that moment a voice says: “Do not
be childish! Do not touch it!”»
237 She was astounded when I showed her my newly-translated text of
the Hall ar-rumuz, from which she could see that the symbol of her dream
was not only a creation of a period in her personal life. She felt as if the
message was coming from a higher authority telling her that she had to step
back from an immature desire to handle or to grasp the mystery of her
divine-hellish love. Her willingness to suffer this conflict consciously led
to the gradual realization of the stone that contained another stone that was
moving. The dream symbol provided her with a consoling indication that
comparable strivings to contain the fire within had led to the creation of a
similar symbol, and that there was a mysterious process going on behind
the pain of her incomprehensible love problem. That gave her the feeling
that there was meaning in this unfathomable enigma called love.
238 Text: (39.10) They also named the male the dog and the female the
bitch. And they named the fiery body the lion and they named the magnesia
the immaculate virgin, the honoured body, the master of bodies, the body
of the beloved and the alkali. (41.1)
239 This image of the stone inside the stone is then compared to the
male dog (kalb) and the female bitch (kalba), as well as to the lion (asad)
that is contained by the magnesia named here the immaculate virgin. The
male dog is again a symbol for the fiery soul, while the female bitch is the
446 AI-M& al-waraqï, p. 47.23-24.

[Page 186]
The Container and the Contained 185
dog that is able to win over and to contain the fiery drive. This polarity is
clarified in the Mushaf as-suwar of Zosimos. In this book, Theosebeia, the
pupil of Zosimos, asks her teacher: «“Tell me about the dog and the wolf
(canis anthus) about which you wrote in your books.” He said: “The wolf
looks similar to the dog outwardly although he is different in his inner
essence. Indeed the wolf is a thief while the dog is honest and a guardian.
This is why they are both mentioned by the sages in their books. They took
the dog as a guardian, because he stays by it [the work], and pushes the heat
of the fire away.”»447 The domesticated dog, described in this text of
Zosimos as the controlling or containing dog, corresponds to the female
bitch in our passage, while the male dog corresponds to the undomesticated
fiery wolf, symbolising drivenness and desirousness. The following picture
(fig. 64) shows that this example of Zosimos concerning the symbols of the
fiery wolf from the East (= fire), and the female dog from the West (= water)
continued to be known and pondered over in later Latin alchemy. The one
Fig. 64: Wolf and dog as symbols of the fighting opposites. From the picture series
of A. Lambsprinck, “De lapide philosophorum”, in: Museum Hermeticum, 1678.
Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 77a. 15-18.
447

[Page 187]
186 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
that kills the other is the dog or the female, winning over the wolf that is
the male, and dissolving it. «[...] And from them comes a great poison. But
when they are restored to life, they are clearly shown to be the great and
precious medicine, the most glorious remedy upon earth, which refreshes
and restores the sages [...]» as we read in the poem that goes along with
the presented Lambsprinck picture.
The symbol of the lion (asad) being contained by the immaculate
virgin (cadm: batiil) is described extensively in Ibn Umail’s al-MäJ al-
waraqî, where he quotes Marqünis’ explanation of the hunting and the
transformation of the lion. This story must have been known to the reader
