of the Hall ar-rumüz. C. G. Jung has mentioned this lion-hunt in his
Mysterium coniunctionis, based on the rather unsatisfactory Latin transla­
tion of Ibn Umail's book.448 Marie-Louise von Franz has reflected further
on this symbolic story in her commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz 449
As the Arabic original clarifies some aspects of what Ibn Umail
meant by the extraction of the fiery soul (= the lion) from the body, I give
here a translation of the whole Arabic text: «Let us return to [...] the
extraction of the soul little by little from its body. King Marqünus gave the
symbol and comparison of his hunting of the lion. [...] Marqünus
explained to his mother, how he hunts the lion: “O mother, the way I hunt
the lion is astonishing. I watch him when he wants to go the way I told you
about. Then I go out ahead of him and I wait for him on the path. I dig a
pit for him in the middle of the path—he means by that the retort—and I
build over that pit a cupola of glass so that I can see when he dies after he
falls into it, and how he changes after his death from one colour to another.
So, when he approaches the cupola, I light at the bottom of that pit a fire
that has neither smoke nor flames. It moves like the creeping of black
scarabs, or like the compassionate mother moving [her hand] over the belle
of the son.” When he says ‘black scarabs’ he means the charcoal. He
compares the gentleness of the fire’s heat and its moving on the coal to the
movement of the hand of the compassionate mother on the belly of her son.
And that is because of the gentle creeping of the heat of the fire on the coal.
Then Marqünus’ mother said to him: “O Marqünus, such a fire must be
gentler than the heat of a fever.” He said to her: “O mother, indeed it is
made to be like a fever. Then I proceed, O mother, when I have kindled that
fire in the way I told you, and I put a stone onto it. Whoever knows this
448 C. G. Jung, Myst. coni., § 386 and § 409 f.
M.-L. von Franz, Commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz, CALA I A. § 185-187.
449

[Page 188]
The Container and the Contained 187
stone puts it on his eyes, but whoever does not know it, throws it away.
When I put it on that fire it exudes a smell which the lion loves. When the
lion smells that stone’s odour he rushes to enter that glass house and falls
into the pit. Then that stone swallows him and I cannot see him anymore.
This stone, which the lion loves is the female, because she covers him up
with her colour, which is why he says: T cannot see him anymore’ (see
fig. 66). Then, when he [the lion] has stayed there for some days—by that
he means when it remains in the operation and the nourishment they
receive—he extends his right paw (Arabic yad = hand). When he extends
it, I go to his paw and cut it off. He remains for some more days, then
extends his left paw. When he extends it, I go to his paw and I cut it off.
He remains for some other days, then he extends his right [hind] paw
(Arabic rigl = foot). When he extends it, I go to it and I cut it off. He
remains for some days, then he extends his left paw. When he extends it, I
go to him and cut it off. He remains for some days, then he extends his
head. When he extends it, I go to him and cut it off. At that point the killing
is complete. Then I turn to his paws and the head, for which I heat up water
that is extracted from the hearts of idols made of white and yellow stones
that fall from the sky when it rains. We gather these and we keep them with
us for cooking the paws and head of the lion. When we have cooked him,
we take his broth and put it in a glass vessel. Then we turn to the body of the
lion and cook it in that broth. By this, he means the cooking of the compo­
nents, and putting them back until the soul is gradually freed in the way
mentioned above. All of it is returned to the lion, and it is cooked with him,
and distilled from him until there is no moisture remaining in that body. It,
meaning the soul, has all gone out with the water. When he says: ‘Once that
lion is cooked we clarify its broth, and we throw away his flesh’, so when
he says ‘his flesh’, he means the sediments of the stone. And when he says:
'We heat for these water, which is extracted from the hearts of idols made
of white and yellow stones’, so what he means by ‘hearts’ is the souls of the
stones which are collected in the magnesia. These are the male, the female
and their sisters, and they are ‘the white’. By ‘the yellow’ he means the
males, the first of which is the male whose colour is reddish-brown, and the
second of which we said became male, and it has the nature of the air (see
also quote on p. 94, § 103). For them [the sages] the colour of air is red.
Regarding his statement: ‘it falls from the sky at the time when it rains’
thus what he means by rain is the distillation of their water. Then we turn
to that broth and cook it gently in a vessel of glass until it turns red,

[Page 189]
188 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumnz
coagulates, and becomes a stone. When it becomes a stone like a sapphire,
we put it in the temple. So you see, when we put that stone in the temple,
it lights up.”»450
242 This lion-hunt describes the entire alchemical work in symbolic
form. What is initially surprising is the fact that Marqünis does not speak
of the paws of the lion but of human extremities, the hands and feet of the
lion. As Jung has pointed out in his commentary to this story, the lion
symbolizes the animal aspect of the old king who is worked on in this
process. The cutting off of hands and feet from the body of the lion shows
the fact that the lion was imprisoned and swallowed by the stone that started
to allow its true nature to emerge: that of the renewed king. As described
by Ibn Umail, this lion corresponds to the first body with its fiery,
overwhelming emotions, indeed a mixture of animal and human. This
chaotic energy has to be overcome by an intelligent procedure. The story
of the lion-hunt tells us that the aggressive, greedy lion-nature must be
consciously activated in order that this energy can be imprisoned in a
prepared transparent vessel that allows this emotion to be looked at. Inside
the vessel is a magical, attractively-scented stone, called the female, lying
on a bed of fiery coals. When the lion falls into the trap, the stone swallows
it. Jung worked out the symbolism of this motif and was able to show that
the stone is a symbol of the mother that swallows her son. He states «that
in the "lion-hunt" the incest, though veiled, is patently clear. The love affair
is projected onto the lion, the animal nature or “accrescent soul” of the
king; in other words it is enacted in his unconscious or in a dream. Because
of his ambiguous character, the lion is well suited to take over the role of
this indecorous lover. As the king is represented by his animal, and his
mother by the magic stone, the royal incest can take place as though it were
happening somewhere “outside”, in quite another sphere than the personal
world of the king and his mother. Indeed the marriage not only seems to be
‘unnatural’ but is actually intended to be so. The tabooed incest is imposed
as a task, and as the wealth of allegories shows, it is always in some
symbolical form and never concrete».451
243 This incestuous union of lion and stone is followed by the separation
or discrimination of the human parts from the lion body, the human
extremities from the overwhelmingly strong animal-driven desirousness
Al-MäD al-waraqT, 32.14-33.13; this lion hunt of Marqünus was translated into Latin and
450
gave rise to various interpretations.
451 C. G. Jung, Myst. coni., § 410.

[Page 190]
The Container and the Contained 189
Fig. 65: King carrying the sun, riding the lion. This is another symbol for a lasting
relationship to the drive-soul (nafs), a reconciliation of the upper and the lower. The
king is connected to the sun, symbol for the renewed consciousness that enables
him to ride safely the fiery instinctual basis, even without reins. From the Kitäb al-
Aqältm as-sabaca of Abü al-Qäsim al-cIrâqï. (British Library London)

[Page 191]
190 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
and power motifs. This extraction of the four human parts describes the
complete analysis of the meaning of this lion-greed, lion-craving or lion-
aggression. Cutting off the head as the fifth part of the lion is then the
symbol for the removal of the centre of the drives that seek to achieve
goals “according to one’s own head”, i.e. according to the desirousness of
the ego-centred appetites. It is the final killing of the lion, a symbol of
the dangerous red sulphur that Ibn Umail openly calls Satan (saitäml) in
his ad-Durra an-naqlya. This, according to the great vision of Ibn
Umail, corresponds to the end of the first part of the work. It is the
description of the killing of the old body that is a mixture of opposites.
The discrimination of the human parts from the animal parts is what is
also called purification, i.e. a careful separation of the opposites by way
of seeing what is human and what is not. This does not come about by the
suppression of what is experienced as subhuman, devilish, or unorthodox
emotion, but rather by choosing to welcome it, albeit in a prepared container
of the psyche. This is a description of what can be experienced in active
imagination. The vessel is, for instance, a piece of paper, on which the
stone—a symbol of «what the lion likes»—is represented. And the part
which the lion then extends would correspond to a new image that comes
out of the union of lion and stone. This has to be separated from the emotion
and kept apart. Then finally all these images that appeared during the
process are cooked in a gentle heat. This means that these pictures are
pondered over in a loving way till their symbolic meaning becomes visible.
The process of cooking is quite similar to distillation as both of them lead
to the transformation and sublimation of a solid substance. This cooking or
distilling is a further process of separation or purification, an act of deep
pondering and thinking over, as we have seen in the psychological
explanation of Tumtum al-Hindï (see p. 34 f.).
244 The transformation of these five parts with the water of the hearts
of the stones which fell from the sky at the time of the rain, together with
the cooking of the rest of the body of the lion with that broth, points to the
second part of the work, where the reunion of the upper with the lower part
becomes the central topic (see fig. 65). The allegory of the lion-hunt
shows the importance of a very careful step-by-step procedure. Trap, vessel,
coal-fire, and the stone—they all have to be well prepared. After all, in
ancient times, a lion-hunt was a life-threatening undertaking.452
The sphinx, a lion with a human head, is a symbol for the humanized lion-fire-energy.
452

[Page 192]
The Container and the Contained 191
Fig. 66: The lion hunt from a picture-series made many centuries later. Left we see
the fiery lion being swallowed by the wet toad; on the right we see the lion being
captured in the glass vessel and swallowed by mother moon. Representation in one
of the Mutus liber by J. C. Barchusen, Elementa chemicae, 1718, image no. 14
and 15. The lion remained a central symbol in symbolic Latin alchemy.
The example of a dream of a middle-aged man shows what happens 245
when such a hellish fire is activated in the outer life. He dreamt:
I have to climb down into the cellar of the solid stone house of my
woman analyst. There J realised that 1 was entering hell itself and a voice
tells me to go right into that hellfire, as there, right in the centre of that fire
I would find the greatest treasure for which people long for.
After this dream, the man worked for a long time on an extensive 246
active imagination. This led him to the discovery of an unexpected treasure.
His woman analyst, who had been able to create a solid vessel for her own
divine fire, became a role model for the man as he, too, constantly strove
to create a solid psychic vessel for his fire. The container, together with the
hellish-divine fire inside it, is the goal of the alchemical work. If we take
the dream on the inner psychic level, the woman analyst of this man would
symbolize his anima guarding the inner fire, in whose centre is hidden the
great treasure, a symbol of the self.

[Page 193]
192 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
Text: (41.1) While it was in the state of blackness they named it the
coal of the mountain, the kohl, earth of Ethiopia, and with everything black
in the world. And they named it with everything white when it becomes
white, and with everything red when it becomes red. (41.5)
From al-MäJ al-waraql we learn that with all three names—coal of
the mountain, kohl, and earth of Ethiopia—Ibn Umail meant symbols for
the second blackness The coal of the mountain (fahm al-gabal) as
,453
symbol for the second blackness is quite revealing when this substance is
experimented with and pondered upon. At first glance, coal is just a piece
of solid black matter. But when held over a flame, for instance over a candle,
it starts to turn red and glows, as if revealing a concealed fire.454 The coal
of the mountain is a second black form of the stone, a blackness that hides
inside itself all the living redness that the adept is looking for. This second
blackness appears after the completion of the first part of the work. It is
now a blackness that is no longer behaving as an autonomous complex, but
has become part of the whole, just as the night belongs to the day.
Kohl (kuhl) is a shining-black substance that is prepared from
pulverized antimony sulphide.455 It was already used in prehistoric Egypt
for darkening the edges of the eyes and was known in Ancient Egypt and
the Middle East as a remedy for eye diseases.456 The same is said about
kohl in the Arabic world.457 In the One Thousand and One Nights tales, the
origins of which go back to the time of the Abbasids (750-945), kohl is
quite often recommended as eye-shadow, not only for cosmetic purposes
but particularly because it was said to improve vision and protect against
the evil eye.458 Later in Europe it was also used as a disinfectant and to
treat diarrhoea.459 Apart from here in our text, Ibn Umail mentions kohl only
once in his Kitäb al-Mabäqil as-sab ca, and twice in his al-Mäal-waraql,
Al-Mä3al-waraql, p. 25.3, 73.16.
453
This is an observation that can be made at any teahouse in the Orient where the water
454
pipe is served.
Sb2S, = stibnite.
455
456 E. O. von Lippmann, Entstehung I, p. 630-634.
Ibn Zakarlyä ar-RäzT (860-925) describes it as an effective remedy for eye diseases. See
457
E. O. von Lippmann, ibid., p. 635.
458 Ibid., p. 423 f.
Al-kuhl was used in Europe in the Middle Ages to refer to any very fine dusty substance
459
and later also any fine liquid. Our word 'alcohol' comes from this al-kuhl.

[Page 194]
The Container and the Contained 193
Fig. 67: Kohl stone (stibnite). The
shining-black kohl is prepared
from stibnite (antimony sulphide).
Already in Antiquity it was used
for darkening the edges of the
eyes, and was known in Ancient
Egypt and the Middle East as a
remedy for eye diseases.
as a symbol for blackness.460 Zosimos, on the contrary, mentions this
substance quite often in his Mushaf as-suwar. In the dialogue between
Zosimos and Theosebeia she asks: «Then tell me now about the statement
of Ostanes, when he operated the iron and he declared that the iron has a
relationship with the magnet, and that there is a strong relationship
between kohl, copper and magnesia». He answered: «By God, Ostanes was
right! Do you not know that when the iron is brought close to the magnet
it cannot but cling to it? It does that because of its relationship to it.
Likewise is the relationship between the kohl, the copper, and the magnesia.
Therefore Maria said: “The secret magnesia, which our God taught us
how to mix, is the coppery kohl”.»461 From this we can see that kohl is
another symbol for that dark arcane substance which needs to be united
with its opposite, like the iron with the magnet. Black kohl is comparable
to black lead or the shadow. This has to be united with the copper, which
for Zosimos is also a symbol for the inner god-man. In the same Mushaf
as-suwar he explains to Theosebeia why the creation of black kohl is in
fact a primary objective by telling her: «Pray night and day to God, the
exalted and blessed, that he may make you see the great stone. Then put that
460 Kitâb al-Mabaqil as-sabca, fol. 208a; al-MaJ al-waraqî, p. 25.3 and 68.2.
461 Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 164a.ll-15.

[Page 195]
194 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
stone on fire in the name of the greatest almighty God and cook it gently
for seven days. Then take it out to see whether it has turned into a black
stone. If it has, then you have operated perfectly. Otherwise operate on it
with the white one, which is the greatest secret, till it becomes kohl, and
thus the blackness covers all the surface of the composition.»462 According
to Zosimos, this kohl-stone has then to enter into the further process in
different ways.463 From all this we can see that the second blackness is an
acquired and consolidated blackness that is not rejected but is, on the
contrary, sought-after. Psychologically, it means the acceptance of the
totality of the objective psyche, which consists of bewildering blackness that
can reveal, paradoxically, light-bringing insights. It also means the realization
of the autonomy of this second psychic system from the conscious world
with its sometimes hard to understand—but ultimately healing—urges that
emerge from this realm. That is why this stone as «shadow of the sun» can
become the greatest asset of the adept, as it reconciles the opposites.
The «earth of Ethiopia» takes its name from the blackness of that
country’s people. Ibn Umail describes this symbol for the second black­
ness in detail in his al-M& al-waraqf. «Thus when both of them [soul and
body] become refined and subtle together, this unstable second blackness
becomes hidden again and concealed inside the second body, about which
the sage told us that all of this blackness is a dye. The whiteness which
disappeared and was concealed appears, and this blackness is the foetus
which is the sought-after one, and it is the greatest king. Indeed, the great
king is concealed and hidden in this blackness, and it is the gold, which
was given the colour of silver when it turned white and it made it silver.
And we need to return it back to this gold, which is concealed in this
blackness, which they name the Ethiopian earth.»464 From this we can see
that concealed within this second blackness there is the sought-after treasure:
«The great king is concealed and hidden in this blackness, and it is the
gold.» The greatest treasure, a renewed consciousness (the gold) cannot be
obtained without first accepting the frightening blackness of the collective
unconscious.
As the prima materia, which is the stone at the beginning, can be
found in anybody or anything, the different stages of its transformation, its
462 Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 116a. 15-116b. 1.
463 Ibid., e.g. fol. 49a.7, 68b.l0, 74a.4, 76a.l9, 120b.l, 130a.5, and 182b.8.
464 Al-Mä^ al-waraql p. 73.12-16.

[Page 196]
The Container and the Contained 195
blackness, whiteness, and redness can be named accordingly with every
thing that has the corresponding colour and that points by its characteristics
to a certain aspect of the stone.
Text: (41.5) And they named it the mountain of lead, and the tree 252
from which whoever eats never will be hungry anymore. (41.6)
The symbol «mountain of lead» we have seen before. Ibn Umail 253
then names the stone «the tree from which whoever eats never will be hungry
anymore». This symbol is mentioned in Ibn Umail's comment to his QasTda
Nünîya, where he states that «the tree is the six soakings».465 Earlier we
saw that the six soakings are connected to the circle of the year (see p. 122).
Their relation to the tree shows that Ibn Umail understood the alchemical
work as a continuous circular process. It is like the growth of a tree, once
the first part of the operation has been accomplished. This is confirmed by
a key passage in al-Ma] al-waraqT that I have quoted from already in
greater length (see p. 146 f.): «If you have any left over operated water [...]
and you soak this earth with one part like one of those first parts, the
intensity of dye would increase for you. It would multiply for you like that
forever, in the same way as a tree. The more you water it, the more it gives
you its fruit, never stopping as long as it exists.»466 In the same way as a
tree is perennial and grows continuously, the opus involves a continuous
living with the unconscious. Such a relationship with this other world leads
to the growth of a spiritual life that nourishes the individual like «a tree,
from which whoever eats of it never will be hungry anymore.» This is what
C. G. Jung called the process of individuation, a lifelong process of striving
for consciousness.
A most remarkable further explanation of this special tree can be 254
found in the Mushaf as-suwar. Theosebeia asks her teacher Zosimos: «Tell
me about his statement: “Whoever eats from the tree will never feel hungry
again.” He [Zosimos] said: “We were told this by the sages who continued
to look for the nature which was called their tree, until they found it and ate
its fruit. I asked them about it, and about its state. They described it to me as
pure whiteness, and they said that it exists. But your asking me about it is not
sufficient. They did not give a complete explanation of it, nor of its food.”
She said: “They veiled it then. Favour me with a complete explanation of
465 Al-Qaslda an-nünîya, Beçïr Aga 505, fol. 216b.6.
Al-Mä: al-waraqT, p. 61.20-23.
466

[Page 197]
196 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
what they veiled.” He said: “I will. But understand that I will answer you
symbolically.” She said: “Please do!” He said: “Take that white tree, and
build a dark, round house for it, with dew surrounding that house. And with
the tree put in an old man who is more than a hundred years old. Then close
him up with her, and make sure when you close it that no wind reaches the
two. Leave them in their house for 180 days. Know that this old man will
become a young boy. How astonishing are you natures! That old man was
changed into the body of a youth, so that a father turned into a son. So God
be praised, the best creator, the creator of what he wants. O Theosebeia.
upon your life, the sages were right when they named that water life,
because whoever drinks from that water dies, then lives and turns into a
youth.”» 467 4*68 This text shows that the tree is—like the stone in the story of
the lion hunt—the female that first kills the old man and then rejuvenates

Fig. 68: The renewal of the old sun by its night-journey. The 180 days are half of
the Pharaonic year that had 360 days. They correspond to the winter- or night half
of the year, wherein the Sungod gets rejuvenated.467
467 From Th. Abt, “Epilogue" in: E. Homung and B. Bryan (eds.), Quest for Immortality
Treausres of Ancient Egypt, p. 245.

[Page 198]
The Container and the Contained 197
him. The 180 days point to the old sun that is renewed during the six
months of the winter, the ‘night-time of the year’, a well-known metaphor
from the Books of the Afterlife in Ancient Egypt (see fig. 68).
Text: (41.6) And [they named it] the mixture, the composition, the 255
solid composed water, the violent lion, the blessed water which prevents
the fugitive slave from escaping, the horse, which does not run [away] as
long as it lives, the widow, the green chrysolite which does not accept the
dyes, and the strong bull with six horns. They meant by the six horns the
six parts that are rising up on the surface of the water. (41.12)
The first symbols mentioned in our passage are now familiar to us. 256
But then comes «the fugitive horse, which does not run [away] as long as
it lives». This calls for a comment. Only by a very long process of taming
does the horse, being by nature a wild animal, become willing to stay with
humans and accept them as masters. Horses even allow people to sit on
their backs and to go obediently wherever the rider wishes. For Ibn Umail,
this miracle in the outer world became a symbol for a successful contain­
ment of the fugitive fiery drive-soul (;nafs), the physical drive-energy that
can be successfully tamed so that it becomes like a well-behaved horse,
permanently at peace with human beings.469 This symbol can be illustrated
by the dream of a middle-aged man:470
I was riding my wife's horse through the countryside. Suddenly I see
that thorny tendrils have torn open the horse's coat. Through the hole I can
see that the whole horse is phosphorous inside. I realize that this hole has
to be closed up again because the divine quality of the horse should not be
visible to people.
This dream shows that phosphorus, which like sulphur is easily 257
ignitible, needs to be well protected and contained under the skin of the
4«8 Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 174b.l3-175a.8. This example is later found in the Turba
philosophorumas the sermon LVIII of Balgus (Pelagios), see J. Ruska. Turba phil., p. 161.15
f. In the version of the Turba, almost all the elements correspond to the text of Zosimos,
except the fact that in the Turba the old man eats from the fruits of the tree till the days are
completed, i.e. till the old man becomes a youth. For a comparison of the two texts, see
Facsimile of Zosimos’ Mushaf as-suwar,CALAIT 1, Introduction, p. 52-53.
469 The taming and containing of the drive-soul (nafs)has been compared to the education of
a stubborn horse. See A. Schimmel, “Engelsflügel und Eselsschwanz”, Eranos2001-2002, p. 32.
Quoted from Regina Abt-Baechi, “Cat, Dog and Horses in Dreams”, in Psychological
470
Perspectives,Vol. 49, issue 2/2006, p. 223.

[Page 199]
198 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
Fig. 69: King riding bull. Riding the bull or the horse is another symbol for a last­
ing relationship to the drive-soul (nafs), a reconciliation of the upper and the lower.
This picture illustrates how the symbolism of riding the wild bull is to be seen at
the same time as the vessel containing the fire (nafs) inside the white circle (rüh).
From the Kitäb al-Aqâlïm as-sabaca of Abu al-Qäsim al-cIrâqî. (Brit. Lib. London)

[Page 200]
The Container and the Contained 199
animal. As a lasting companion—it was the horse of his wife—this
instinctual base not only gives energy (horse-power) to the rider but also
light from below (phos-phoros = light-carrier). But this source of insights
has to be kept secret, as it is also close to the hard-to-understand Luci-fer
(from Latin lux ferens - the light-carrier).
The «widow» and the «green chrysolite, which does not accept the 258
dyes» have hitherto not been found in any other texts of Ibn Umail. For
both symbols I refer to the commentary of Marie-Louise von Franz.471
The bull with the six horns is another symbol for the six soakings 259
that we have seen before in connection with the tree. So far I have not been
able to trace the bull in other texts of Ibn Umail, but in the Mushaf as-suwar
of Zosimos we find the bull (taur) in different places, including in images
illustrating his text.472 There the bull symbolizes the male fire and creativity
(see fig. 69). The six-horned bull points to an act of fertilization which
takes place at the time of the reddening, when the six parts of the divine
water enter into the second body. The symbol of the bull with the six horns
can be understood as a continuous second fecundation, like when rain falls
on the earth. (The first fecundation, as we saw earlier, took place right at
the beginning of the work.) Psychologically this points to an ongoing
creativity that is ignited by the spirit of the unconscious.
Text: (41.12) And [they named it also] the eggs, the eggshells, the 260
talc, and the laughing youth. They mixed the names of the male with the
names of the female, and the names of the female with the names of the
male because they named each one of them with the name of the other.
(41.15)
The symbol of the egg is mentioned again later and will be treated 261
there (p. 226 f.).473 Our next symbol, talc (talq), is a white or coloured
substance that crystallizes in a laminated or scaly way and has a greasy feel
to it.474 As stone it is known as rather soft soapstone that can easily be
worked upon. Ibn Umail mentions talc in his al-M& al-waraqi as a symbol
471 CALA I A, § 195-197 for widow, and § 198 for the green chrysolite. In a text of
Olympiodoros (5th cent. ) the chrysolithos as male gold-creating stone is mentioned. The green
chrysolite in our text must be a dyed crystal or glass. See E. O von Lippmann, Entstehung I,
p. 99 and p. 272.
Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 68a.l2, 86a.8, 89a.l8, 98b. 10-11, and 133al3.
472
See Hall ar-rumüz, p. 51.14.
473
See I. Vereno, Studien, p. 187nl5.
474

[Page 201]
200 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
for the white stone,475 but also as a symbol for their purified (= divine)
water.476 From this we can see that for our author, talc is a symbol for the
union of spirit and soul, be it in the form of the divine water, or as the white
stone. The greasy feel of talc suggests that it contains fire, in a way similar
to alabaster-stone. Talc, referred to in one place as a name for the white
stone, and in another as a name for the divine water, illustrates what Ibn
Umail means by stating at the end of our passage that «they mixed the names
of the male with the names of the female, and the names of the female with
the names of the male, because they named each one of them with the name
of the other». Talc is again a symbol for the otherwise inexpressible miracle
that occurs when, during the process of the work, something crystallizes out
of the unconscious that solidly contains the fiery (greasy) soul.
Fig. 70: Talc stone. This stone is known as the rather soft soapstone that can easily
be worked upon
«Thus they name them [the five] at that time the white stone, the grey white magnesia,
475
the talc, the glass, the complete lead-copper, the alabaster-stone, the alabastarite, and white
lead. It is named with all of these names because of its intensive whiteness and it is given the
name of very white.» al-Mä3 al-waraqï, p. 24.11-13. See also p. 107 of this book.
476 «They named their ashes also talc and glass. And they named it the pure water because
it became purified from the darkness of the soul, and the materials of the blackness. And they
made its badness that is their earth disappear from it, and it is the silver and his sister, I mean
the soul and the spirit which is in it. And they named it the water clarified from the sediments.»
Al-Mä: al-waraql, p. 25.5-7; talc is also mentioned on p. 53.17-20.

[Page 202]
201
8. Earth and Heaven
Text: (41.16) And we distinguish between the names of their earth 262
and their heaven. And it is the body which we named, and the names of
their water I am going to mention [now]. They named their water four
bodies, six bodies, lead-copper and magnesia. I have already explained that
the six bodies and the seven bodies are the divine water. Their statement
about the fugitive bodies means their divided water because they were
bodies which were dissolved and rose up. Thus they became spirits. It is
the one that things cling to, and the body is the one that clings. (43.5)
Here Ibn Umail distinguishes between the names of the sages’ earth 263
and their heaven. Indeed, the main components of the alchemical work for
him are, on the one hand, «their earth», which he also names the «black
earth», i.e. the bodily reality of the adept as we saw in his great vision (§ 14),
and, on the other hand, «their heaven», which is the divine water that for
Ibn Umail is the union of soul and spirit.
Our author now talks about the names of their water, which he first 264
calls «four bodies» or «six bodies». He also calls them «fugitive bodies»
because this divine water was distilled from the black earth. In this way,
soul and spirit both became spirits. The «four bodies» as a name for the
divine water relate it among other things to the four elements or the tetra-
somia of the four metals that we saw at the beginning of our text.477
Psychologically, the four bodies connect the divine water to the four-fold
structure of consciousness that is based on the four-fold structure of the
self, the inner regulating centre of the psyche. Through the emerging divine
water, this four-fold structure of the self can become visible in time, if this
divine water is related to the process of creating consciousness with its four
Hall ar-rumüz, p. 7.1-2, amplified on p. 90.
477

[Page 203]
202 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
functions. By pondering over our dreams and fantasies for a long enough
period of time, we can slowly come to realize that we not only dream regularly,
but that there is also something or somebody dreaming us that has a far
greater knowledge than consciousness in the form of images. Through a
process of a continuous observation of, and pondering over, these images,
this inner centre that has all along been producing these images, gradually
becomes a living reality which consciousness can relate to and rely on.
The «six bodies» refer to the six waters that are to be used for the
reddening, while the «seven bodies» are related to the seven planets.
Together with the head and the tail of the dragon, the so-called two dragon
knots, these seven planets make up the nine parts of the divided-up divine
water, as Ibn Umail writes later in the Hall ar-rumüz478
This divine water is then called a «fugitive body», because the
insights which come from the unconscious are evasive. They have to
coagulate, i.e. to be connected in the second part of the work to the puri­
fied body (= the second body), i.e. to the unique reality of the adept. Then
the divine water will «cling to the body and the body is the one that clings»,
namely to the divine water. This is clarified by Zosimos' Mafâtïh as-san ca,
where Zosimos comments on a quotation by an unknown sage, who said:
«“When the seeker overcomes the fugitive then they cling to each other,
and out of them comes what you are looking for.” Know [Theosebeia] that
the fugitive is the moist one, while the seeker is the dry one.»479
This second phase of reuniting the divine water with the body is
paramount for Ibn Umail. Today, on the basis of the vast empiric know­
ledge that depth psychology gives us, we can understand that procedure.
Not a few people have the most astounding dreams and visions. But all of
this remains little more than a dream or a beautiful fantasy, if these images
from the inner world are only fixed by being written down, or told to
another person. These documents need first to be intensely pondered on so
that their meaning can be distilled out, and then, secondly, the carefully and
critically discovered meaning of these images must be connected to daily
life. As long as their meaning is not connected to life, little has happened.
Only through the coagulation of the divine water in the second body, in other
words, only when the psyche has become subtle enough to be receptive to
these insights, can the feeling of being connected to the inner self take
478 On p. 61.1-3, and 67.8-9; see also al-MS3 al-waraqi. p. 82.25-27, and p. 90.4.
Zosimos, Majatïh as-sanca, fol. 52b.
479

[Page 204]
Earth and Heaven 203
place. This leads more and more to a permanent commitment towards this
inner world, enabling the divine influx to become real in daily life. Then
one can feel and understand what Ibn Umail means when he states: «It is
the one that things cling to, and the body [the second body, i.e. the subtle
or completely receptive body of the adept] is the one that clings».
Text: (43.5) It [the water] is the ferment of the gold, the flower of 268
the gold, the glue of the gold, the aqzal gold, and the mercury of cinnabar.
The cinnabar is the sulphur, and the mercury is what is rarely found. And
[it is] the red sulphur [masc.], the sulphur, the red sulphur [fem.], and vinegar
mixed with honey. [By that] they meant the spirit and the soul carried in
the water. (43.10)
For the first time, we find here the name «aqzal gold», which was 269
always considered to be a higher quality of gold. A detailed explanation of
this symbol is found in Zosimos’ Mushaf as-suwar. There his student
Theosebeia asks: «How does it come that he named it [the gold] aqzall» He
said: «In spite of the fact that it is white in its outer appearance, it is red in
its essence, and the redness is concealed in its inside, and it is the redness
which he named aqzal.»4S0 And in another place in the same text, we find a
statement by Zosimos: «Mix the gold with the mercury, then it becomes
aqzal gold and purple gold.»480 481 In Zosimos’MaJatTh as-sanca, we read:
«When it [the water, the poison] gets cooked with the silver it becomes
gold, and the gold becomes aqzal gold.»482 All these statements point to
aqzal gold being a symbol for the conjunction of two metals, with the white
(mercury or silver) on the outside and the red (gold) inside, the union of the
opposites of the lunar-mercurial and the sun. This union of silver and gold
was well known in Ancient Egypt. There it was called djäm (electron =
white gold). It was used to cover the pyramidion, the top stone of pyramids
or obelisks, the place that was to receive the first rays of the rising sun.483
After the extraction of the mercury from cinnabar, the «male red sulphur» 270
and the «female red sulphur» are mentioned as names for the divine water.
This refers to the separation of the cinnabar (HgS) into mercury (Hg; the
moist) and sulphur (S; the dry), the well-known symbols for spirit and soul.
But now each one of them appears separately as a symbol for the divine
480 Zosimos, Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 179a. 3-5. 48i Ibid., fol. 206b. 1-3.
482 Zosimos, MafdtTh as-sanca, fol. 47b.
483 This shows that aqzal must come from qizi = red. Red was the colour of the rising sun
in Ancient Egypt.

[Page 205]
204 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz
water, meaning, as our author explains, «the spirit and the soul carried in
the water». It is the uplifted fiery soul, contained in the watery spirit.
271 This is «the vinegar mixed with honey» that we saw earlier as
oxymel, the union of soul and spirit.484 Of these two symbols, honey is the
soul, according to a later explanation in our text.485 However, according to
al-MaJ al-waraqï, vinegar is a name for the soul,486 and in other places in
the same work, vinegar is the divine water, the union of soul and spirit.487
Certainly, this confusing use of names makes not only the works of Ibn
Umail, but all texts on symbolic alchemy, difficult to understand. But as
the authors of these treatises state, it is not confusing for the person who
keeps in mind that all these names are symbolic expressions. I make a point
of repeating this: this constant connection to the world of symbols is the
only way to experience the miraculous birth and growth of something uniting
within, namely the symbol of the self in the psyche of the adept, which
reaches into matter (the bodily instincts) and into the spirit (the archetypes).
272 Text: (43.10) [They also named it] milk of a woman, milk of a bitch,
milk of an immaculate virgin, milk of the she-asses, milk of a male calf,
milk of a female calf, milk of a pregnant animal, milks of euphorbia plants,
milk of all animals, milk of the dragon, milk of the asses, milk of a goat,
the milk of every tree which has milk, and water of the tree of love. Every
body is their water. (45.3)
273 The different milks are further symbols of the divine water, «the
spirit carrying the soul». Female mammals produce milk after giving birth,
as nourishment for the new-born. But here we find some contradictions in
nature, as neither an immaculate virgin, nor a male calf, nor a pregnant
animal, nor a dragon can give milk. These different milks invite a deeper
understanding of the mysterious paradox called the divine water, which has
such a bewildering number of names.488 The insights gained from pondering
over these symbols are, indeed, like milk that nourishes, i.e. makes alive the
experience of something central inside, and makes it grow up. This is
described by Ibn Umail as a circular process: «It is the suckling which
484 Hall ar-rumuz, p. 9.2, commented on in § 114.
Ibid., p. 45.15.
485
AI-Mä: al-waraqï, p. 30.24-25. Ibid., p. 16.7, and 17.12.
486 487
In al-Mä} al-waraqï on p. 17.12-13 we also read: «And they name this divine water
488
with the names of every moist, like vinegars, urines, milks, fats, bloods, and semens
because it has life.»

[Page 206]
Earth and Heaven 205
comes to him from the milk of his mother that was extracted from her for this
purpose, until the dye becomes stronger, and the purple dye appears that is
the blessed and beneficial king.»489 The unconscious nourishes itself by its
own images that become conscious (extracted divine water = dye), and
then reunite with the second body (= soaked by the milk) in the second part
of the work. Another passage from al-Mä9 al-waraqï shows that Ibn Umail
understood that this process of nourishing the inner self (here called the
king) with the symbolic milk is a never-ending one: «The king is the ash
coming out from the ash. And they call him after the name of his sister “the
crown of victory” and “the pure eternal water”, because he is resistant and
remains forever patient in the fire. The sages also called him “the pure
one”, because he has been purified of the darkness of the soul and the dark­
ness of his tomb, which is the thick body [= the first body]. This is his
tomb, from which he was extracted, and [now] he grows up. And he
becomes stronger by suckling his milk that comes out, after he returned to
it [the second body], and he and his sister (named the crown of victory)
entered the new body. In it the colours and the dyes appear. This blackness,
in which this king is concealed, is the dyer-gold of the sages. It is the
darkness and none of the first sages was able to understand what powers
and dyes lay under that darkness. Therefore I told you that every time they
add another soaking [to the second body], their dye multiplies without any
limit.»490 The milk, according to this text, is the nine soakings after the
resurrection of the king, which is a symbol of the inner god-man or the
self. This milk nourishes him, turning the inner psychic centre into the
source of an ever-growing experience.
The milk of the virgin (laban cadrä:> batül) is of special signifi- 274
cance, as Ibn Umail speaks also of the stone as «mother of gods».491 The
stone is the virgin mother of the divine couple of soul and spirit, which are
the eternal fire and the watery spirit. When the divine fire is contained by
the watery spirit, they become the divine water or the «milk of the virgin»
that is returned to the king, the inner god-man in the purified psyche of the
adept (now called the second body). Yet, paradoxically, at the same time,
the divine water, which is returned, is also the king, as we have seen before.
Milk as a symbol for the divine water has a long tradition in 275
Pharaonic Egypt. We have numerous representations of a goddess who
489 Al-Ma: al-waraqi, p. 73.1-2. 490 Ibid., p. 73.18-24.
Hall ar-rumüz, p. 11.2, and p. 81.1.
491

[Page 207]
206 Commentary on the Hall ar-mmuz
suckles the resurrected Pharaoh with her divine milk. In the tomb of
Pharaoh Tutankhamon, for instance, a golden amulet was found with the
adult Pharaoh being suckled like a new-born with «the milk of a dragon»
(Gr. drakön = serpent) given to him by the serpent-goddess Meritseger
Fig. 71: The Pharaoh Tutankhamon being suckled by the serpent-goddess Meritseger
with the milk of the serpent. This shows how «the milk of the dragon» (in Greek
drakön = snake) is nourishing the dead Pharaoh, granting him immortality.

[Page 208]
Earth and Heaven 207
(the Great Enchantress, see fig. 71). In his tomb, the Pharaoh Thutmosis III
is shown being nourished with «the milk of a tree», given to him from the
breast of his mother Isis who is represented as a tree (fig. 72). This milk
revives him, making him eternal. These Pharaonic symbols of «the milk of
Fig. 72: Tuthmosis III receiving the «milk of the tree». Picture from the Tomb of
the Pharaoh, which is situated in the Valley of the Kings on the West bank of
Luxor/Thebes in Upper Egypt.

[Page 209]
208 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
the dragon, the milk of the tree, etc.» show in a symbolic way the gradual
deification of the deceased. Milk is also a well-known symbol in Greek
alchemy as «milk of all animals» that is equated with the divine water or
with sulphur.492 In the Mafätlh as-san ca of Zosimos, we read of it as being
spiritual nourishment: «And from its subtle parts comes out an airy milk
and that milk is the nourishment of the boy.»493 In his Mushaf as-suwar,
Zosimos equates the milk with sulphur, which is the transformed, lifegiving
aspect of this initially so dangerous fiery energy.494 In later Latin alchemy,
Fig. 73: The sages being suckled by Sophia, the sapientia dei or Wisdom of God.
(From the Aurora consurgens 1420/30, Zentralbibliothek Zurich).
492 M. Berthelot, Alch. grecs, Lexicon II.I. Different milks are also explained in the Risäla
Qalübatra malikat Samannüd (Epistle of Cleopatra, Queen of Semennud) published by M.
Ullmann, '“Kleopatra”, p. 169 f.
From Zosimos, Mafaffh as-sanca, third key, fol. 55b.
493
Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 132b.3.
494

[Page 210]
Earth and Heaven 209
this divine milk was associated with the milk of Sophia, the Wisdom of
God, as can be seen, for instance, in an illustration of the Aurora consurgens
(15th century, fig. 73).
The description of the different milks shows us the various origins 276
of the nourishing symbolic contents of the unconscious. One person may
be nourished by the gradual transformation of an overwhelming craving,
such as an unattainable love or a continuously nagging jealousy. This
would be «the milk of the dragon». Other individuals are more nourished
by «the milk of a plant or a tree», symbols of a unique individual, spiritual
life. The many different types also point to a relationship between the one
that nourishes and the one that is nourished, in the same way as the specific
milk of the mother contains in a perfect way all that the new-born will need
to grow safely in this world. Similarly, the divine soul-child has to grow on
the specific milk of its mother, the unique soul food for the individual, that
needs to be trustingly received and caringly provided for. The last symbol
in our passage, «the milk of the tree of love», expresses that mysterious
spiritual nourishment which is created from the steady devotion to the
work. This leads to a continuous growth of the inner king, the immortal
kernel. As the teacher Hermes of Dendera says at the beginning of the
treatise entitled Risäla al-Fakïyât al-kubrä (Epistle of the Great Sphears):
«Whoever serves continuously the highest light, to him things happen with
his love.»495 The attractivity of the female breast for adult men can, seen in
this light, also be understood as a symbolic expression of the longing of
men for the divine milk of Mother Nature.
Text: (45.4) And likewise they named their body with [the name of] 277
every body and a sea, hence waves, stones and seven idols. The water is
the ferment of the body and the body is the ferment of the water, and each
one of them is ferment to its companion; that means it improves its
companion. Thus at that time they became one single dyeing dye. (45.8)
The body, which is the origin of the alchemical work, is given here 278
the name of «every body». Indeed, the work can be triggered off by anybody
or anything in the material world that catches the projection of the divine
fiery inner centre. And anybody who seeks an answer to the origin of this
overwhelming attraction, can do the necessary work of pondering over the
mysterious reason that has given rise to it. This is what is meant by “distilling
Edited by I. Vereno. Studien, p. 160 (my translation into English).
495

[Page 211]
210 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
out” the divine water from the body-drives in order to gradually become
more conscious of an inner divine fire, the treasure hidden in the fasci­
nating beloved.496
~9 The body is then named «sea, hence waves, stones and seven idols».
By this, Ibn Umail refers to the emotional background of the individual and
the seven planet-gods («the seven idols»). The whole work originates from
this archetypal realm. In the Risâla Qalübatra malikat Samannüd (Treatise
of Cleopatra, Queen of Semennud), a dispute of Cleopatra with a group of
sages, she asks them why they named their body sea (bahr). They answer
that it is named thus «because it bestows benefits and a blessing like the sea.
The science of it [the stone] creates pearls of wisdom in the same way as
precious stones come out of the sea.»497 The stone of the sages is bom out
of the stormy emotions of the unconscious (sea, hence waves) with the
fateful implications of the archetypes.
The distilled divine water as a union of the fiery soul and the watery
80
spirit is then called a ferment of the body (hamïr al-gasad), at the time
when the second body is soaked with this divine water. At the same time,
«the body is the ferment of the water, and each one of them is a ferment to
its companion.» Reuniting the insights obtained from the symbols which
emerged from the unconscious with the bodily reality of the individual,
changes that given reality in the same way as ferments transform bread or
wine. But in return, the bodily reality is also a ferment for the water. This
indicates a mutual transformation of consciousness and the unconscious,
as result of the repeated soaking, which is a symbol of the continuous
relationship between the adept and the unconscious. As the alchemical
process deals with the transpersonal dimension of the psyche, the effect of
the work of the adept is quite astounding. It means that the adept not only
influences, transforms, and renews his or her own consciousness, but that
this continuous renewal of an individual conscious attitude also has a
transformative effect on the collective unconscious. This hypothesis was
formulated by C. G. Jung towards the end of his life in his book Memories,
Dreams, Reflections. There he writes that the purpose of human life is the
creation of consciousness, and then states that «it may even be assumed
that just as the unconscious affects us, so the increase in our consciousness
496 In German we call someone we love mein Schatz (= my treasure). This indicates
instinctive awareness of the real treasure that is initially seen in the beloved.
Risäla Qalübatra malikat Samannüd. published by M. Ullmann, “Kleopatra”, p. 170;
497
(my translation into English).

[Page 212]
Earth and Heaven
211
affects the unconscious.»49S This corresponds with observations by Ibn
Sïnâ (980-1037), who as Avicenna became an authority in Medieval Europe.
Seyyid Hosein Nasr tells us that Ibn Sïnâ believed «the soul of men can
also act upon the world to the extent that it can purify itself and concentrate
its energies. In most people, the soul is weak and confined to the body so
that it is unable to act upon the outside world. Some souls, however, by the
power of apprehension (wahm), can act upon other human beings of a
weaker nature, and others of even greater powers can act upon the world of
the elements. This action by the soul is, according to Ibn Sïnâ, the source of
miracles and unnatural events. [...] The sympathy between man and the
Universe is based upon the love (cisq) which pervades the cosmos.»499
Psychotherapists can frequently observe that the outer world reacts syn­
chronistically to changes in the attitude of an individual.500 This is also
confirmed by documents from archaic tribal cultures, which were dependent
on their relationship with nature for survival.501
Text: (45.8) [They also named it] the herbs, the water of copper, and 28i
the flowers of gold. They also named it the speech and the mouth because
of its coming out of the mouth of the alembic in the same way as speech
comes out of the mouths of people. (45.11)
The symbol of the speech (kaläm) is another new symbol for the :s:
divine water. It is explained in detail in Ibn Umail’s commentary on his
al-Qasïda an-nünïya (Poem Rhyming on the Letter Nun), where he writes:
«Therefore they named their distilled water talk, which is speech, because
they compared the alembic to the head of the human being, as it is the
centre of all of the merits. And it is the most honoured and high-ranking
part of the human being. And thus the most honoured part of their stone is
what is raised up from it to the top (= head) of their vessel.»502 Later in the
«s C. G. Jung, Memories, Dreams, Reflections,p. 326. See also E. F. Edinger, The Creation
of Consciousness,p. 57.
S. H. Nasr, Islamic Cosmological Doctrines, p. 260 f.
499
-xi E.g. the story of the rainmaker, reported by Richard Wilhelm. H. F. Etter, “Der
Regenmacher”, Jungiana(Reihe A. Band 15).
?:i E.g. the Naskapi Indians, a tribe living in the Labrador semi-peninsula, say that if the
Great Old Man is respected well, his messages in the dreams become more and more under­
standable. This means that the attention of the individual has an effect on the spirit of the
unconscious. See F. G. Speck, Naskapi, Oklahoma 1935.
Al-Qasïda an-nünïya, Ms. Beçir Aga 505, fol. 213b.20-23.
so:

[Page 213]
212 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
same text, Ibn Umail continues this theme on the basis of a picture that he
copied from an Egyptian temple, on which one sees the head of a man and
higher up another head. He explains the picture as follows: «Between the
head of the sage and the higher head is written: The picture of the sage.
[...] the sheikh is the stone out of which comes everything f...] [the head]
is made as an analogy for the stone, because the head has in it all the senses,
hearing, sight, smell, and taste, and the fifth is the head which unites them
in itself. And thus the stone of the sages is of four things, and the fifth,
which unites them, is the male. And out of the head come the speeches,
which are the words. Therefore speech is the distilled water from the
alembic. And it is the head of the apparatus, in which lies the stone, from
which everything comes that they need for the work.»503 From this we can
see that what Ibn Umail means by the stone is the sage or the adept and that
the head is the quinta essentia. Out of this head comes the divine word,
which is identical with the divine water (that is the union of soul and spir­
it). It is the result of the union of the four sense organs, hearing, sight,
smell, and taste. The union of the
four creates consciousness, which
allows the true word be spoken by a
human being. And that is, as Ibn
Umail observes, «the most honoured
and high-ranking part of the human
being».504
Fig. 74: The alchemical oven depicted
by G. Domeus (Aurora Thesaurusque
Philosophorum 1577). It shows that in

503 Al-Qasïda an-nüniya, ibid., fol. 214b. 1-8.
504 Ibid., fol. 213b.22.

[Page 214]
Earth and Heaven 213
The flow of words, as a result of the union of the four senses of the 283
head, corresponds to the result of the successful union of the four functions
of consciousness, and is another symbol for the well-known goal of the
opus. In the words of the psychotherapist Edward F. Edinger, we could call
this the liquid version of the self.505 As an illustration of this he reports a
dream of a woman he was working with in analysis. She dreamt after an
analytical hour that «she was with her analyst and a stream of crystal clear
water was pouring from her analyst’s mouth.» Edinger comments on this
dream, saying that «this would be an example of the divine water, which
happened to have been constellated during the hour of analysis. It does not
belong to either of us personally, and it is a grave mistake to identify with
it, but when things constellate just right, it may be activated and pour itself
out. That is what happened then.»506 The comment of Ibn Umail and the
dream of this woman recall a statement by Zosimos: «By God, I swear to
you that if you do this work in the right way, you will have a running river
of gold.»507 The union of the four elements as the goal of the opus, is
indeed the Artis auriferae (The Art of Creating Gold, i.e. consciousness),
as it was later called in Latin alchemy.
Text: (45.11) Maria the sage named it a king who comes forth from 284
the earth and descends from heaven. And it is the new-born one about
whom Marqünis said: «It is conceived at the bottom of the vessel and it is
born at its top.» He means the dye that is the soul, which they named the
chrysocolla and the honey. About this water Hermes said «It rises up to
heaven where it acquires the lights from above and then descends down to
earth, having in it the power of the above and the below. Thus it is given
mastery over the above and the below, because it has with it the light of
lights. Because of that, darkness flees from it.» That is as Maria said: «It is
a king coming from the earth and descending from heaven.» Then she said:
«And the earth with its moisture accepts him [the king].» The explanation
for her saying «the earth accepts him with its moisture» is that she meant
by the earth the second body, to which they return the spirit and the soul
extracted from the first body. By the second body, which she named «the
earth that accepts him [the king] with its moisture», she meant the unoperated
soul and the spirit, which are the moisture in it [the earth]. It is that about
E. F. Edinger, Mysterium Lectures, p. 162.
505
506 Ibid., p.c.
507 Zosimos, Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 6a.4.

[Page 215]
214 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
which we said earlier: «The relative meets its relative, and nature enjoys
nature, and being holds on to being.» (47.13)
285 The motif of ascending and descending is a key issue in alchemy.
By distillation, cooking, etc., substances become volatile, and by cooling
down the upper vapours they descend as rain, which is a symbol for the
vitalizing divine water. Let us recall the statement made by Hermes in the
passage just quoted: «It rises up to heaven, where it acquires the lights from
above and then descends down to earth, having in it the power of the above
and the below. Thus it is given mastery over the above and the below,
because it has with it the light of lights. Because of that, darkness flees
from it.» This is almost a verbatim quote from the Arabic original of the
text that was in later Latin alchemy known as Tabula Smaragdina This
,508
proves that Ibn Umail must have known this or a related text.
286 The ascending and descending is described here as something that
happens once, but it has to be understood at the same time as a gradual life­
long process. Ibn Umail speaks about this in his al-Mä3 al-waraql: «And
this foetus was created through a frequent ascending to and descending
from the air. Thanks to the long operation, something came to exist in this
operated water'that was not in it. Therefore the sage said: “Turn nothing
into something!” And thus their stone was able to make something from
nothing.»509 From this statement we can see that in the circular process of
going up and coming down, something is created out of nothing. And that
something is named a foetus in the above text, while in the quoted passage
of the Hall ar-rumüz it is a new-born king, symbol for an emerging higher
principle. Because this process of ascending and descending is the central
aspect of the work, we find this motif frequently in Ibn Umail’s al-Mä3
al-waraqï Often he starts his explanation of the process by quoting
,510
either Maria or Hermes as here in the Hall ar-rumüz. From one statement
in al-Mä3 al-waraqï,viz learn that the moisture in the earth is the soul and
the spirit which unite with the soul and the spirit coming from above. The

Smaragdina, p. 113 f. In the text edition of the al-Mä3 al-waraql, the full length of the quote
of Hermes in our passage can be found; it corresponds to § 8 of the Tabula Smaragdina.
Reprint, together with M. Plessner’s, “Neue Materialien zur Geschichte der Tabula
Smaragdina”, Der Islam 16. 1927, Frankfurt, 2001.
509 Al-Mä3 al-waraql, p. 27.11-13.
sio Ibid., p. 37.24-38.2; see for quotes also the same text on p. 15.2, 51.1-4, and 99.22-25.
su See al-Mä3 al-waraql, p. 61.7-11: «Therefore Maria also spoke about their water and

[Page 216]
Earth and Heaven 215
earthly soul and spirit are named «the sulphurs of the earth».511 This would
mean that the second body, symbolized by the moon, contains unknown
quantities of further sulphurs that are united with the divine water that
descends from above at the time of the nine soakings. The initial pair of
sulphurs of the earth are called non-operated by Ibn Umail. These two are
symbols for the male and the female in the second body. For a male adept
that would be his ego and his anima that are now working together in the
second body. The divine water (= the watery spirit containing the fiery
soul) of the nine soakings, which comes from above, is what has become
conscious from the divine fire or from the collective unconscious. This
now unites with the bipolarity of the adept, the sulphurs from below. Ibn
Umail explains this further in al-Mfr al-waraqf with the help of a statement
by Hermes, who says: «“The subtle one is more precious than the thick
one.” By “the subtle one” he means the spiritual divine water, and by “the
thick one” the earthly body. And by his statement: “With gentleness and
wisdom it rises up from the earth to the sky, thus it acquires the lights
which are above” he means the distillation and the rising of the water into
the air. And by his statement: “It descends [back] to the earth, having in it
the power of the above and the below” he means its inhaling the air and its
taking the spirit from it, and its rising up after that to the degree of the
greatest heat, which is the fire. “The below” is the body and the earthly
power which is in it, namely the regulator that is the provider of the
colours. Thus these higher powers came into existence in it, while the
earthly concealed powers were in it, and the natural operation and the
rotting made them appear. In this way the power of the earth, the air, and
the highest fire came into existence in it. Then he said: “It overcomes the
above and the below because it has the light of lights with it, and that is
why the darkness disappears from it.”»512 This passage shows that the two
sulphurs of the earth have a regulating function and a power of their own
their earth, and about the soul and the spirit which are concealed in their earth, and she named
the moisture. She said about their water: “It is a king that ascends from the earth and descends
from the sky, and the earth accepts it by its moisture." The earth accepts the water, which is
the spirit and the soul, because like both of them it has a spirit and a soul, which Maria named
moisture. Then she said: “And the earth accepts it by its moisture." This soul and spirit are
those which I named the sulphurs of the earth. And about both of them, and also about what
is in the water, the sages said: “The relative meets its relative.”»
512 Al-Mä: al-waraql, p. 64.12-18.

[Page 217]
216 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz
that is usually connected to or identified with darkness and evil. But it is
precisely this reality of the bodily existence that generates the colours of
life in the second part of the work, and this reality is able to regulate «the
greatest heat, which is the fire», i.e. to contain the divine influx from
above. In this way, the sulphurs of the earth contain the sulphurs from
above in a firm quaternion union, a result that is symbolised by the stone
that contains the fire, the «Rock of the Devil», as we have seen earlier.513
The psychological meaning of the circular process of ascending and
descending, which is initiated here by cooking, distilling and again coagu­
lating, is explained in detail in a quotation by Tumtum al-Hindi briefly
mentioned already: «Tumtum speaks about this part according to his
knowledge. He gives the comparison and analogy of repeated thoughts
which rise and fall in the mind as one changes the thinking by that repetition
and reiteration. Therefore he compares the distillation with how a human
being changes his thinking, in order to extract the soul from its body by
repeated distillation. By this, Tumtum sought to inform us and teach us
how to do it [the work], that this is the same as when a human being
changes his thinking in order that by his repeated thinking he might extract
the meaning about what he thought. So, by this operation, the soul acquires
the rank of the spirit, and the spirit is subtler than the soul. It is also the
cause for life after it was a soul, and after the soul was a body, because the
body can neither penetrate into any of the bodies, nor can it dye them,
unless it is transformed by the operation into a soul, for the soul is subtler
than the body, and the spirit is subtler than the soul. However, when the
body gradually rises by the operation, the transformation, and the dividing
after the dissolving until it became a subtle soul, then, by the operation and
the transformation it turns into a fiery spirit. At this time it submerges into
the bodies, and dyes them.»514 This surprising psychological explanation of
the alchemical process could have been written in the 20th century. It shows
that around or before the 10th century introverted alchemists were able to
513 See fig. 56 on p. 159.
514 Al-Mä} al-waraqï, p. 96.20-97.1. The quote from Tumtum al-Hindi suggests that an
important influence on Arabic alchemy came from India. Central here is the idea that symbolic
alchemy is a psychological work, and this would tend to confirm C. G, Jung’s hypothesis that
alchemy is the Western path of Yoga. Already in the lexicon of Greek Alchemy we find
chandra as one of the symbols for moon. The word simply means moon in Sanskrit (Alch.
Grecs II, I.). The Indian influence on Arabic symbolic alchemy will need to be further
investigated. See M. Eliade, Yoga and Immortality.

[Page 218]
Earth and Heaven
217
penetrate the mysteries of the psyche. They realized that by continuously
pondering on the symbols that were emerging from their unconscious, light
might be extracted which in time would become a concentrated light, nür
cälä nür, the light of lights (as a free translation of these Koranic words
could go), which is nothing less than an illumination from Allah. This light,
when reunited with the bodily reality of the adept, would finally «make
darkness disappear from it», as we read in the quote above.
The phrase «The relative meets its relative, and nature enjoys
nature, and being holds on to being» at the end of our passage refers again
to the final double quaternio that we saw earlier. It is the union of the upper
soul and spirit with the soul and the spirit of the earth. Seen in the light of
Ibn Umail's great vision, it is the illumination by the upper divine light
(represented by the three suns on the stone tablet of his vision) of the micro­
cosmos of the adept's inner world, symbolised by the moon that is at the
same time also a symbol of the collective unconscious.
Fig. 75: Maria the Jewess and the flowers. Maria points to the mystery out of which
the flowers, the quinta essentia, emerge. The quaternion of soul and spirit from above,
and soul and spirit from below is symbolized by the two vapours from above that join
with the two smokes from below. (Michael Maier’s Aurea Mensa, 1617)

[Page 219]
218 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
289 At the beginning of the first part of the work, sun and moon were
united in the first conjunction. Then soul and spirit got extracted step by step
from the body, and thus the eternal soul made the spirit eternal in the second
conjunction.515 Now this extracted eternal divine water of the upper world
is reunited with the lower second body, and this third conjunction makes it
eternal, as well.516 For the psychological meaning of these three conjunctions
I can refer the reader to C. G. Jung’s Mysterium coniunctionis. There the third
conjunction is the union of the adept with the unus mundus.517 The psycho­
logical summary of this third conjunction as unio mystica of the adept's soul
with God is found in von Franz’s commentary of the Hall ar-rumüz,518
290 Text: (47.14) All their speech has different examples and similes,
but the meaning which they want to point out with that is a single one with
no disagreement. They only differ in the names, descriptions, and images.
This is because in their body there is a soul, and in their water there is the
soul of their first stone, coming forth in the cold moist spirit which is the
vapour. And the soul is coming forth with it, and it is the sulphur and the
arsenic [about] which they say: «Make them [the sulphur and the arsenic]
rise up in the vapour.» Ares, the sage, spoke thus: «Every moisture that is
rising to the above is a vapour. And the soul that is rising up with it, and in
it, is the sulphur and the arsenic about which they say: «Raise both of them
in the vapour.» And they named it the eagle, the shining lightning and the
brilliant light. (49.10)
291 This text is understandable in the light of the explanation of Ibn
Umail's great vision: It is a description of the extraction of the fiery soul in
the cold-moist spirit that is named vapour. The soul is raised in this vapour
by the process of distillation. The psychological meaning of this process is
the intense pondering over an emotion, as we have seen in the explanation of
Tumtum al-Hindï. The result is «the shining lightning and the brilliant light»,
an illumination by the symbolic meaning of an overwhelming emotion.
292 Text: (49.10) They also named it the word. By the word they meant
the saying of the exalted God to things: «Kun!» (Arabic = Be!) Thus they
come into being between the letter käf and the letter mm, and after the letter
Al-MäD al-waraql p. 35.1, 65.26, 69.14 and 69.24.
515
516 Ibid., p. 76.17.
517 C. G. Jung, Mysterium coniunctionis, Chapter VI, “The Conjunction".
sis M.-L. von Franz, Psychol. Commentary on the Halt ar-rumüz, CALA I A, § 217.

[Page 220]
Earth and Heaven 219
käf and before the letter mm. And they named their water [= spirit] the
word, because of its effectiveness in the things they have and because of
the quickness of its perfect working. (49.15)
The stone is created in the void between the two letters käf and nun.
This points again to the creation of something out of nothing: Out of the
void between the opposites of the male käf ( ) and female min ( Ù ) the
stone is born. This creation is compared to the creativity of Allah, as is
further clarified in Ibn Umail’s aJ-MäJ al-waraqT, where we read: «Among
its greatest and most honourable names [that they gave to the stone] they
called it «the word». By «the word» they meant «kun!» [Arabic = Be!].
This comes from the saying of the exalted God: «kun!», so that that thing
comes into being from the letter käf and the letter nün. They named their
stone «the word» because it penetrates and changes things at once in a very
short time.»519 All this reminds a Muslim reader of different passages in the
Koran, where one finds the words Kun! Fa-yakün. (Be! And it is.) In Sura
4.47, for instance, Mary, the Mother of God, asks in a dialogue between her
and the messenger-angel of God: «“O my Lord! How shall I have a son
when no man hath touched me?” He said: “Even so; Allah createth what
He willeth: When He hath decreed a matter, He but saith to it ‘Be’ and it
is!”» And in another place, in Sura 3.59, we read: «The similitude of Jesus
before Allah is as that of Adam; He created him from dust. Then He said
to him ‘Be’ and he was.» A commentary on this passage of the Koran
points out that Jesus was bom without a human father, similar to Adam,
who was bom without either a human father or a mother, and it says: «As
far as our physical bodies are concerned, they are mere dust. In Allah’s
sight. Jesus was dust, just as Adam was or as humanity is. The greatness of
Jesus arose from the divine command ‘Be’, for after that he was—more
than dust—a great Prophet and teacher.»520 This shows that for Ibn Umail,
the stone is a symbol of the realization of the living creative emanation of
Allah,521 which our author connected directly to prophethood in his intro­
duction to the Hall ar-rumüz.522 «They named their water the word» as Ibn
Al-Ma: al-waraqT,p. 62.22-23.
519
520 Note 398 of the Bombay edition of the Koran. Further places with «Kun! fa-yakün.»in
Sura 2.11, 3.42, 6.72, 16.40, 19.35, 36.82. and 40.68.
This is comparable to the later equation of the stone with the Sapientia Dei in the Aurora
521
Consurgens.See M.-L. von Franz. Aurora Consurgens,s.v. Sapientia Dei.
Hall ar-rumüz,CALA I, p. 5.26, see also later p. 95.13 and 97.7.
522

[Page 221]
220 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
Umail says, whereas the stone is bom in the nothingness between the two
letters käf (the male) and mm (the female). As we saw earlier, something
is created out of nothing and this new thing is the tertium non datur, that
which is logically not possible but which is incessantly created by Mother
Nature out of the opposites. This is a well-known psychological truth:
Wherever the tension of “the yes and the no” can be borne long enough,
then, out of the void of despair and not knowing, the creation and growth
of a new centre, symbolized by the stone, can take place. This new creation
is compared to the first word uttered by Allah at the beginning of time, which
leads us to the first moment in the creation that is now repeated by the adept.
Indeed, by the alchemical work he or she repeats a second creation of a new
consciousness that is to become the centre of his or her personality, the
renewed living experience of the self as a complexio oppositorum.
294 It is possible that this thought of Ibn Umail goes back to the Kitäb
Balïnûs al-Haklm fi-l-cilal of Apollonios (The Book of Balinus the Sage
about the Causes). There, according to The Book from the Tomb of Hermes,
Apollonios reports that the first word of the Creator was «Be!» (kun!). It is
the cause of the rest of creation, and this can never be grasped by the sense
organs of the creatures. Far less can they sense the Creator [Himself], who
is far beyond all reason and knowledge. And the first thing that was created
was light.523 Similarly, the unique creation of an individual is a second
birth, compared in alchemy to birth from an egg out of which the eternal
human is hatched.524 This second birth has always been considered a new
creation, for example baptism for Christians, or the birth of an artist who
may also take a new name to express his or her new creative identity.
Everybody has this possibility, the stone is cheaply available to everybody,
as the alchemists say. Everybody is born as an original, but most people
die as a copy. From all this it becomes evident that the stone is a symbol
for the creation of a firm trust in the creative spirit of the unconscious,
which in time will give birth to the unique, individual ego-consciousness.
See J. Ruska, Tabula Smaragdina,p. 132-135 with German translation afterwards. In the
523
Turba Philosophorum,considered by J. Ruska to be a source for Ibn Umail’s work, God's cre­
ation of the world comes about with the word «Be!», out of which the four elements—whose
opposites are united by the opus—came into being. And that is the stone. This is found in
the statement of Anaximenes, see J. Ruska, Turba Philosophorum,p. 32 and 52.
524 For example, in the Risäla Qalübatra malikat Samannüd.In the second part of the book.
Cleopatra teaches that the creation of the egg out of the four elements is the first part of the
work. See M. Ullmann, “Kleopatra”, p. 173, and footnote 155 in this book.

[Page 222]
Earth and Heaven
221
Text: (49.15) And the chrysocolla is the meat and it is the nourish- 295
ment, and the flower of the copper that grasps all things. They also named
it [the water = the spirit] the head, and the head is not complete without its
body. And the sediment is the two legs. [They named it] the water of the
arsenic, the water of the sulphur, moist pitch and tar because it came out of
a dry body, flying eagle, falling eagle, fresh olive oil, balsam tree, oil of the
balsam tree, oleander, and leaves of oleander, a herb with seven leaves and
by that they meant the seven parts of the water, originating from it [the
water]. [They further named it] gums and all types of gum. (51.5)
All the names mentioned here are symbols for the substance that 296
comes out of the body as a result of the alchemical work, some of which
we saw earlier. One of these substances is now called «the head». Ibn
Umail symbolizes with the head the separated upper part in the vessel, that
which has been extracted from the first body. It is the spirit in which the
dyer soul is concealed. We also find this symbol of the head with the same
meaning in the Mushaf as-suwar of Zosimos, who states: «It [the stone] is
called the head, because the dye is concealed in its interior, and it hides it
[the dye] in its subtle part, just as the head collects the thoughts. People can
see the head but they cannot see the thoughts in it. In the same way, the
white colour is seen on the stone, but the dye is concealed in it and cannot
be seen.»525 The separation of the head from the body is the first part of the
work, i.e. the separation of soul and spirit from the bodily drives. This
separation or beheading is sometimes pictured in alchemical images (see
fig. 76). Yet, as we read in our text of Ibn Umail, «the head is not complete
without its body. And the sediment is the two legs». The upper and the
lower belong together for the completion of the individual, and that needs
the later coagulation, that is the coniunctio of head (sol) and body (luna) in
the second part of the work. High stands on low.
Pitch (zift) and tar (qlr) as symbols for the divine water deserve spe- 297
cial consideration. Both are sticky, oily, black liquids that are extracted
from stones by heating or distillation.526 For Ibn Umail, according to
al-Mä^ al-waraqï, tar is also another name for the second blackness.527
This statement becomes understandable when we investigate where tar
Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 220.10-12.
525
Liquid pitch is one product of a distillation that is already described by Pliny the Elder
526
(23-79 AD), Naturalis Historiae 16.52 f. See also I. Vereno, Studien, p. 12 fn 29.
527 Al-MâJ al-waraqï, p. 25.3.

[Page 223]
222 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
m
Fig. 76: The blue mercurial serpent-dragon kills the white female and the red male.
This is the separation of the two heads as spirit and soul from the body, the way it
was represented in Latin alchemy in the 15th century. «All their speech has different
examples and similes, but the meaning which they want to point out with that, is a
single one with no disagreement.» as Ibn Umail says. (From the Aurora consurgens
1420/30, Zentralbibliothek Zurich).

[Page 224]
Earth and Heaven 223
originates from. It is extracted from porous limestone, filled with tar of
different consistencies. To extract this tar, the stones have first to be broken
into fragments. Then they are heated till they turn into liquid. This has to
be done very carefully in order that these fragments do not catch fire, as
they contain oil. With the extracted tar, one is then able to glue things
together. Furthermore, tar contains the fire inside it, reminding us of some
