fleece inside. The wool as a symbol for the divine water describes the
natural process of the extraction work, which is, however, also a process
against nature, as the adept is requested to put the fleece in the woollen
bag, i.e. to contain the result of the work. The other symbols are now
understandable in the light of our previous amplifications.
Text: (71.8) When the stone became alive by returning its spirits to 353
it, and its turning purple by the soakings, they named it the human being of
the sages, whom they put to death, seized his spirit, and returned it to him.
So they revived him to eternal life by the natural operation by which they
put him to death and revived him. It is said he died and then came back to
life. They named the colours that appeared from him [the human being of
the sages] people and children. Therefore they said: «Our children, that
means we gave birth to them and we made them appear.» (73.2)
The réanimation of the stone takes place «by returning its spirits to 354
it» in the second part of the work. By this Ibn Umail means the soaking of
the second body nine times (we remember the three and the six times)
when the divine water (that is the union of soul and spirit) soaks the second
body, these nine also being simply called spirits by Ibn Umail in al-Ma: al-
waraql The old ego-attitude of the adept has been gradually put to death
by the first operation. That is what is called the extraction of the soul and
the spirit from the first body. This extraction of the soul by the spirit might
need further clarification, expressed in other terms. A text written by
Zosimos explains this in clearly psychological language: «As for when he
says that the burnt, dry bodies benefit from it, he means the composition,
which he called copper (= first body). [This is] because they said that the
copper is like a human being with a soul and a spirit, the soul being the
colour of the copper, and the spirit being the dyer. Also, just as a human
being cannot gain any understanding, until he controls himself, under­
stands his passions, and develops subtle thoughts about what enters his
[life], the dyer spirit of this work, which only comes out with subtle
thoughts, will not emerge unless the thick part of the work is destroyed and
the work is burnt until it turns into untouchable, subtle ash. And just as the
sage’s subtle thinking only appears when he frees and purifies his soul
from passions and concerns in the thoughts he adopts, this work will not

[Page 259]
258 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
dye unless you make it fine and subtle, subtler than any other subtle thing
until it turns into an invisible spirit.»591 This evidence is conclusive: The
psychological work of the adept on his or her own soul, as the goal of
symbolic alchemy, is evident in this quote. In the light of such documentation,
historians of alchemy will have to consider these facts and in consequence
the psychological implications of symbolic alchemy.
555 Now, after the long process of purification by numerous distillations,
the cooking, etc., the extracted soul and spirit (which have turned together
into the divine water) are reunited with the dead, white second body, which
is «revived to eternal life by the natural operation». Here this second body
is named «the human being of the sages», who was put to death and who
is now resurrected (see fig. 87). This describes the death of the old king
or the old dominant attitude, and the resurrection of a new attitude which
is in tune with the nine waters or the nine «influences» of the planets,
symbolizing the archetypes of the collective unconscious.592 This second
body (or subtle body), which gets soaked with the eternal divine water, is
turned by that union into an eternal body, as Ibn Umail says in his al-Mä]
al-waraqï. «At the time when its spirits (= soul and spirit), which were
extracted from it, returned to it, and the dyes appeared in it, they said: “The
body lives and has eternal life.” This is the meaning of their statement: “It
died then it became alive.”»593
356 In alchemical texts, we frequently find the statement that the dead
body is resurrected to eternal life. This shows that for Ibn Umail, as for
many of his predecessors, the alchemical work was in fact a quest for
immortality. The preparation for that reunion with eternal life corresponds
to that continuous, patient separation of the «upper» sun-quality of the
conscious ego-personality from the Tower’ archetypal contents that have a
tendency to take possession of the ego. But at the same time it is a separation
of the lower human dimension from the upper, archetypal or divine realm.
A continuous and patient separation and then reunion of consciousness
with the unconscious is the aim of the alchemical work. C. G. Jung
commented on the final result of the work. He writes: «As a transcendental
idea, immortality cannot be the object of experience, hence there is no argu­
ment either for or against it. But immortality as an experience of feeling is
Mafàtih as-sanca, fol. 80b.21-81a.9.
591
592 See C. G. Jung, Mysterium coniunctionis, § 291-308.
Al-Mä^ al-waraqï, p. 76.16-17.
593

[Page 260]
Fire and Water
259
Fig. 87: The image of Lucifer-Mercurius who with his one hand holds a fiery ginn-
dragon that kills the old king, and with his other hand holds or guides the adept to
revive and renew the dead king—his own old personality—using the divine water.
From Abü al-Qäsim al-clrâql’s Kitàb al-Aqâlïm as-sabaca, (British Lib. London)

[Page 261]
260 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
rather different. A feeling is as indisputable a reality as the existence of an
idea, and can be experienced to exactly the same degree. On many
occasions I have observed that the spontaneous manifestations of the self,
i.e., the appearance of certain symbols relating thereto, bring with them
something of the timelessness of the unconscious which expresses itself in
a feeling of eternity or immortality. Such experiences can be extraordinarily
impressive.» Then he adds as a footnote: «It goes without saying that these
concepts offer no solution of any metaphysical problem. They neither
prove nor disprove the immortality of the soul.»594 The alchemical work
corresponds to the creation of the diamond body in the East, the creation
of an immortal subtle body.
From this eternal inner human being that came to life, colours
appear named «people and children». This is the live-giving inspiring quality
of the self, the inner anthropos that makes creativity appear and by that
brings meaning into the life of the adept.
Text: (73.2) And they named the male cinnamon, saffron, the red­
dish and the red-brown, and they named the female the camphor. Both of
them cause burning, because even though they differ and their natures are
different with regard to hotness, coldness, moisture and dryness, their
effect is one, namely to cause burning. And the cinnamon is hot burning,
while camphor is cold burning. Thus both of them are burning. Therefore
the sages did not give a name to any of their things nor compare them with
anything, unless there is an aspect which requires the observer to contemplate
about it and think it over. And know that the minds of the people [= sages]
outweighs their imagination. Thus they did not coin examples or
descriptions except in order to point by them to their hidden stone. They
did not coin them for fun or for amusement. Likewise the water of the
sages is hot and cold, but the effect of them both is one, which is that both
burn the second body which they operate on and make subtle, remove its
roughness and its thickness, and render it spiritual and subtle like them
both. At that time, the soul and the spirit are pleased to enter permanently
into it [the second body] and to stay in it, because they have turned it [the
second body] spiritual like the two of them. (75.6)
Most of the symbols here are familiar to us by now. Ibn Umail
gives us growing confirmation of what he assured us of at the beginning:
C. G. Jung, “The Psychology of the Transference” in: The Practice of Psychotherapy
594
[Coll. Works 16], § 531, and fn. 15.

[Page 262]
Fire and Water 261
namely that pondering over the different symbols creates more and more
light, allowing us to understand some of the meaning of the quest for the
enigmatic stone. The more we ponder on this text of the Hall ar-rumüz and
compare it with other texts of Ibn Umail or books he quotes, the more we
realize that there is a hidden meaning in all these enigmatic sentences, one
that can turn into a source of inspiration. But discovering this requires
patience that is always mentioned as a prerequisite for any success in this
work. The text demands an attentive, repeated reading to enable one to
realize what could be meant by these different symbols. Our journey has
enabled us to read this passage and understand now why Ibn Umail praises
the soul that has turned into water. It is the fiery drive that has revealed some
of its hidden meaning, turning from a possessive craving for a concrete outlet
into a symbolic, life-giving fire-water. The soul which is now contained in
the spirit then can unite with the white earth, the purified reality of the
adept. It is a final union of the divine, eternal transcendent with the limited
frame of an individual life, as Marie-Louise von Franz worked out in her
commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz towards the end of her life.
Text: (75.6) They [also] named their water snail, broth, flower of the 360
salt, water of snow, water of lime, gall bladder of the whale, pupil of the
moon and heart of the sun. Archelaos said about this water that is raised in
the vessels—about which they said: «May its soul be sanctified», and which
they named the holy water—: «0, all you seekers of this science, understand
and beware of the multitude of things. I did not find anybody among the sages
who named this water that is raised in the vessels with a single name.
Everyone of them named it according to the extent of his view and his know­
ledge and what his intelligence guided him to prevent you from knowing.»
They named it accordingly copper, body of magnesia, and mercury. (77.3)
Again we recognize most of the symbols. Only «the heart of the 361
sun» (qalb as-sams) will be amplified here. It is a symbol that cannot be
traced in any of the other texts of Ibn Umail. But a passage in Zosimos'
Mushaf as-suwar sheds light on this heart of the sun: Theosebeia asked:
«Tell me about when the sage says that nothing among the bodies is purer
than the sun. And a pure sun cannot come into being except by the sun and
its shadow.» He said: «The sage was right and true. It is something you are
not worthy to inquire about, because the sun is the reddish one, and its
shadow is the stone which is not a stone. I will make you more sure that
whoever wants to make the poison of the sages without these two, makes
a mistake that will lead him to distress and the loss of whatever he might

[Page 263]
262 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
possess. But whoever makes the poison of the sages from the sun and its
shadow will obtain the greatest secret.»595 From this we can see that «the
heart of the sun» as a name for the divine water is another word for the
shadow of the sun, that paradoxical other of the logos principle, its shadow
or its heart, which is the eros principle. One more time we have in the heart
of the sun another symbol for the dichotomy of consciousness and the
unconscious, for logos and eros, for sol and luna.
When the fiery soul is well contained in the water, when the meaning
of a devilish drivenness has been recognized, then there is a shift in the
attitude towards what was in the beginning perceived as a problem. This
change is expressed in the quote of Archelaos: «May this soul in the water
be sanctified.» It is similar to what C. G. Jung said at the end of his life about
his soul: «[...] I loved her and I hated her, but she was my greatest wealth.»596
Text: (77.4) And they named this water the holder of the fugitives,
and the holder of every dye. It is the key with which the doors of wisdom
are opened. It is that about which Amüras (Homer) said: «It is a lock without
which doors cannot be opened.» It is the staff of Moses and the dragon
which has nine heads. They [also] named it coppery milky water, and they
named it four rotls and the other six parts of it are six rotls. This is what
they named water, salt, alum, water of the gum, fat and natron. These six
names are the six parts with which they dye and raise the three. (77.11 )
Here Ibn Umail again describes the water as a symbol of the spirit
that holds the fiery soul, the soul being a fugitive as well as a dye. The soul
contained by this spirit is called the divine water. Paradoxically, the fugitive
soul is depicted in Ibn Umail’s great vision as the bird without wings that
is held by the water-spirit, the bird with wings. The fire that drives humans
autonomously has as such no meaning and is bound to the material world,
yet in spite of this it comes and goes in a ‘devilishly' independent way, and
is therefore fugitive. As we have seen earlier, the red sulphur is described
by Ibn Umail as the fugitive slave, or as the escaping horse which has to be
tamed by the alchemical work.597
We have already commented on the staff of Moses, which unites
all the different snakes that would be a symbol for autonomous affects. In
Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 73a.l5-73b.3; see also fol. 51b.5-52a.2.
595
596 C. G. Jung, Memories, Dreams, Reflections, p. 192. The German passage reads: «[Damals
stellte ich mich in den Dienst der Seele.] Ich habe sie geliebt und habe sie gehasst, aber sie was
mein grösster Reichtum, (p. 196; R. and C. Winston translated Seele wrongly with psyche.)
597
Hall ar-rumüz, p. 41.8.

[Page 264]
Fire and Water 263
one version of our manuscripts the nine-headed snake is called the feathered
snake, thereby pointing to the spiritual aspect of this uniting force.
Then we have six other names for the six parts of the divine water
366
with which they soak the thirsty earth. These six substances all have a
transforming effect on the earth, and are referred to as dye. A little of such
a substance, as with spices, can have a great effect. As the six soakings
with the divine water are a symbol for the final union of the upper divine
sun with the lower ‘earthly moon’, all these six substances, «water, salt,
alum, water of the gum, fat and natron», allude to the divinification of the
lower world, once it is united with the eternal divine water. This becomes
especially clear from the symbol natron (natrüri) as it was the salt which
was used to render corpses eternal by the process of mummification. As
already mentioned, this substance was called ntr in the Egyptian language,
the same word that they used for god. As alchemy has one of its main roots
in the Ancient Egyptian mummification ritual, it is probable that this symbol
goes back to this source.598
Text: (79.1 ) This is some of what I extracted from the names of the
367
ashes of the sages which are extracted from ashes: Ashes, dust, sediment,
dung, human excrement, salt, alum, sand, gypsum, salt of the house, the
salt of potash \qalT], the face of the old woman, ferment and the ferment of
ferment, the lime of skulls, the lime of bones, the lime of eggs, the lime of
wool, the lime of marble, the lime of the moon, the lime of the sun, the lime
of Jupiter, the lime of Saturn, the lime of hair, the litharge and every lime
in which something fiery is concealed that is resistant to fire. (79.S)
Flere we find all the symbols of the stone in its final stage, when
368
ashes are extracted from the ashes of the sages. Most of the symbols we
have seen before. The different limes are now new symbols of the stone as
in «every lime [...] something fiery is concealed that is resistant to fire».
Lime is the popular name for calcium carbonate (CaC03) but also for
calcium oxide (CaO) or calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH2)). Back in ancient
times burnt lime or quicklime was already produced by intense burning of
shells or limestones. It was important in construction. When water is added
to burnt lime it turns into hydrated or slaked lime, producing a reaction
involving remarkable heat. When gypsum, sand, etc., are added, it turns
598 For further details, see M.-L. von Franz, Dreams and Death, especially p. 92 and also
p. 98.

[Page 265]
264 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
into mortar. This creation of heat by adding water to quicklime gave early
alchemists the idea that quicklime was able to store the fire from the
burning inside it, and that it can give this fire back. Thus it symbolizes the
truth of the stone in which the eternal fire is well contained. Already in
Greek alchemy, lime was understood in this way, as can be seen, for
instance, in the following passage for Zosimos’ Majatïh as-san ca\ «I asked
him: “Why did they name the composition lime and yolk?” He replied:
“The lime was a dry stone. When it was cooked, it inhaled the spirit of the
fire and its purity and hid it in its inside. And our composition is like that,
taking the purity of the spirit and thus being purified. Then it hid it in its
inside.”»599
369 «The salt of potash» (qaliK2C03) is the alkaline part of all ashes
that remains after burning plants, apart from those that grow in or near the
sea. It has a purifying effect, known since antiquity. Because of its purifying
effect, potash has a close relationship to alum.
370 Text: (79.9) Marqünus named it the liver of the earth, and he named
it earth of gold, earth of silver, earth of pearls, and he named it Hermes,
controller of the spirits. And it is the rennet, the sponge, the glass, the talc,
the earth of metals, the mother of colours, the mother of dyes, idols, the
mother of the boys, the mother of gods, the dead king in his tomb, the solid
water, the strange bodily air, and the eternal water. And [it is named] after
every durable salt, the rock salt and others like it. And it is the welder and
welding, the bones of the elephant, the whitened litharge, the shackle, the
holder, the clasper, the controller, the seeker, the falling eagle, the earth of
Ethiopia, the earth of Egypt, the shell of the snail, the white marcasite, the
yellow marcasite and the white sulphur which neither bums nor gets burnt.
It is the marinai white zinc, the vapour of the earth, the moon of the earth,
the mother of the two seas, the clay of the sea, the body of the sources, the
body of magnesia, the kohl, the stone of the eagle, a calf, a chameleon, the
thirsty holy earth, the skin, the crown of victory, the crown of everything,
the fiery body and the earthen zinc ore, which is the dried up earth that is
longing for its water. And [they named it] with the name of everything and
names which have been given before, like magnesia, the ashes of fats, the
ashes of the holm-oak. the tower, the furnace, the chrysocolla, the stranger,
599 Mafâtlh as-sanca, fol. 72a.l0-13.
599 Ibid., fol. 72a. 10-13.

[Page 266]
Fire and Water 265
nature, being, the bubbles, the tail of the dragon, the white firewood, the
solid poison, the spiritual body, the storehouses, and the raincloud. And the
raincloud is the cloud and the rainclouds are the rainy clouds, carrying the
water. (83.11)
In this passage, towards the end of the part of his work dealing with 371
symbols, Ibn Umail seems to be entering a somewhat excited state. He
enumerates many names that he has already mentioned before, giving
hardly any more explanations. It simply seems to be important to him that
all that he has been talking about is finally One, and One alone. It is as if
he were testifying that there is no God but God.
Concerning the symbol the liver of the earth (kibd al-ard), Ibn 372
Umail gives some extensive explanations in his al-Mä: al-waraqï, where
he says: «The king Marqünus named this soul which is in the body the liver
of the earth, comparing it with the liver of the human being. This is because
the water takes colour from it, in the same way as the blood in our bodies
takes colour from the liver. Therefore Marqünus named the soul that is in
their body the liver of the earth, because the divine water takes colour from
it, and from the crown of victory, which is [a name for] the ashes. Thus the
dye which is in the water becomes stronger by that. By the dye I mean the
soul which is in the water, and that is because it was white in its outer
appearance and red in its inside. Thus the concealed redness appears—
when it enters their white earth—through that soul which Marqunis named
the liver of the earth, because it made the colour of the soul concealed in
the white water to appear. Thus the great thing of huge importance, the
exalted dye and the splendid matter come into being. Therefore Maria also
spoke about their water and their earth, and about the soul and the spirit
which are concealed in their earth, and she named it the moisture, and she
said about their water: “It is a king that ascends from the earth and
descends from the sky, and the earth accepts it by its moisture.” But indeed
the earth accepted the water, which is the spirit and the soul, because like
them it has a spirit and a soul, which Maria named moisture, saying: “And
the earth accepts it by its moisture.” This soul and spirit are those which I
named the sulphurs of the earth. The sages said about both of them, and
also about what is in the water: “The relative encounters its relative.”»600
«And also these dyes that are the soul get stronger with the sulphur that is
600 Al-Ma> al-waraqï, p. 61,1-11.

[Page 267]
266 Commentary on the Hall ar-rwnuz
in the body, which is concealed in it. By that I mean its eternal soul which
is residing in it, which Marqünus called the liver of the earth, because its
colour appears caused by the earthly soul, in the same way as the blood
takes colour from the liver of the animal. Thus they make each other
stronger and both perform one action. And the body, which is the dyed one,
and the water, which is the dyer, become one dye.»601 And in another place
in Ibn Umail’s al-Mä] al-waraql, we read: «Marqünis named this soul
which is in their body, and which is in their earth, the liver of the earth.
This is an analogy because the dye of the water appears, caused by what is
in their body. And the liver of the earth—and this is a true statement that is
not symbolic (probably in the sense that they are synonyms)—is the ashes
of the ashes. And I call it the sulphurs of the earth, because the dyes appear
in the spiritual water, caused by it in the same way as the colours of the
flowers of trees and plants appear by the white water.»602 All this now brings
the adept and his liver, his life-giving mother, into focus. We will see more
about that in the last passage about the symbolic work, before Ibn Umail
changes to a completely different topic about the seeker him- or herself.
373 Text: (85.1) They named it with all the names of magnesia because
he is her son, and from her he came out and became corporeal, and
resisted the fire. And he is the brother of the soul to whom the magnesia
has given birth, and ascended to the air. He said: «This is the brother who
said: I cannot do anything except with my sister, and my sister cannot do
anything without me. My sister and I, we cannot do anything except with
our mother.» He meant that he could not do anything except with his sister.
And his sister, who is the upper heavenly soul, cannot do anything without
him, because he is the one who constrains her and he is her controller, and
when he holds her, she is a dyer. He and his sister cannot do anything
except with their mother. And their mother is the water, which operates
them both, the spiritual spirit. She is the one whom the sages named the sage,
who never left them [the sages] during the operation until they both reached
the end and perfection. And she [the mother] is the organizer and nourisher
of both of them. Peace be upon him who follows the right path. (85.15)
374 This passage is the culmination of the Hall ar-mmüz, showing the result
and summarizing the effect of the work. The magnesia as a symbol of the
unconscious gave birth to her son, the stone, that became a real experience
Al-Mä: al-waraqï,
601 p. 65.24-28.
602 Ibid., p.77.25-78.1.

[Page 268]
Fire and Water 267
for the adept. The main quality of the stone is that it is «resistant to fire». This
points to the solid container for the divine fire. The spirit who ascended to
the air, is now all of a sudden the male, and he is the brother of his «sister,
who is the upper, heavenly soul», whom he is able to constrain and control.
This shows that both spirit and soul have now changed gender. At the very
end of the symbolic part of the Hall ar-rumüz this surprising transforma­
tion takes place: The watery spirit, which has always been the female
before, is suddenly called the male, while the male fiery soul from before
has also changed gender and is now, as «the upper heavenly soul», the
sister of the male spirit. And the magnesia that is the stone «who became
now ‘corpo-real’» has given birth to both of them.
Throughout the work, the male fiery soul was symbolized by a 375
corresponding substance or animal: sulphur, arsenic, ammonia, horse,
lion, etc. Now, at the end of the work, this fiery male has entered the human
realm. Humanized, it has taken a female human shape. Seen from a man’s
psyche, this «upper heavenly soul» that has transformed from initial driven­
ness into a heavenly female is what could be called the archetypal feminine
or the eros-principle that has become conscious. This «upper heavenly soul»,
which was born from the magnesia as symbol of the first stone, could now
be called «the daughter of the fire». In modem psychological terms and from
the point of view of the male adept, we would call this sister a symbol of the
female aspect of the self.
The same thing occurs with the female watery spirit. All along she 376
was named after all sorts of substances that were able to contain the fiery
male soul that we understood as drive-soul. Now this spirit also appears in
human shape and turns into the brother of the «upper heavenly soul». In
psychological terms and from the point of view of the male adept, we would
call the brother a symbol for the logos-principle or for the male aspect of the
self. This upper spirit is now the new sun or new king, which emerged out of
the sea, the heavenly spirit or «the son of the water». In Pharaonic Egypt, this
was Homs, the son of Osiris who was considered to be the first Pharaoh. This
illustrates how alchemy continued the crucially important symbolism that
was developed in Ancient Egypt, a culture that was devoted to the constant
renewal of their highest principle embodied in the Horus-Pharaoh.
Together, brother and sister are the perfect lead-copper (,abär-nuhäs 377
tâmm), the stable union of lead and copper that has changed to silver and
gold. It is the marriage of Sun-King and Moon-Queen, as becomes clear

[Page 269]
268 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz
from al-Mä: al-waraql, where, towards the end of the book, Ibn Umail
writes: «They name it the gold, and the gold is related to the sun, and the
sun is the bigger one. And this soul, which is the red sulphur, which is the
gold of the sages, Gäbir named his bigger brother, which appears at the end
of time. And his smaller brother is the ashes coming out from the ashes.
The third one is the body to which they return. And they named these ashes
the moon of the earth and the moon of the night. And they name the soul
the sun of the daytime. And the spirit is the operator of the stone that comes
out with the soul, with which it came out of the body. And the spirit is cold-
moist and it is related to the water. And the water is related to the moon. And
the moon is the smaller brother of Gäbir. And it is the one which has the
[operating] effect. Thus he pointed to the moon by the effect which is in
the moon, and it is smaller than the sun. The earthly moon is the crown of
victory and it is truly the brother of the soul. And he pointed to the sun by
saying the bigger one.»603
378 At the end there now comes a surprising turn to which Marie-Louise
von Franz referred in her commentary: «This part of the text is atypical. In
most alchemical texts, when the couple and the coniunctio appear, the
mother disappears, but here she remains as a kind of embracing matrix in
which the coniunctio takes place. This reveals that the realization of the
coniunctio has remained for the author in a state of projection. This is so
for all adepts who tried to bring forth the coniunctio in the retort, and not
in their psyche. This, however, is an advantage. Compared to the later Sufi
texts, the alchemical mystery is treated as a completely objective event and
not pulled into the sphere of subjectivism. In this respect our author is more
naive, but through that also more profound than some of the later Sufi
masters. It seems to me, as far as I know, that in the later Sufi movement
the emphasis shifted more and more towards a worshipping of the Father
Spirit and away from Mother Nature. In our text, on the contrary, we have
a subtle balance between the two aspects of the divinity.»604
379 With today’s knowledge of Ibn UmaiTs other writings, this comment
can be amplified and clarified further. Al-Mä: al-waraqî shows that he
already had a view of a marriage quarternion after the great vision and after
his poem called Risälat as-sams ilä al-hiläl. The main feature of sun and
moon is their male-female aspect. In the Arabic language, the sun is of
Al-MäD al-waraqï, p. 94.27-95.5.
603
604 M.-L. von Franz, Commentary to the Hall ar-rumüz, CALA I A, § 283.

[Page 270]
Fire and Water 269
feminine gender, but in Ibn Umail’s poem the moon addresses the sun as
being dry and hot, using adjectives with male endings naming her, the sun,
«the father of the luminaries». The same male-female aspect is found for
the moon, which has the masculine gender in Arabic. In the poem, ‘he’, the
moon, is clearly the female partner to the sun, covering him/her with
his/her blackness. In this poem, sun and moon are in each case symbolised
by two metals, the sun by copper and iron, and the moon by lead and tin.
This view of the inner polarity of sun and moon originates in Ibn Umail’s
great vision. This bi-polarity in the luminaries points to the origin of bi­
polarity in matter that is then encountered in human beings as well, in their
body as well as their psyche: every human carries his or her opposite gender
in his body and his psyche, as C. G. Jung was able to make visible in the
concept of the anima and the animus. It is this pattern that lies behind the
Fig. 88: Dux tibi natura (Let nature be your guide). The adept follows the foot­
prints of Mother Nature, «she who organizes and nourishes the process». (From
Michael Maier’s Atalanta fugiens 1618)

[Page 271]
270 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
relationship of Zosimos and Theosebeia (see introduction to Zosimos’
Mushaf as-suwar), known today in the psychology of transference with its
personal and transpersonal dimension.
Now the text includes the mother of the brother and the sister—the
divine couple of soul and spirit. Of her we read that «the sages named her
the sage». She is the water which has guided the sage throughout the work,
as Ibn Umail explains further, «never leaving him [them] during the
operation until they both reach the end and perfection. And she, the moth­
er, is the organizer and nourisher of both of them. Peace be upon him who
follows the right path.» Here Ibn Umail points in a most discreet way to a
shocking fact: Mother Nature, who was behind the whole alchemical
process, together with the adept and his bodily reality—who was the servant
to this process—are now the other couple to emerge out of the process.
Thus we have a marriage quaternion, consisting on the one hand of the
brother and sister, the upper Sun-spirit and Moon-soul, and on the other
hand the lower couple the individual companion of the adept, his or her
Daena-mother-water 604 as well as the adept him- or herself. These two
were named before by Ibn Umail «the sulphurs of the earth» (see fig. 89).
This last passage from the symbolic alchemical work of the Hall
ar-rumüz shows that the mother of the work is the inborn principium
individuationis hidden in the unconscious. However, it needs the commit­
ment of the adept to follow the footprints of Mother Nature in order to
bring the stone into reality (see fig. 88).
The adept has to organize the work and nourish it, while at the same
time the whole process is organized and nourished by the unconscious. The
mother of the work, and discreetly the adept as well, are in this passage not
leaving the process. They are—by now firmly united as a couple—witnessing
the other divine brother-sister couple that joined as divine water and divine
fire, longing for each other, and finally holding each other in a mutual
embrace (see fig. 90). This new stable quaternion, which unites the four,
the adept and his anima (or animus) and the male-female aspect of the self,
is the goal of the work. It contains in its centre the divine child, a new born
psychic reality, that also includes the dark and female aspects of the divine,
the unique bodily reality of the individual. This is stone of the sages, the
perfect lead-copper. 605
605 Daena is the divine anima of the man in Persian mysticism. See H. Corbin. Spiritual
body and Celestial Earth, esp. p. 3-105.

[Page 272]
271
Fig. 89: Sun-Moon conjunction, surrounded by the four evangelists. This picture
shows the goal of the work, the emerging of a more complete image of the divine,
uniting sun and moon, the day-world with the night-world, or the One and the
Many. This picture is from Abü al-Qäsim al-cIräqi’s Kitäb al-Aqalïm as-sabaca,
which also contains three pictures that originate in Zosimos’ Mushaf as-suwar.
Thus it must have a Christian origin, as this developed symbolism of the self is sur­
rounded by the well-known symbols of the four evangelists. (British Lib. London)

[Page 273]
272
Fig. 90: The soul ready to receive the spirit of the beyond. The inner-psychic
opposites—that were first experienced in symbols as fire and water or as substances—
are finally humanized by the alchemical work and named brother and sister.
(Miniature by Hossein Behzad, Rubaiyat of Omar Khayyam)

[Page 274]
273
10. The Different Types of Seeker
Text: (87.1) I have seen people who applied these names and 383
examples to dirty, impure things, which they adopted. They followed
many different ways with respect to these things and said: «The sages
spoke about something that can neither be known with words, nor can it be
understood.» So I said to some of them: «Ponder and search well and do
not make asses of yourselves nor ignorant barbarians, your eyes blind and
your ears deaf.» Then you will say: «Indeed the sages spoke about what
they knew, while we, we do not know.» They uttered in accordance with
their minds, and with these minds some explained the speech of others, who
sought the wisdom from those who preceded them. If you put this [the
speech of others] in your souls you kill them [souls], and keep them from
reaching the wisdom, and cut off the sharpness of your minds. Then the
hearts get filled with sadness, the minds become dull, the perceptions
become blind, the understanding gets covered, thinking diminishes and
hope gets cut off. Were there not among the past sages of the sons of Adam
men and women like you? But they had an advantage over you in their
peculiarities of their doing, their thinking, and their principles that they
stuck to, not ignoring them. Therefore they passed the night awake while
you slept, and they were searching while you gave up, and they studied
while you refused, and they suffered while you took it easy. So they
became knowledgeable, while you remained ignorant. (89.6)
Here we have clearly the standpoint of Ibn Umail concerning the 384
value of introversion and he clearly addresses «men and women like you».
The creation of the stone is not the privilege of men, as is stated by all great
masters of the art. But only by continuously keeping the night- and the
day-world together can an understanding of these alchemical texts turn

[Page 275]
274 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
into inspiration. For that the ancient masters «passed the night awake while
you slept». This is a symbol for being awake to the night-world, and for
continuously paying attention to what emerges from the unconscious.
«And they studied while you refused, and they suffered while you took it
easy. Thus they became knowledgeable, while you remained ignorant.»
The inspiration comes from studying the symbolism of the masters who
came before us, and who wrestled with the opposites in their psyche so that
they were able to slowly become conscious of what this fight of their inner
opposites could mean. In this way the dormant inner-psychic uniting self—
symbolized by the stone of the sages—can be brought to consciousness.
385 Text: (89.6) Indeed in this way they were able to reach the sought
for wisdom and other philosophical sciences which are related to that,
thanks to a truthful and sincere intention and keeping away from all
preoccupations. The books of the sages were their occupation and their
joy and they devoted their mornings and evenings to those [books]. Thus
their thoughts and minds were inspired, their ears listened attentively, their
souls were purified, the fire of their intelligence was ignited, their mind
was sharpened, and their thinking became strong. Then their thought and
the attainment of their studies fell upon the pure truth, from which those
who were before you obtained their life substance. (89.15)
386 Here Ibn Umail emphasizes the importance of studying the books
of the sages, something well-known from earlier Greek alchemical
authors, such as Zosimos. In his Mushaf as-suwar, his student
Theosebeia asks him: «Who of the people is the most capable with it [the
stone]?» Zosimos answers simply: «The one who reads our books with
utmost persistence.»606
387 Text: (91.1) Thus the one among them who continued to pursue the
straight path advanced until he attained the ultimate goal. The one whose
effort fell short of reaching his aim was deprived of it [the ultimate goal].
After it became evident to him that the work is indispensable, he failed to
reach it because of his inability. Therefore he abandoned the pursuit of the
investigations which the sages of the work ordered for the work, as well as
Mushaf as-suwar, fol. 126b 16-17. It is not surprising that we hear something quite similar
606
from Wei Po-Yang, the great master of Chinese alchemy: «A thousand readings will bring out
some points, and ten thousand perusals will enable a man to see others. At least revelation
will come to bring him enlightenment.» (J. Needham, Science and Civilisation in China, Vol.
5, part III, p. 57)

[Page 276]
Different Types of Seeker 275
the pursuit of the advice and urging of the sages to study their books and to
ponder over them. Therefore his exclusion became necessary because of
that. (91.8)
There is no way around the careful studying of the books of the sages.
Those who do not follow this advice of the sages to ponder continuously,
and, thanks to that effort, proceed step by step, will be disappointed and
finally abandon the work (see fig. 91). «Therefore his exclusion became
necessary because of that» as Ibn Umail says at the end of our passage. This
is the brutal reality for those who forsake their endeavour to stay related to
the reality of the objective psyche, that is to the enigmatic spirit of the
Fig. 91: The teaching of Morienus. The picture shows in the foreground the master
Morienus showing the adept that the gold of the sages is found in the dungheap,
where the adept sinks in with his right foot. The background shows an adept who
tries to reach the entrance to the tower by ascending without a ladder. That means
he is not going step by step in order to attain a higher consciousness that would
allow him to enter the tower, symbol for his or her own consolidated personality.
This adept falls down into the flatness of everyday reality, breaking his or her neck,
i.e. suffering psychological death. (Michael Maier's Aurea mensa, 1617)

[Page 277]
276 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
unconscious. If this inner truth is abandoned, one becomes abandoned by
one’s self, one’s own greater inner personality.
389 Text: (91.8) For another one it was sound and proven that the work
is true, yet he did not know what it was, since he did not know the origin
[of the work]. He arbitrarily follows ways in every direction and with every
operation with different kinds of remedies. Then he hopes in his aberration
to gain through his faults whatever he foolishly hopes and wishes for, or
something like that. But he is the one who wastes his life and his money on
something useless and dies in agony, because he does not know what he is
searching for. He is in love with a description, while he does not know what
is described. Therefore his body became weak and his mind became
confused because he loved what he could not see and what he did not
know. And he did not know what its operation could be, because of his
excessive confusion. And the sages said that the arbitrary seeker would
never obtain anything [from the work], because the science cannot be
without knowledge and endeavour by someone who knows. And another
one works for it foolishly and ignorantly because he has a deficiency of
mind. So he relaxes and abandons the tiresome work of the arbitrary search.
But the arbitrary seeker is for us in a higher rank than the one who abolishes
the science, because he [the arbitrary seeker] has knowledge that the art is
true. But his insufficiency hinders him, since his understanding fails to
know the origin. (95.3)
390 This passage again connects the reader with the introduction to the
Hall ar-rumuz, where Ibn Umail stated that love, patience and passion for
the art are the crucial criteria for success in the work. The one without love
for the art does not patiently ponder over the symbols contained in the texts
of the sages. Therefore he will never experience the unfolding of symbols
of the self, neither the ones offered by his or her own fantasies, dreams and
visions nor the ones mentioned by the old masters. These inner images, that
come along with unorthodox instinctual urges, which are kept in suspension
in order that they have to reveal their meaning, are the true origin of the
work. This is what «the arbitrary seeker» does not recognize. Only by
having such a personal experience, how such inner symbols emerge, do
symbols in alchemical texts make any sense, and are able to shed light on
such personal experiences.

[Page 278]
277
11. The Sister of Prophethood
Text: (95.4) Thus when the wise seeker examines the origin of the 391
art, from what it must be, and from where it came, he finds that it is a gift
from the exalted God for his prophets, the saints, the pious, and the people
of wisdom, whose religion, right living and detachment from the world
finds God’s approval; they can be people of any religion. (95.8)
As the stone can be produced by «people of any religion», this ability 392
to gather «the people of wisdom» guides us to the eros-quality of the stone,
which is able to unite people of all religions. The relationship of the
individual to his or her own centre, achieved by creating and then remaining
related to one’s own stone, carries the potential for humans to find a basis
for living together in the global village. The stone then becomes a true
collector, mosque or temple, collecting the different religions into one
house of God. The following dream of a 51-year old European, who was
in despair over a difficult creative project, illustrates this possibility:
I am at the inauguration of a mosque, which is at the same time also
like a Christian basilica, a Jewish synagogue, but also like the church of
the village where I grew up. It seemed to be in fact a house of God for
everybody, like the house of God of the global village. The king was now
on his way to inaugurate this house of God by inserting a huge black cubic
basalt, diorite or—as I thought when waking up—pyrite stone, over 10
metres in length, into a hole prepared for it that was exactly the size of the
stone. The inserted stone was then the floor in front of the prayer-niche or
altar, which was, however, just a white linen screen. How this stone was
inserted remains a riddle to me. But before the final finish of plastering the
joints of the inserted stone takes place, the king—who is now clearly a
sultan, reminding me somehow of Nathan the Wise—asks what is now a

[Page 279]
278 Commentary on the Hall ar-mmuz
huge assembly of people, whether somebody could give the inaugural
speech, because he feels unable to give this speech. Nobody volunteers. As
I know that only those words could be said which the stone itself has to say,
I raise my arm to show my willingness. But this is not noticed at first by the
sultan, because of the mass of people, so I wave my arm to draw his attention.
He is clearly relieved that somebody has come forward. As I start, I am
standing right beside the floor made up of the inserted black stone. This
square black floor is respectfully kept free of people. Nobody stands on it.
On the opposite side I clearly see a close friend of mine as well as a woman
friend and other people familiar to me. I maintain eye contact with these
people while 1 start to talk. I fold my hands. The talk starts with the stone,
who says that he first came from the earth, was going up to heaven, and
unites now again with the earth, and says that this is the crucial thing. I do
not remember in detail everything that happened in between the beginning
and this end, its ascending up and coming down into the prepared hole, but
I do know that it had to do with the work that I am involved with.
393 The dream can be understood in the light of the knowledge of
alchemical symbolism that we have now acquired. It shows that the black
stone could indeed become a basis for the future centre of the global village,
where we have to learn to live together with different images of God, that
everyone, for him- or herself, can project onto the white linen screen that
is now the altar. The unifying quality of the black stone means that the
common basis of a real world community will be a becoming conscious that
we all have to see that our common basis is our own shadow that goes
under the earth, «the shadow of the sun» or the «heart of the Sun», the
lunar «Rock of the Devil». The fact that in this dream the dreamer is the
one addressing the inauguration assembly and communicating «what the
stone has to say to them» was a strong compensation for the dreamer’s
state of mind. He had to complete a difficult creative project that seemed
much too big to him. So he had to realize that he himself had to do this
work, yet, that it will not be his ego that will have to accomplish the work,
Fig. 92: The adept and female in the vessel. The continuous pondering over the
symbols from the unconscious in the closed vessel of the adept’s psyche
humanizes the unknown other, and turns it into a living, visible-invisible, attractive
human partner. This allows him or her to hear what the stone, the other side of
consciousness, or the objective psyche has to say. (Miniature by Hossein Behzad,
Rubaiyat of Omar Khayyam)

[Page 280]
The Sister of Prophethood
279

[Page 281]
280 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
but that he only has to remain as transparent as possible in order that those
words could be said through him which the stone itself has to say—as the
dream says. The black stone in the centre, as the common basis of every­
thing in the house of God, corresponds to the feminine side, the soil, the trans­
formed black earth. It is a symbol for the feminine side of the divine that
unites human beings in the one centre, hidden in the unconscious (see fig. 92).
It is a symbol of what each one has to extract from the unconscious in order
to have a basis from which to see the living image of the divine on the
white screen that is visible at the place of the qibla, altar, or sanctuary. On
a white screen anything can be projected. It receives any image of the
divine, and as it is not affected by the projected images, it also unites all
the projected images or symbols into one. Standing on a common ground
of the worked upon black earth, the united congregation can come from
any religion. The different images that are seen on the screen at the place
of the holiest of the holy may be as manifold as there are people present,
everybody will just see his own projected image of the divine, his
unique enigmatic, magnetic «some body» or «some thing», that keeps
him or her going on in his or her path of individuation.
394 It is remarkable that when the dreamer visited the Hagia Sophia in
Istanbul, this building reminded him of this house of God. The Hagia
Sophia as it stands today was rebuilt on the orders of Justinian in 532-537.
It was then used for the coronation of the Byzantine emperors for almost a
thousand years. When Constantinople was conquered by the Muslims in
1453, this church was converted into a mosque. Thanks to the brilliant
Ottoman architect Koca Mimar Sinan (1491-1588), who had built four
minarets together with some reinforcements, the huge dome was prevented
from collapsing. Under Muslim rule the place continued to be called Hagia
Sophia (Turkish: Aya Sofya). It is not named after any saint or emperor but
after the Holy Wisdom, the feminine consort of God before creation.
Today, thanks to Kemal Atatürk, the founder of secular Turkey and its first
president, this building has been turned into a museum or, as one might
also say, a house of God for everybody.
395 Text: (95.8) The exalted God entrusted it [the art] to Adam, the
father of mankind, as a substitute for the Paradise from which he was
expelled. So when it became clear to him [the wise seeker] that this was
given to the owner of wisdom, and it was confirmed that it is a secret of the
secrets of God, and from Him came this perfect Wisdom, which is a sister

[Page 282]
The Sister of Prophethood 281
to prophethood, it was then no longer possible for him to take up dirty or
stinking things. Nor could he say that this Wisdom, with its nobility and its
high-ranking majesty for the exalted God, is found in human excrement, or
urine, or in discarded dirty hair, or in rotten, stinking eggs, or in sperm, or
in blood, or in the gall bladder, or in the brain, and he does not need the
lime of skulls, because all of these are filthy things that change things into
a most ugly sight and a most awful smell. (97.6)
Here the true goal of symbolic alchemy becomes clearly visible. It 396
is an inner quest for the perfect Wisdom of God and outer experimentation
could only distract from the true work. The external symbols are, however,
as we could see from the whole Hall ar-rumüz, a bridge to this inner centre.
For that reason, the outer world also becomes most precious for the adept,
becoming more and more holy with the work. But the adept must see it as
a symbol for the divine, and not become possessed by outer matter. When
reduced to concrete outer matter, instead of revealing the truth, these symbols
would turn into stinking “nothing but ...” material which would leave the
adept without the revelation of the Holy Wisdom, hidden inside the symbol.
Text: (97.7) How could the sister of prophethood by the testimony 397
of the Commander of the Faithful—may be peace on him—be like that an
impure, filthy thing, or come from something filthy, dirty, stinking filthy
while it is the glorified secret of God and His excellent treasure? [How
could it be] that the Wisdom of God, whose names are sanctified, comes
from the buttocks of a hen? The honoured Wisdom of God, which gives
nobility to its people, is far from what they claim. No, by God, the exalted
God does not place His spiritual divine Wisdom, which is too subtle to be
attained by the minds and by the understanding, into any of these abominable,
stinking, impure, and dirty objects. (99.8)
Here Ibn Umail points out that the introverted work of alchemy is 398
«the sister of prophethood». It is an experience comparable to the experiences
of the prophets in the Koran. It is, however, not the brother but the sister of
prophethood, referring to the feminine quality of the stone of the sages,
which compensates for the one-sided masculine image of God in the book-
religions of Judaism, Christianity and Islam, pointing deary to the feminine
side of God, symbolized by His Wisdom.
The word prophethood (nubüwa) is based on the root naba:a. The 399
verb means, among other things, (II. form): to inform, to notify, to reveal.

[Page 283]
282 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumüz
This is the original meaning of prophethood. The prophets of the Koran
were communicating what God had to say, and they stood up for the new
values of monotheism that were to be seen by their people. They were
strong personalities, and as individuals they lived by their new values. In
the Muslim world, the prophet Muhammad is the highest human being,
second in importance to Allah. In the Koran he is named the hatm (Sura
33.40), the seal of the prophets. Usually this is interpreted as Muhammad
being the final prophet. But for the ShiTtes there was also in the Imams a
continuation of the living emanation of Allah that gave direction.607 * In
some alchemical texts we find that the stone is also named Imäm.m This
shows that there is an experience of an inner guide that can be developed,
so that it becomes a guide for the individual and may be for the people
around him or her. The Hindus speak in this respect of the inner guru.
400 At the beginning of the work, this stone is a possibility, open to
everybody. If the inner symbols connected to the fire of hellish emotions
are developed by intense pondering, and the obtained insights get connected
with daily life, the tree of life can grow, nourishing the individual «with its
golden fruits», as Maslama al-Magrïtï put it.609 This story sums up the
essence of the work. He writes: «Oh you seeker, this (alchemical) work is
similar to the water that enters from within the earth in a certain garden, be
it by means of a vessel, be it directly from a source coming out of the earth
there. That water moistens the dry, dusty earth. In time, by the help of God
the almighty, the water is transformed to golden shining fruits.» The hard
dry outer world, when united with the life-bringing influx of the water from
within and cultivated, will result—with the help of God—in golden
shining fruits, symbols for durable insights or illuminations.
607 Imam (Arabic = leader in the sense of a “pattern whose example is followed”). The
Sunnites regard the man appointed Imam as capable of erring, and to be obeyed even though
he personally sins, provided he maintains the ordinances of Islam. But the Shicites believe
that the divinely appointed Imam is also divinely illumined and preserved (ma csum) from sin.
This is the greater Imamate. The lesser Imamate is the leadership in the Friday prayers.
Summary from the Encyclopedia of Islam, s.v. Imam.
60
s See, for instance, Gäbir ihn Haiyan, Kitäb al-Malik (The Book of the King) in: M.
Berthelot, La Chimie au moyen âge III, p. 91 f. (Arabie); p. 126 f. translation and Gäbir. Kitäb
ar-Rahma as-sagïr (The Small Book of Mercy), ibid., p. 99 f. (Arabic); p.126 f. translation.
See also J. Ruska, Arab. Alch., p. 50 f. Whether Gäbir ibn Haiyan is the author of these texts
is not important in our context.
609 Maslama al-Magrïtî, K. Rutbat al-hakim wa-mudhal al-ta cIm (The Levels of the Wise and
the Introduction to the Science), Rampur Library (North India), Ms Nr. LXXVII, fol. 215b.

[Page 284]
The Sister of Prophethood 283
In accordance with this experience, our author understood the word 401
hatm in the Koran rather as meaning that Muhammad was a testimony to
all what the earlier prophets had revealed to their people, and that the living
emanation of Allah did not come to an end with his prophethood. To purify
one's psyche in order to become subtle enough to express the will of the
divine in nature, is to allow what the stone has to say to emerge. That was
the goal of the adept. Therefore the stone is the speech, as we have seen
earlier (§ 281-283). And as an additional effect, the work gives the adept
the feeling of being connected to the timeless and incorruptible.
The relationship between alchemy and prophethood has a long 402
tradition that goes back to the Pharaonic Culture and Greek alchemy, as
the god Hermes-Thot was known to be the head of the prophets. To give a
few examples: Democritus calls the adepts in his treatise “Physika kai
Mystika” synprophetai, which Ch.-Em. Ruelle translated as «confrères en
prophétie».610 In the text “Isis to Horus”, Isis speaks of herself as a prophet,
and Maria is called a prophet.611 In the Risälat as-Sirr, a text attributed to
Hermes, the alchemists are called seers (näzirün).612 I. Vereno relates this
expression to prophethood with convincing arguments. It looks as if Ibn
Umail wanted to bring this tradition into focus with his statement about
prophethood.613 This connection of the alchemical work to prophethood
remained alive in later Latin alchemy.614
The intense relationship of the sages with their fantasies, which 403
originate in the fiery emotional background of the psyche, brought them
into contact with all that was constellated in the collective unconscious.
That allowed them to perceive what was striving to emerge and what needed
to be seen in order to fulfil the will of the divine nature. In this way they
came into contact with the unconscious Zeitgeist of their time, which
needed to become conscious. That is why Ibn Umail could speak of alchemy
Berthelot, Coll. Alch. Grec II.1.14.
610
Sources with Vereno, Studien, p. 97, n225.
611
Edition by I. Vereno, ibid., p. 156.
612
613 Ibid., p. 330 and 327. See also J. Ruska, Arab. Alch.. p. 91, where we read in a text
attributed to öacfar as-Sädiq: «... people of this science have to admit that it is a revelation
of the exalted God to his prophets» (my translation into English).
614 For example, in Petrus Bonus “Pretiosa margarita novella”, in J. J. Mangetus, Bibliotheca
chemica II, p. 30. where he says, quoting Morienus: «Scias quod hoc magisterium non est nisi
Secretum secretorum Dei altissimi & magni. Ipse enim hoc magisterium suis Philosophis vel
Prophetis commendavit, [...].» (Know that this work cannot be without the secret of the
secrets of the highest and great God, which he entrusts to his sages and prophets ...)

[Page 285]
284 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
as being the sister of prophethood. Indeed, the root of all the knowledge
needed for the future of humanity lies in the form of images in the depth of
the unconscious.615
404 Text: (99.9) The mighty and exalted God inspired His prophet
Moses—may peace be on him—to decorate the Torah with gold that must
not be touched by impure hands. When Moses—may peace be on him—
knew this, God sent this wisdom down upon him. So how could this [wisdom]
be in an impure or dirty thing or [how could He] give him [Moses] and the
others the order to make that [work] from human excrement, or urine, or
blood? The exalted God would not occupy His prophets and the righteous
among the people of this wisdom with operating something impure. And
the secret of the exalted God would not be in these things nor in anything
similar. ( 101.6)
405 This passage reconfirms that the alchemical work has nothing to do
with becoming obsessed with concrete objects of the lower world like
«human excrement, or urine, or blood», as they are to be seen as symbols
for the lower rejected realm of the unconscious psyche. In these rejected
realms, the gold of the sages is found, in stercore invenitur (in the
dungheap [the gold] is found) as the later Latin alchemists said. But this has
nothing to do with concretely turning to these rejected substances (see fig. 54).
As has been mentioned repeatedly, in the future we will rather have to speak
of the religious-mystical branch of alchemy as symbolic alchemy.
406 Text: (101.7) If he [the seeker] knew the rank of this art, and did not
ignore its merit, and if he knew its great significance he would know that
this secret, as a secret of God, is mighty and noble, and that it is wisdom of
the exalted God and that all other noble sciences are not like it, [and] he
615 Details in C.G. Jung, Aion, and in his letter to Sir Herbert Read of the 2. IX. 1960: the para­
graph that starts: «What is the great dream? It is made up of all the many small dreams ...». For
practical examples of this aspect of the alchemical work I refer to my extensive research
based on archetypal dream material, and its coagulation in collective issues. First in my
diploma thesis at the C. G. Jung-Institue in Zurich, Entwicklungsplanung im Licht unbewusster
Zeitproblematik (1975), then in my PhD thesis at the Federal Institute of Technology (ETH)
in Zurich, Entwicklungsplanung ohne Seele? (1977), then in my thesis for habilitation at the
same Institute, Progress without Loss of Soul (in German 1983, in English 1988), and more
recently Wissen und Ahnung (2007), and Landentwicklung mit Seelengewinn (2008), as
well as my ongoing lecturing since 1990 at the Federal Institute of Technology on the topic
“Archetypal Dreams concerning our Environmental Problems”. See, for example, Th. Abt,
“Auf der Suche nach einem Dialog mit der Natur”, GAIA 1/6 (1992).

[Page 286]
The Sister of Prophethood 285
would not assume that it comes from anything but the most noble things,
highest in rank, the best in element, the purest in body, the most noble in
soul, and the most subtle in spirit. Therefore, when the seeker realizes that
about the greatness of the art, he would venerate and honour it, and consider
it the most exalted and noble thing; the noble art would honour, ennoble,
and upgrade him and make him operate on the most noble, pleasant­
smelling and elevated things. But when he disdained the art and made it
from the basest and most despicable things, such as human excrement,
urine, arsenic and sulphur, which stink and burn, and base things [in gen­
eral], the art would despise him also in turn, and humble him, and made
him operate on the impure, base and abominable things because everyone
who believes in a thing must operate on it, and the work places him where
he places himself. Then he sees the evil of his work (Mss A and B have:
When he complains about his work), his defect becomes evident too, then
the others see in him what he does not see in himself. We ask God out of
His favour that he may grant us success, and help us in the science with
regard to the beloved [stone]. Indeed he is powerful over everything. ( 105.3)
This shows that the result of the work has to stand in this world.
When, however, shadowy ego-centred motivations for developing a
relationship with the unconscious remain unconscious, the whole work
becomes corrupt, evil, unsatisfying. But this type of adept often cannot see
that because he became possessed or blinded by the archetypal world. Only
«the others see in him what he does not see in himself». The “bad smell”
around such an inflated person is hopefully noticed in his or her surroundings.
As we read in the introduction of Ibn Umail’s Hall ar-rumüz (§ 72), the
alchemical work is only «for whoever loves this art passionately and has
patience for the bitterness of the love and the passion, and continues to
think about how he can reach his beloved, and who is not overcome either
by impatience, bitterness, denial or fatigue.» It is only the love for that
inner, unconscious Other that forces one into the painful yet also so precious
and adventuous quest that will bring the hidden treasure in the depth of the
ocean to consciousness, and thus can give a satisfying answer to the riddle
of life’s meaning. But whether somebody takes up this work, and then
stays faithful to it, is by the grace of God, as all the great masters of the
art agree. '

[Page 288]
Part III
The Ending of the
Manuscripts A and B
and Epilogue

[Page 290]
289
Text and Commentary
(Text: 135.1) One has to take from the mercury (plant) five rotls and 408
you work upon it in a new glass retort without resounding lead, four hands
in height and one hand in width. Its head (upper part) should be hook­
shaped (?) in the measure that it lets enter the head (upper part) of a pack
needle. And that comes after you have wiped the outside of the retort with
the distillate of the hoof of animals (= glue). Then you cool it well and
install a big alembic that has a wide base and a spout (?). And you close
the joint well. Thus when it (the glue) dries up, put the retort on a well-
proportioned stove whose height is a cubit and a half, as it is normally
done. And it should have outlets for the smoke. Then you install the receiver
and you connect it well. Then set the retort on a gentle fire until the stone
and the retort get used to the fire. Thus in the first round it will distil one
dirham. When the distillation stops, you take out the stone—after it has
cooled down—and you wipe it with a piece of cotton. (135.13)
The ending of two of the manuscripts of the Hall ar-rumüz concentrates 409
on the distillation process. Keeping in mind that distillation is a symbol for
intense pondering and thus refining and purifying the thoughts (see § 25 f.),
we can understand this ‘recipe’.
(Text: 135.13) Then you return the distillate back on it and pound it 4io
intensely. After that you return it to the retort and you distil it as usual. In
this round it will distil two dirham from each rotl. Then you do with it as
you did the first time. Between each distillation there should be seven
appointed times. You continue like that until the stone gets completely
distilled. Then when that occurs, you work upon it in a clayed retort whose
middle is round. And you put it in the belly of the horse for seven appointed
times (e.g. days). (135.20)

[Page 291]
290 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
411 The seven appointed times refer to the seven planets or metals, that
is the complete inner-psychic structure, while a clayed retort is now needed
to protect the vessel from breaking. The heat is obviously now more
intense and needs this precaution, in order that the ego-stucture of the
adept, symbolized by the retort, does not crack. In Ibn Umail’s ad-Durra
an-naqiya this clay is named «clay of wisdom».616 The picture on the opposite
page shows such a retort, covered with clay (see fig. 93).
412 (Text: 135.20) Then when the appointed time is over, take it [the
retort] out gently and be careful not to expose it to the air. Then you take
the stone out and rub it with a piece of cotton as before and be aware of the
air. And you return the distillate and you install the alembic and the receiver
as usual and be aware of the air. And you must distil and return what is
distilled onto what is not distilled. You do that seven times, having seven
appointed times between each distillation. Then when the period is over,
put it in a retort and fasten the joint well and bury it in horse dung for seven
appointed times, having seven distillations between each burying. You let
the stone rest (?) and put it in a clayed retort and fasten its joint well and
bury it in the dung for 21 days. Then when the period is completed and the
retort has cooled down, you install the open (alembic) and you distil the
stone. And you return what is distilled onto what is not distilled until you
distil the whole as red water, and black earth remains in the vessel. In order
to make it red, you install on the vessel a closed (= blind) alembic, and close
its joint well. Then place it on a low fire. After that you should separate the
distillate in a viol and seal it with wax. (137.12)
413 This part seems to be a summary and a continuation of the theme
of ascending and the descending, the persistent pondering over the
symbols. Hence the reference to «creating something out of nothing» as
we have seen earlier in our text (see § 286 and § 293). It is the longissima
via operis, the extremely long way of the alchemical work, as it was
described in Latin alchemy.
414 (Text: 137.12) During this process the stone takes on a (different)
colour every day and every night as was said before. Then when it becomes
red and turns like the red ruby (yäqüt ahmar), resisting the fire, cool down
the vessel and take apart the joint and take the stone out while it is red.
ad-Durra an-naqïya
616 M uham m ad ibn Um ail gives in his , at the end of the third chapter
on fol. 13 (ÄsafTya Lib. 1410), a description of how to m ake the clay of wisdom .

[Page 292]
The Ending of the Manuscripts A and B 291
Fig. 93: Traditional distillation.

[Page 293]
292 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
solid and hard. Then pound it and soak it with that red water until each part
of it absorbs nine parts of it [the water]. Then you will see the earth blossom
with all kinds of flowers. Then you take the stone and work on it in a retort,
having on it an alembic that is open towards the inside of the retort, whose
joints are well closed. And set it on a low fire so that a hot thing descends
on a hot thing lest the retort should break. And your soaking should be
during the daytime not during the night, every day twelve dirhams in
twelve hours. And during night you leave it on a low and gentle fire. You
do that until it [the stone] absorbs all of the water. When that is completed,
the stone turns red and resists the fire and is dissolved as wax dissolves.
And you use gentleness. Then you take from the silver of gold and you
nourish the stone with it on the fire in portions of one sixth at a time. And
whenever one sixth gets dissolved, it has to be nourished with one like it.
until all of it gets dissolved. Thus when you reach that level, work upon it
in a closed retort and put it in the belly of the horse for 21 days. Then cool
it down and separate its joint and repeat the distillation and return what is
distilled onto what is not distilled until there is no more distillation, but it
returns thickened again. (137.32)
415 Here the connection and interaction of the activity during day and at
night is remarkable. It leads to a different colour each time, to a different tone
of feeling. The soaking during day-time is the connection of the effect of the
divine water from the unconscious with the reality of life. Nourishing one
sixth after one sixth points again to the circular process mentioned earlier
(§ 138). Six times the radius of a circle is its natural division.
416 (Text: 137.32) If you take from the honoured stone one unit on 15
measures of the sun, then it becomes red earth; and one unit of it on 10,025
measures from any body you want, then it becomes a sun. And if you wish,
throw from it one measure on the fugitive, all of it [will turn] into red earth.
And one measure of it on 20,000 measures from any body you wish
becomes a sun. And if you throw one measure on a 1000 measures of
water, it dyes it into a ruby, God the exalted willing, and God knows best.
(139.6)
417 This ending recalls the ad-Durra an-naqïya, the book that Ibn
Umail wrote shortly before he passed away. In this book most of the chapters
end with a sentence that goes somehow like this: “Throw some of your
acquired substance upon much more of something that is less precious and

[Page 294]
The Ending of the Manuscripts A and B 293
you will turn it into something more precious.” This is a well known way
of ending a chapter or a book on alchemy. Here in our text it is clear that
the result of the work is the emergence of a symbol named a sun that is the
gold of the sages, the new king, or newly acquired consciousness. This
psychological goal is most beautifully described in ad-Durra an-naqiya of
Ibn Umail, where he writes at the end of the seventh chapter: «[...] repeat the
distillation until the copper is dissolved and becomes subtle, after having
been dense, and it becomes one single human being in the colour of ruby
water. Then thicken it in hot ashes in a goblet coated in the clay of wisdom
till it coagulates red like purple. Then put one part of it on 1000 parts of
non-worked mercury that has been purified by a piece of linen, return it to
the retort, and let it rot for a complete week. Thus it coagulates into a white
stone if it is white, and if it is red it turns into a carnelian-red stone. One
misqal of it dyes 1000 misqals of any metal you want. It will be a moon
from the white, and if it were red, it will be a sun, standing for liberation
[or remaining till (the day) of deliverance].»616 This part shows the creation
of the anthropos, «the one single human being in the colour of ruby water»
that then coagulates in a goblet coated in the clay of wisdom, symbol for
the psyche of the adept that is able to contain this divine figure, by being
well protected by the clay of wisdom. This is the realization that in order
to be able to hold the influx of the cosmic dimension of the collective
unconscious one needs utmost self-limitation. Then the moon and the sun,
gold and silver, will solidify in the psyche of the adept; eros and logos as
the word principles will become incarnate as equals.
The still greater value at the end of the work is also named «ruby» 4is
here, but what is meant is of course «the ruby of the sages». By that Ibn
Umail means this one single red stone of the sages, a symbol for the solidified
union of the fire and the water. This stone is, however, not just a ruby, but
is white outside and red inside, as we learn from the following passage in
Ibn Umail’s al-MäJ al-waraql: «And by his statement: “We cook it gently
in a vessel of glass until it turns red, coagulates and becomes a stone like
the ruby” he meant the cooking of the water with the soul, after they are
extracted from their sediments, until they ripen, and mix fully and well.
Thus this their mixing is named coagulation. And by his statement “like the
ruby” he means the dyeing soul, because it obtains strength from the fire.
Ad-durra an-naqiya
517 , M s Â safïya Lib. Hyderabad, fol. 17.6-10.

[Page 295]
294 Commentary on the Hall ar-rumuz
Although it is white—because the spirit covers it with its colour—he
knows that it is red like the ruby, although it is hidden in the water and
cannot be seen. But when he reached with it to this operation of the cooking,
he knew, thanks to science, that it reached the limit with which it became
a penetrating dye. Thus he stated that its colour is like the ruby.»618 This
final coagulation is also the perfect lead-copper that we saw at the
beginning of the Hall ar-rumüz, the unio mystica of the female white and
the male red, of silver and gold, of moon and sun, that finally connect in a
lasting way with the sulphurs of the earth, the soul of the adept and
ego-consciousness of the adept him- or herself.
618 Al-Ma3 al-waraqi, p. 33.13-14.

[Page 296]
295
Epilogue
After our journey through the amplification and interpretation of
419
the many symbols of Muhammad ihn Umail’s Hall ar-rumüz, we must
now try to summarize the basic message of his text in order to see in which
direction any further research on symbolic alchemy might go. The central
theme of his book is the description, explanation, and praise of the creation
of the stone of the sages. However, as our author emphasizes in his
